Abstract (英文): Vision-language-action (VLA) models need to continually acquire new manipulation skills while retaining the ability to follow previously learned instructions. Experience replay (ER) can mitigate catastrophic forgetting, but retaining only a small number of historical expert frames per task leaves many old-instruction responses without direct supervision. Under limited replay, we observe a structured failure mode in which an earlier instruction can trigger a subsequently learned, competing goal. To address this gap, we propose Cross-Instruction Anchoring (XIA), which supplements expert replay with instruction-conditioned response preservation. At each task boundary, XIA freezes the previous policy as a teacher and matches its responses to old instructions paired with current observations. This extends supervision beyond stored historical examples without requiring additional expert replay data. Experiments across multiple VLA architectures and three LIBERO suites demonstrate improved success rates and reduced forgetting compared with ER under identical historical expert-data budgets. Additional RoboCasa and real-world experiments further support the effectiveness of XIA.

</details>

Abstract (英文): GUI grounding requires resolving fine visual details within the context that gives them meaning. Additional views can improve localization, but their value depends on what evidence they expose and how much context they preserve. We explore a capability of vision-language models (VLMs): even when their coordinate predictions are incorrect, they can describe visual evidence worth inspecting. We introduce \textbf{SPARK}, \textit{\textbf{S}emantic \textbf{P}robe-guided \textbf{A}daptive \textbf{R}egion see\textbf{K}ing}, a training-free framework that turns this semantic guidance into targeted visual observations. Exploration probes describe relevant context and distinguishing target details, while verification probes identify evidence needed to assess emerging hypotheses. Their spatial attention guides view placement, contextual responses determine crop extent, and observation history encourages complementary inspection. The resulting predictions are combined through uncertainty-weighted voting. On ScreenSpot-Pro, SPARK improves grounding accuracy over single-view inference by 13.9, 25.2, and 16.1 percentage points with Qwen3-VL, Qwen2.5-VL, and UI-TARS, outperforming all compared test-time methods for each backbone. Consistent gains across four additional benchmarks demonstrate its effectiveness across diverse interfaces and grounding tasks. These results establish semantic probing as an effective mechanism for turning a frozen VLM's understanding of what to inspect into more accurate GUI grounding.

</details>

Abstract (英文): 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) makes one screen tile the cell of partitioning, the cell of rasterization, and the unit of parallel work. Partitioning favors large tiles and rasterization small ones, and with one unit per tile the densest tiles set the frame time. We present Hierarchically Tiled Gaussian Splatting (HiGS), a unified architecture for rendering and reconstruction that gives each role its own granularity: Gaussians are partitioned over coarse macro-tiles, rasterized over the fine render tiles within them, and processed in bounded depth-sorted batches. A dense region issues more batches instead of one longer unit, so frame time follows the work in a view rather than its densest tile. The same batches carry reconstruction: compositing supplies the transmittance and color needed to differentiate each one independently, leaving optimizer memory traffic as the dominant training cost. We fuse the optimizer into the backward pass, halve the parameters and moments it stores and updates directly, and defer appearance updates outside the view. HiGS renders scenes faster than every published method we evaluate, by the widest margin on views with uneven load, and reconstructs them faster and in less memory, at equal image quality and with exact front-to-back alpha compositing.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Affordance grounding localizes where an agent should interact with an object to perform a task. Many everyday manipulation tasks, however, require bimanual affordance, in which two hands assume complementary and cooperative roles to accomplish a shared goal. Existing 3D affordance datasets primarily ground single interaction regions, and manually annotating dual-hand, cooperative contacts does not scale. To address this gap, we introduce DUALingo, a large-scale dataset for task-conditioned, role-aware bimanual 3D affordance grounding on diverse everyday objects. DUALingo represents each task as two cooperative, hand-agnostic roles, each grounded as a heatmap on the object surface. It contains 271K free-form tasks on 71K objects, generated by a fully automated pipeline of open-weight foundation models without human labeling. Expert ratings and a validated LLM judge confirm the quality of the resulting annotations. Trained on DUALingo, existing 3D affordance models learn to ground both cooperative roles from natural-language instructions, with the best-performing model achieving a more than threefold improvement in aIoU over the zero-shot baseline, from 10.3 to 37.8. Robotic manipulation demonstrations illustrate the actionability of the predicted bimanual affordances in performing cooperative tasks. These results position DUALingo as a scalable foundation for learning everyday bimanual affordances. Project page: https://nycu-pair-labs.github.io/DUALingo/

</details>

Abstract (英文): For endoscopic robotic surgery, accurate dense depth perception serves as a foundational capability, which enables navigation, instrument localization, and tissue interaction. However, endoscopic video lacks reliable metric depth, while metric measurements obtained via tracking, time-of-flight sensing, or instrument contact only cover a small fraction of pixels in each frame, rendering sparse depth completion an urgent and unsolved challenge. In this work, we propose Temporal Anchor Densification with Reliability-Aware Memory (TANDEM), a causal sparse depth completion framework for endoscopic video. It adopts a lightweight design and leverages the video stream: past measurements re-projected into the current view recover the metric evidence unavailable within a single sparse frame. To keep this evidence interpretable, accurate, and efficient, an explicit memory scores each re-projected measurement with analytic re-observation, age, and motion-parallax factors without overriding current measurements. A compact network then reconstructs depth via reliability-weighted gradient integration and a frequency-gated reference prior. We evaluate TANDEM on the C3VD colonoscopy and StereoMIS laparoscopy datasets. With only 5.58 M parameters, TANDEM reduces the root mean square error by 11.7% and 32.0%, respectively, compared with the strongest baseline, and achieves state-of-the-art accuracy. It also remains accurate under pose errors of the magnitude reported for endoscopic pose estimation. Code will be released at https://github.com/Wk-cpu-art/TANDEM upon acceptance.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Shape decomposition represents 3D objects as a compact set of geometric primitives for interpretable modeling and simulation. However, existing decomposition methods are trained primarily for geometric fidelity, without explicit constraints on physical plausibility, and can therefore produce disconnected parts or physically unstable reconstructions. We present PhysDec, an agentic framework that draws on a pretrained vision-language model's semantic understanding and commonsense reasoning to predict material-aware primitive decompositions from a few images. Within this framework, PhysDec-Loop is an agentic pipeline that reasons about part structure and materials, using a reference 3D point cloud for geometric feedback and physical verifiers to guide iterative refinement. Through self-evolution, we further develop another agentic pipeline, PhysDec-Evolved, which learns from prior corrections to predict primitive decompositions from images without point-cloud feedback or a correction loop at inference. Experiments show that PhysDec-Loop improves geometric fidelity, physical plausibility and visual quality over geometry-focused baselines, while PhysDec-Evolved approaches its performance at substantially lower inference cost.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Interactive video world models enable action- and camera-controlled exploration, yet struggle to maintain consistent spatial geometry and appearance when revisiting previously explored locations. Historical KV caches help auto-regressive video diffusion models maintain long-term consistency, but their retrieval and attention computation introduce substantial latency as history grows. We propose DraftKV, a training-free framework that retrieves historical KV caches based on geometric grounding and query-conditioned relevance, while combining CPU offloading with tile-wise streaming attention to reduce GPU memory usage and inference latency. Theoretically, we show that draft relevance reproduces the mean branch of full-resolution block relevance exactly, so its deviation is confined to the $β$-weighted max branch, and we bound retrieval utility loss in terms of the measured Top-K selection overlap. We further show that draft relevance cannot distinguish blocks with similar pooled descriptors regardless of camera pose, motivating geometric grounding before refinement. Then, we introduce streaming CG-Attention, which overlaps CPU-to-GPU KV transfers with attention computation through tile-wise streaming, hiding transfer latency while maintaining bounded GPU memory usage. Experiments across diverse model families show that DraftKV achieves superior generation quality and consistency while achieving $3.27\times$ end-to-end speedup and 42.2% peak GPU memory reduction over other CPU-offloaded KV cache retrieval methods. Notably, DraftKV enables high-quality interactive world generation with historical KV retrieval on a single H100 GPU using SolarWM-33B.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Hands grasp objects with soft skin. A fingertip flattens against a mug, and a palm curves around a bottle. Yet hand-object datasets and estimators represent the hand as a rigid articulated mesh, which hovers above or penetrates the object where skin would press against it. Existing refiners keep the mesh rigid and adjust only the pose, trading pose accuracy for physical plausibility. We ask whether a rigid hand can instead touch the object as skin does while staying close to its original pose. We lift annotated or estimated rigid hands to contact-consistent meshes through an optimization that (i) slightly adjusts the joints and wrist to bring the fingers onto the object, (ii) deforms the skin so that penetrating regions settle onto the surface, and (iii) keeps this deformation consistent over time. Since deformation reaches only skin already near the object, we further train a lightweight head on the lifted annotations to predict where an estimated hand should touch and steer the deformation there. Unlike existing refiners, the lift more than doubles the skin lying on the object surface for three published estimators while barely changing the pose. Guidance from the learned head further improves grasp stability by closing contact gaps that the estimate leaves open. Existing hand-object datasets and estimators can thus adopt contact-consistent hands without changing the hand model or retraining the estimators.

</details>

Abstract (英文): A driving planner needs both scene semantics, which vision-language models (VLMs) provide, and spatial structure, which geometric foundation models recover from visual history. We present VLG-Drive, which keeps both models frozen and trains only a fusion interface and a planner from trajectory labels. At each camera-grid cell, the interface fuses the paired VLM and geometry features into one joint token through a geometry-anchored residual, and a command-conditioned planner reads these tokens to predict the ego trajectory. VLG-Drive achieves 31.78 EPDMS on NavHard under perturbed initial states, the highest among the compared methods, and 88.27 PDMS on NavTest from cameras alone. Pointwise fusion outperforms independent VLM and geometry streams on both benchmarks. In closed-loop driving on 420 HUGSIM scenarios, VLG-Drive outperforms all compared planners. These results indicate that a lightweight planner trained on fused features of frozen vision-language and geometric foundation models is an effective alternative to adapting either model for planning.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Advancing surgical automation requires a comprehensive spatio-temporal understanding of how instruments act on tissue throughout an action. Existing surgical benchmarks, however, largely define supervision through task-specific targets, leaving the relationships between action semantics, instrument motion, and tissue interaction incompletely specified. To address this limitation, we introduce TACTUS (Tool, Action, Contact and Trajectory Understanding in Surgery), a new dataset and benchmark that establishes a unified annotation framework for complete surgical actions. The framework jointly specifies tool and action identities, nested action and interaction windows, instrument-specific landmark trajectories, and tissue-region tracks. To construct these annotations at scale, we develop a two-tier pipeline combining human annotation, point tracking, and model-generated spatial prompts. Using this pipeline, we re-annotate 141 videos from five public sources, producing 22,995 action clips spanning 2.9 million frames. The dataset comprises 15,668 Gold clips with human spatial prompts followed by review and re-checking, and 7,327 Silver clips combining human semantic and temporal annotations with model-generated spatial prompts. We further introduce TactusNet, the companion reference network, to benchmark semantic, temporal, and spatial prediction from shared video features. Across TactusNet and 13 published baselines, tool-action accuracy reaches 95.8% with per-head encoder fine-tuning, while the best temporal recall and contact Dice reach 46.6% at temporal IoU 0.7 and 0.351, respectively. Moreover, adding Silver improves the default TactusNet's pair accuracy from 93.8% to 95.5%. Together, these contributions establish a foundation for learning and evaluating the instrument-tissue interactions underlying future surgical automation.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Generating videos with physically plausible dynamics remains challenging despite advances in visual quality. Latent world models offer predictive signals for evaluating physical plausibility, but higher agreement between predicted and observed features may reflect easier-to-predict scene content rather than more plausible dynamics. We propose \textbf{S}elf-Referenced \textbf{P}hysical \textbf{A}lignment \textbf{R}ewards (\textbf{SPAR}), a framework that uses world-model predictions for inference-time physical alignment. SPAR compares each video's prediction error with that of static clips constructed by repeating its own frames, providing a content-matched reference for evaluating generated dynamics. To account for the uneven spatial and temporal distribution of physical interactions, we introduce a unified \textbf{spatiotemporal reward refinement} strategy. \textbf{Spatial refinement} emphasizes localized prediction discrepancies, while \textbf{temporal refinement} moderates brief error peaks and supplements prediction errors with agreement between predicted and observed feature changes. Evaluations across multiple benchmarks demonstrate that SPAR improves the physical plausibility of both \textbf{text-to-video generation} and \textbf{image-to-video continuation}, bringing generated dynamics into closer alignment with the physical regularities governing real-world events. Additional results and resources are available at https://gavin-jy.github.io/spar-page/

</details>

Abstract (英文): Vision foundation models can precisely segment objects based solely on a text prompt. However, they struggle to recover objects that are small, occluded or whose appearance is poorly captured by the original textual description. Vision language models can examine the image and its context, they can propose richer descriptions of the objects to be detected. But these models struggle with precise segmentation and localization. We introduce Visual Thinking, a training-free framework that combines a vision-language model with Segment Anything Model 3 (SAM 3) through an iterative active perception loop. At each iteration, the VLM proposes short noun phrases using the image, candidate crops and history from past iterations. A controller ranks these phrases using embedding similarity between the original prompt and the proposal, an entropy-based verification priority and the novelty of the prompt. Next, SAM 3 uses prompts to detect objects in the image. We associate detections across iterations and update each object's confidence score using a weighted mean of scores obtained from SAM 3 with a larger weight for detections using narrower prompts. We supply confident detections as positive visual exemplars for SAM 3, and incorporate image tiling to recover small objects in cluttered images. We evaluate counting and localization on CARPK, FSCD-147, PixMo-Count, and OmniCount (Fruits). Compared with SAM 3, Visual Thinking reduces the counting mean absolute error (MAE) by about 18 on FSC-147 and about 8 on CARPK. It improves box average precision (AP) by more than 12 on FSCD-147 and CARPK. Visual Thinking, with fixed or no tiling, performs on par with Claude Opus 5.5 at extra-high reasoning effort, while running on single desktop GPU in about 5.91 seconds per image, compared to about 46.53 seconds per image for Claude through its API.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Modeling 4D hand-object interaction (HOI) is fundamental to understanding manipulation. This requires physics, since hands move objects only by touch and each material responds differently. Yet existing methods ignore this physics or simulate only a narrow range of matter. Programs can instead describe any matter with its physics, but coding agents omit the hand that drives the interaction. We close this gap with CodeHOI, to our knowledge the first agentic framework that writes physically plausible 4D HOI as programs. To write one, an Object Agent first builds each object with the physics of its matter. An HOI Agent then specifies the hands by their contacts and simulates each kind of matter with an established physics solver. Finally, an Evaluator Agent checks each result against physical laws. We also introduce CodeHOI-Bench with 100 reconstruction and generation tasks across four kinds of matter. With off-the-shelf frontier models, CodeHOI writes interactions that are plausible for every kind of matter and remain so when transferred to an independent physics engine. It thereby enables coding agents to write physically plausible HOI across diverse matter.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Looped transformers reuse one block of shared weights along the depth of the network. Recent looped diffusion transformers are trained from scratch, which repeats the full pretraining cost although strong pretrained non-looped diffusion transformers exist. We propose Distill to Loop (Dooop), which distills a pretrained non-looped diffusion transformer into a looped student whose blocks are initialized from the teacher's. We cast distillation as a fixed-point iteration toward the teacher, in which the output after every loop is trained toward the teacher, and each loop is one step of an ordinary differential equation in depth. As a result, one model runs any number of loops at test time without retraining, and the higher-order ODE solvers of diffusion model sampling can be applied along the depth dimension for better results. Meanwhile, because a looped model produces an output after every loop, we also construct loop guidance between the outputs of different loop counts, which improves the generated samples at little extra compute. On SD3-medium, a looped student with about 37% of the teacher's parameters matches the teacher on average over GenEval, ImageReward, PickScore and HPSv2.1 with two to four loops, and with two loops it uses about 58% of the teacher's compute. And on a four-step SD3-medium, loop guidance raises the average of the four metrics from 88.2 to 99.2.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Recently, physics-inspired neural networks have gained widespread attention due to their interpretability. The closed-form, frequency-time decoupled solution of the wave equation offers linear complexity and allows different frequency components to evolve independently, making it a potential alternative solution for efficient image super-resolution. Therefore, a physics-inspired wave evolution attention framework is proposed, namely WaveSR. The WaveSR framework involves two key modules, i.e., wave evolution attention (WEA) and Frequency-Adaptive Feed-Forward Network (FA-FFN). The WEA module achieves feature interactions through the analytical solution of the 2D wave equation in the Fast Fourier Transform (FFT) domain, enabling independent evolution of different frequency components with learnable wave speed \(c\) and damping coefficient \(α\). The FA-FFN module realizes adaptive frequency feature selection through learnable spectral modulation, effectively enhancing feature representation capability. Subsequently, we employ a two-stage training strategy with a hybrid objective that combines a spatial-domain pixel loss and a frequency-domain loss to improve reconstruction quality. Our method provides a preliminary exploration of physics-inspired neural networks for efficient image super-resolution.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Even when multimodal large language models (MLLMs) answer incorrectly, their internal cues often point to the relevant region. A widely used remedy re-presents the cue-selected region as an enlarged crop to supply finer detail. However, we find that much of its gain requires no new detail: it largely survives when the crop comes from the already-seen low-resolution input, and partly even when the region's cached tokens are merely copied. Part of re-presentation's gain thus comes from drawing attention to evidence the model already holds. We propose InCue, a test-time framework that makes better use of this evidence without additional visual input. InCue treats visual attention and predictive-entropy gradients as Internal Cues, couples them into a spatial target, and optimizes only an offset on the existing visual tokens to steer attention toward it. Under matched visual-token budgets, InCue achieves state-of-the-art performance, consistently outperforming recent test-time methods and improving four MLLMs by up to 8.9% on V$^*$ and 23.4% on its hard samples. Its gains carry over to eight general benchmarks. Code will be available at https://github.com/paul-swu/InCue.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Comparing two entities in an image, multimodal LLMs (MLLMs) often answer wrongly. We find that the answer bias enters not when the model sees, but when it decides with the image in view: the model's own descriptions of the two entities often already hold the right answer. We therefore propose D$^3$ (Describe, Don't Decide): the model describes each entity alone, decides from these descriptions without the image, and merges this decision with its direct answer. Without any training, D$^3$ improves all 79 model-benchmark pairs across ten benchmarks and eight 7-9B MLLMs. On held-out GQA comparison questions, the mean accuracy of these models rises from 72.4% to 83.2% (up to +21.5% per model), higher than that of Qwen2.5-VL-32B answering directly (82.8%). Code will be available at https://github.com/paul-swu/describe-dont-decide.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Reconstructing images from brain activity has two broad goals. The first is to build brain-computer interfaces. The second is to understand how the brain sees. The eyes deliver incomplete, noisy input, so the brain must fill in the gaps with its expectations of what natural scenes look like. This filling-in relies on the brain's prior over natural images. Image reconstruction lets us test which natural image prior best matches the brain's natural image prior. However, most previous work on image reconstruction relies on an unnatural condition: subjects must hold their eyes still. In reality, primates constantly move their eyes to see the world. We introduce a framework for reconstructing images from neural activity while a primate freely views a static natural scene. We record from five monkeys with high-density electrode arrays in the lateral geniculate nucleus (LGN) and primary visual cortex (V1). The recorded receptive fields cover only a small part of the image. Thus, we combine neural activity across eye movements, trials, and sessions to reconstruct the full image. We first present a simple linear method with a 1/f prior. We then scale this approach up with modern deep learning. We train a decoder by tokenizing spiking activity and measured gaze; these tokens are fed into a transformer and diffusion model to reconstruct the full scene. Three factors determine reconstruction quality: the amount of neural evidence combined over viewing time, the accuracy of eye tracking, and the choice of image prior. Our method sets a new state-of-the-art performance in reconstructing natural images from brain activity, across brain recording modalities: fMRI, EEG, MEG and electrophysiology.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Many pan-sharpening (PS) networks reconstruct high-resolution multispectral (HRMS) images by adding learned residuals to 4$\times$-interpolated low-resolution multispectral (LRMS) inputs, incorporating panchromatic (PAN) detail only through these residual corrections. This raises a simple question: Why start with a blurry base when sharp spatial detail is already available? We propose PanBase, a sharper multispectral starting point designed for more effective residual learning. Guided by physical sensor characteristics, PanBase combines PAN spatial detail with LRMS spectral information while enforcing consistency with the LRMS observations under sensor-specific blur. We construct this base without neural network training, estimating only nine coefficients per spectral band through closed-form calibration. Our PanBase can be easily integrated into existing backbones with a global residual skip through two simple changes: (i) replacing the skip base with PanBase and (ii) concatenating it as an additional input. Using each backbone's original training recipe and matched training budgets, PanBase improves most reduced- and full-resolution metrics on WorldView-3, QuickBird, and GaoFen-2, while achieving 4.4$\times$ faster training convergence on average. In zero-shot evaluation on WorldView-2, standalone PanBase achieves competitive reconstruction without target-sensor fitting or neural refinement.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Promptable video trackers such as SAM2 achieve accurate and robust performance through strong temporal consistency, making them important foundations for downstream video understanding and tracking applications. As their use continues to expand, understanding their robustness to adversarial perturbations becomes increasingly important. Existing attacks on SAM2 largely focus on local mask degradation and short-term temporal feature disruption, leaving its longer-term dynamics and competing object interferences (Distractors) insufficiently explored. We observe that SAM2 itself exposes useful distractor cues when tracking becomes uncertain. Based on these observations, we propose DistractAttack, a white-box attack that uses background distractors to disrupt target tracking persistently. Its core procedure, Counterfactual Distractor Search (CDS), propagates clean and attacked trajectories in parallel to discover plausible competing regions and identify those that become increasingly effective under perturbation. The discovered evidence is accumulated over time to weaken target support and promote recurrent alternatives or disappearance predictions. Extensive experiments under video-level and one-shot perturbations across Visual Object Tracking and Video Object Segmentation demonstrate strong attack effectiveness, raising DRE to $4.4\times$ and NRE to $7.3\times$ their nearest baselines under video perturbation, while transferring effectively across model scales and tracker variants in black-box settings. Project page: https://adonaydem.github.io/sam2-da-page/

</details>

Abstract (英文): A score that distinguishes videos with different temporal-consistency ratings can miss differences associated with motion preference. VideoScore's temporal consistency score distinguished higher- from lower-rated Pika videos in 95% of comparisons with 15 videos per group, yet could not detect the difference between preferred videos and their same-prompt alternatives in any of the 200 comparisons of 41 VideoGen-RewardBench pairs. We introduce DynaCue, a two-sample permutation test that combines complementary measurements of motion, frame continuity, and appearance to compare groups of videos without choosing a single cue in advance. DynaCue measures local changes in optical flow both before and after accounting for each clip's usual activity, captures interruptions through frame differences and freeze counts, and incorporates scores for motion and appearance. In controlled experiments, DynaCue detected group differences across five interventions affecting playback continuity, displacement, or rhythm. In real-world evaluations of generated videos where viewers rated temporal consistency or chose which of two videos they preferred for its motion, DynaCue detected group differences in at least 80% of resampled comparisons on each of the three datasets at the smaller sample sizes studied. Every other method evaluated on all three had a detection rate of 60% or less on at least one dataset.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Latent action models (LAMs) learn action-like latents from unlabeled video by coupling frame-pair inference with latent-conditioned prediction, enabling policy and world-model pretraining. However, what the latent must explain depends on the representation space used for action inference and transition prediction. When this space entangles the agent's evolving physical state with visual context, such as background and persistent agent appearance, inferred latents can encode information unrelated to the agent's actions. To address this challenge, we propose Agent-State LAM, which jointly learns a compact agent-state representation and latent actions modeling its transitions. The compact state aggregates dense visual features into a shared space for both action inference and forward prediction. To guide what this state retains, we introduce spatially matched temporal discrimination (SMTD), which trains the state to distinguish its source frame from nearby frames through spatially flexible matching with frozen local visual features. Rather than comparing global frame representations, SMTD scores each frame by emphasizing its strongest local feature matches across spatial locations. This scoring allows background and persistent agent appearance to remain matched across frames despite displacement, making these shared cues less informative for identifying the source frame and encouraging the representation to capture the agent's evolving physical state. Extensive experiments on distracting control tasks and six real robot video datasets demonstrate improved action recovery, downstream control, and world-model prediction. Code and checkpoints are available at https://github.com/Mid-Push/Agent-State-LAM.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Specialist medical vision-language models (VLMs) are strong within their specialties but hard to unify: their training data are often private, and independently developed specialists cannot be assumed to share an architecture. Deploying these large models separately is costly. We present HuluOPD, an on-policy distillation recipe that unifies heterogeneous specialists into one lightweight VLM using only unlabeled prompts, without requiring reference answers or the specialists' training data. The lightweight model answers each prompt, and the specialist of the prompt's modality supervises its response; a continuation-aligned cross-tokenizer loss makes this possible even when they differ in architecture and tokenizer. We apply HuluOPD to five specialists, for ultrasound, pathology, optical coherence tomography, fundus photography, and dental imaging. To distill and evaluate them, we build HuluOPDCorpus, 914k unlabeled prompts, and HuluOPDBench, 47k held-out test items, from over 90 public datasets, and score all models under one protocol on 132k test items, including third-party benchmarks. A single model with 10% of the five specialists' combined parameters retains 94% of their question-answering performance, and one with 2% retains 89%. To our knowledge, this is the first demonstration of unifying heterogeneous medical specialist VLMs without requiring their training data or a shared architecture.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Bi-temporal satellite imagery is central to disaster assessment: deciding what was damaged requires comparing a pre-event scene with its post-event counterpart. A vision-language model (VLM) can nonetheless appear to answer change questions while relying mainly on the post-event image or on the prompt's temporal framing. We present a controlled diagnostic of temporal grounding and apply it to one lightweight VLM, InternVL3-2B, on DisasterM3: 1,659 optical and SAR image pairs from 41 events and nine disaster types, in event-disjoint partitions, with 9,031 reasoning questions. We compare post-only input, the true pre/post pair, the post image repeated twice, reversed image order, and an unrelated pre-event image. The true pair scores $+0.5$ accuracy points above the repeated-post control (95\% paired bootstrap CI $[-0.2, +1.2]$), and reversing the order changes accuracy by $-0.2$ points ($[-0.8, +0.5]$). Under this protocol we therefore find no measurable benefit from the content of the true pre-event image and little sensitivity to temporal order. The model is not blind to the first image, however: the true pair scores $2.5$ points above a pair whose pre-event image comes from another event ($[+1.6, +3.4]$). State-transition and multi-hop questions remain hard, and optical and SAR inputs show different profiles. We outline temporal scene graphs as a testable next step, not a validated solution, and argue that temporal benchmarks for Earth observation should report whether answers depend on the intended evidence, not only how often they are right.

</details>

Abstract (英文): The realistic rendering of 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) has drawn interest from real-time applications that require maps to be built online. This led to coupling 3DGS with SLAM, providing camera poses and initial Gaussians for the map. However, the map is still optimized with designs inherited from conventional SLAM, which train only on keyframes selected for localization and regularize geometry with uncertain frontend depth. These designs overlook the model complexity of a 3DGS map: with millions of parameters but few views of each region, the map overfits to sparse inputs. Resolving this is challenging, as every view and Gaussian added to the map competes for the same real-time budget. We close this gap from both sides. To add views while keyframes preserve the structure of the map, we progressively admit non-keyframe images as mapping updates complete and allocate the limited updates across different groups of frames according to the supervision each group provides. To control the model complexity of the map, edge-guided initialization places new Gaussians where image detail requires them. To complement the uncertain frontend depth, Self-Referenced Opacity Regularization (SOR) uses accumulated rendering evidence, at no extra rendering cost, to suppress the opacity of Gaussians that persistently contribute in front of a map-derived surface reference. Together, these designs improve both the appearance and the geometry of online maps within the real-time budget.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Occupancy world models predict future 3D scenes conditioned on ego actions, yet their predictions may not faithfully follow the specified trajectory under counterfactual actions. Because driving datasets provide sparse coverage of alternative trajectories for each scene, existing models learn the spatial effect of ego motion implicitly from observed scene--trajectory combinations, which may entangle action effects with scene--trajectory correlations. We propose ReCast, an ego-trajectory-guided transformer that grounds future prediction in action-induced geometric correspondence across ego-centric frames. ReCast decouples historical encoding from future decoding and aligns deformable interactions using relative ego transformations, while retaining flexibility for non-rigid scene evolution. We also introduce a counterfactual action-faithfulness benchmark covering in-distribution, tail, and out-of-distribution trajectories. On Occ3D-nuScenes, ReCast achieves competitive standard forecasting performance while improving counterfactual forecasting, with particularly clear gains under out-of-distribution trajectories.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Generating 3D garments for digital characters requires preserving a reference design while adapting the garment to the target body's shape and pose. Sewing-pattern methods can struggle to reproduce unfamiliar designs and fine details, while image-to-3D generators offer rich shape priors but typically lack explicit 3D body context. We present \textbf{BodyWeave}, a framework that unifies garment generation and fitting through native 3D body conditioning. Building on 3D generative priors, BodyWeave uses the body as a spatial reference throughout geometry generation: a \textbf{Global Body Adapter} guides garment placement and extent in the shared coordinate frame, while \textbf{Local Body Attention} guides surface refinement using nearby body geometry. A \textbf{Surface Penetration Loss} supervises decoded surfaces to reduce body intersections, and the resulting geometry conditions material synthesis to produce garments with physically based rendering attributes. To support learning across designs and wearers, we construct \textbf{GarmentWeave3D} with 293K aligned garment--body observations, including 114K same-design cross-body pairs. Evaluations on out-of-domain \textbf{Artist-Created Garments} demonstrate high geometric fidelity and low body penetration. Cross-body evaluations demonstrate accurate adaptation to new wearers with consistent garment--body spacing, while appearance comparisons show faithful fabric reproduction. Additional results and resources are available at https://gavin-jy.github.io/bodyweave-page/

</details>

Abstract (英文): Self-attention over tens of thousands of tokens dominates the generation time of video diffusion transformers, because its cost grows quadratically with sequence length. The FP4 matrix products of modern GPUs can speed up attention, but the 4-bit rounding of the queries Q and the keys K introduces large errors. We show that the exponential in softmax amplifies the error of their product QK, which therefore dominates the attention error. We propose DeCo-Attention, a co-design of algorithm and system for low-precision attention. DeCo-Attention is training-free and compensates the rounding error of Q and K along a few principal directions found by a per-head eigendecomposition. The lightweight compensation terms are appended to Q and K as extra channels, so the FP4 matrix product corrects its own rounding error while it computes the attention scores. Once the products run in FP4, the softmax bounds the kernel, and we optimize the softmax so that the gain of FP4 computation translates into realized speedup. With QK in FP4, we implement DeCo-Attention with the rest of attention in FP8 on GB300 and VR200 and in FP4 on RTX PRO 6000. On Wan2.2, these kernels run 2.13x, 2.49x, and 2.82x faster than BF16 attention, and the whole generation runs 1.55x, 1.68x, and 1.80x faster. On four video generation models, the FP8 configuration achieves LPIPS comparable to prior 8-bit QK methods, and the fully FP4 configuration lower LPIPS than prior fully 4-bit kernels.

</details>

Abstract (英文): LiDAR reconstruction supports closed-loop autonomous driving simulation, but hours of per-scene optimization make large asset libraries costly to build and maintain. We present Instant LiDAR, a feed-forward pipeline that reconstructs multiple LiDAR scans as reusable Gaussian assets in minutes without per-scene optimization. LiDAR measurements provide strong geometric priors, but directly initializing Gaussians from them yields poor reconstruction due to nonuniform sampling and a limited field of view. Sparse returns leave surface gaps, while combining scans expands coverage but introduces redundant primitives. To address these challenges, Instant LiDAR combines measurement-anchored Gaussian prediction, sampling-aware Gaussian scales, and multi-frame consolidation. The predictor refines attributes anchored to recorded returns, while the scales adapt spatial support to LiDAR sampling. Multi-frame consolidation merges overlapping predictions into a compact asset spanning the driving log. Compared with NuRec, Instant LiDAR reconstructs scenes approximately 46 times faster on Physical AI (PAI) and 70 times faster on Waymo. It also achieves comparable downstream 3D detection accuracy on Waymo.

</details>

Abstract (英文): In-context segmentation (ICS) uses one or a few annotated reference images to specify what to segment in a target image. Existing training-free ICS approaches typically rely on manually designed inference procedures, which may limit how flexibly reference information is combined. Learned in-context inference offers an adaptive alternative, but acquiring this capability through segmentation-specific training can compromise generalization to unseen domains. We therefore ask: can a pretrained in-context learner infer what to segment from annotated references without segmentation-specific training? We introduce SegPFN, a training-free framework that reformulates ICS as tabular classification over frozen visual features. A frozen tabular prior-data fitted network (PFN) uses labeled reference patches as context to classify patches in the target image. We comprehensively benchmark SegPFN against major existing ICS methods across 11 datasets spanning semantic, part, and personalized segmentation. SegPFN improves overall mean intersection-over-union (mIoU) by 4.7 points over the strongest overall baseline and achieves the highest average mIoU in four of five task-shot settings. Further analyses show that hard-negative reference patches improve fine-grained target discrimination, while joint conditioning enables SegPFN to use multiple references effectively. These results show that SegPFN offers a simple and effective paradigm for training-free in-context segmentation.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Surgical scene understanding is constrained by data scarcity and long-tail class imbalance, as annotating surgical videos demands scarce clinical expertise while rare procedural events remain underrepresented. We tackle this through data generation and propose SurgGenesis, a generative surgical world model that learns future surgical state transitions in a latent space from visual observations and textual procedural descriptions, serving as a scalable surgical data engine. To adapt a large-scale video diffusion model to the data-scarce surgical domain without catastrophic forgetting, we introduce a three-stage progressive LoRA strategy that incrementally injects endoscopic visual priors, procedural semantics, and spatial motion cues. Rather than judging generation by visual fidelity alone, we validate SurgGenesis along two axes that separate a world model from a generic generator: (i) future-awareness, by comparing predicted futures against the real future through a GT-latent prediction gap and temporal-horizon analysis, and (ii) action-faithful usefulness, by checking that generated clips contain the commanded surgical triplet and that using them to rebalance long-tail events and drive downstream understanding (segmentation, triplet recognition, 3D reconstruction) yields action-specific gains. Conditioned futures therefore complement conventional long-tail sampling. Code: https://zh-qr.github.io/SurgGenesis

</details>

Abstract (英文): We propose LoopDVR, a framework for converting pretrained Transformer-based dense visual recognition models into parameter-efficient looped networks. Rather than reducing the network width or depth directly, LoopDVR reuses Transformer blocks across layers in both the ViT backbone and Transformer decoder, reducing the number of parameters while retaining the original computational structure. However, naively sharing parameters can disrupt the distinct transformations learned at different depths. To address this challenge, we identify suitable sharing groups based on inter-layer representation dissimilarity and keep the final backbone block independent for multi-scale feature construction. We then initialize each shared backbone block with the pretrained weights of its group's terminal layer to better preserve the learned representations. An independent Loop Adapter at each layer further helps retain depth-specific transformations within shared blocks. Subsequently, we distill visual representations into the looped backbone and integrate it with a looped decoder for task-specific training. Experiments demonstrate that LoopDVR achieves the best accuracy--parameter trade-offs across dense recognition tasks. Compared with the reference models, it reduces parameters by approximately 28% while maintaining competitive accuracy. Notably, LoopDet-S achieves 50.9 box AP with only 7.0M parameters, highlighting its effectiveness in compact models.

Abstract (英文): The widespread deployment of latent generative models (LGMs) makes generated image source attribution essential for copyright auditing and data governance, yet practical attribution should remain reliable under common post-processing and platform-induced processing. Existing reconstruction-based attribution methods summarize an image's reconstruction response to a single global scalar, such as the reconstruction error or the ratio of two consecutive errors. These summaries discard channel-frequency structure, allowing image content and post-processing variation to obscure source evidence and making attribution after spreading difficult. We instead uncover a more stable source-discriminative cue under post-processing: the frequency-resolved evolution of residual energy across repeated applications of the same reconstruction operator. Motivated by this insight, we characterize a phenomenon we term Spectral Contraction and use it to build SpCon, a source-attribution framework that accesses only the target model's VAE. SpCon obtains adjacent residuals through two deterministic reconstructions, builds a spectral-contraction representation from their frequency-resolved relative energy change, and aggregates Student-t response densities for each source-condition pair over a finite set of fitted reference components. The same representation supports three settings according to non-belonging reference availability: none (Target-Only, TO; belonging references only), external out-of-domain sources (External-Reference, ER), or known in-domain competitors (Known-Reference, KR). Across seven LGMs, TO, ER, and KR attain average AUROC/AP of 0.8786/0.6344, 0.9404/0.7886, and 0.9652/0.8626 over the 35 conditions, all outperforming the strongest baseline (0.8154/0.5277), and achieve 0.8581/0.5507, 0.9369/0.7549, and 0.9734/0.8773 on the three social platforms.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Spatial reasoning in hospitals requires tracking look-alike equipment, interpreting patient-relative directions, and judging passage widths, yet existing benchmarks provide limited coverage of these abilities. We introduce Clinical3D-Bench, a simulated benchmark with 3,368 questions of eight types on egocentric walkthroughs of 373 hospital-room layouts. Layouts follow hospital design guidelines, answers are computed from the 3D scenes, and paired scene states test whether models change their answers when relevant evidence changes. Videos are photorealistic, and ten physicians screened the layouts. Among 35 multimodal large language models (MLLMs), the best open and proprietary models reach 45.7% and 53.6% macro accuracy, against 40.8% for an answer prior and 79.0% for physicians on a subset, and 27 of 32 open models score below 40% on two-way patient-relative directions. Fine-tuning on Clinical3D-45K, our 44,750-question training set, lifts two MLLMs 16-20 points above the best proprietary model. 3D maps given to the fine-tuned MLLM as input go largely unused: replacing them with another video's, even when they are ground truth, barely changes its accuracy. The proposed GeoJeV instead lets the MLLM compute with 3D: it reconstructs a scene memory from each video, selects 3D operations and their arguments from the MLLM's question state, and adds their evidence to its option scores. It achieves the highest accuracy of all evaluated models, narrowing the gap to physicians.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Post-training quantization (PTQ) compresses large vision-language models (LVLMs) by calibrating quantization parameters on a small dataset without retraining. However, despite increasingly sophisticated calibration strategies, prior methods suffer substantial performance degradation under low-bit weight--activation quantization. To understand this degradation, we locate where quantization error is concentrated and examine the corresponding weight and activation distributions. We observe that a few outliers dominate the ranges shared by per-channel (PC) weights and per-token (PT) activations, leaving insufficient resolution for most values and mapping many of them to zero. These findings motivate a shift in focus from calibration refinement to the underlying quantization representation. We therefore present Rotate-and-Refine Quantization (RRQ) as a simple, effective baseline for LVLM PTQ, combining local Hadamard transforms with group quantization and efficient group-aware execution. Across 8 multimodal tasks and diverse quantization settings, RRQ substantially outperforms baselines while reducing inference time and memory usage. At W4A4, it retains over 96\% of full-precision performance on 8B models and over 99\% on a 32B model, compared with less than 8\% for these baselines. The source code is publicly available at https://github.com/kmc3661/RRQ

</details>

Abstract (英文): Graphical user interface (GUI) agents translate screenshots and user instructions into executable actions. Most existing approaches rely on vision-language models to generate actions as text, often leading to substantial inference latency due to autoregressive decoding. To address this inefficiency, we propose GUI-PRISM, a hybrid action decoder that directly predicts action types and structured arguments while retaining language generation for open-ended text. Unlike existing spatial decoding methods that primarily focus on target localization, GUI-PRISM extends direct prediction to action types and their associated arguments, enabling complete GUI action prediction. Specifically, GUI-PRISM employs field-specific queries to extract action-relevant semantic and spatial information from a shared multimodal feature bank. It further models dependencies among action components through conditional queries. Gated residual cross-attention refines these predictions using fine-grained multimodal information. GUI-PRISM achieves 95.1\% accuracy on ScreenSpot-v2, outperforming the strongest head-based spatial decoding method by 2.0 percentage points while delivering a 3.8$\times$ inference speedup over the baseline. On AndroidControl-v2 and MM-Mind2Web-v2, it improves complete-action accuracy by 4.8 and 0.7 percentage points while achieving 2.7$\times$ and 3.9$\times$ inference speedups over the baseline, respectively. Our code is available on our project page.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Text-to-scene methods turn a short instruction into a furnished 3D room, but the room that a particular person wants rarely comes across fully in words: many preferences surface only when the person sees a concrete room and can point to what they like and dislike in it. We therefore study personalized indoor scene synthesis under hidden user preferences: over several turns, a system shows a 3D scene, and the user, who sees only rendered images of the scene, replies with brief free-form feedback and a satisfaction score. The system must therefore bring the whole room toward the user's preferences from feedback that comments on only part of it at a time. It must also be able to change every part of the room, because a preference concerns not only single objects but the look and atmosphere that surfaces, lighting, textiles, furniture and their arrangement create together. We present PreferenceScene, in which a large language model keeps an explicit estimate of the user's preferences for every design attribute of the room and, after each turn, infers the taste behind the feedback and revises the whole estimate, including the parts that the feedback does not mention. A scene instantiation module builds every estimated value as its own part of the room, so that each of these parts can follow the user's preferences. We evaluate on the test set of a benchmark with 320 instances in ten room types, with a simulated user and a render-based evaluator. PreferenceScene matches the hidden preferences better than five published text-to-scene baselines adapted to the same interaction, and its match rises with every turn. It even surpasses them when they are given the preferences directly, and a vision-language model rates its scenes as the most realistic and functional.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Graphical user interface (GUI) agents translate user instructions and screenshots into executable actions. To learn executable actions, existing approaches often rely on explicit reasoning during training, which encourage over-reliance on specific reasoning patterns rather than generalizable action-relevant information. In this paper, we propose an implicit framework, RISE, which integrates action-relevant reasoning into implicit state representations to guide GUI action prediction. To this end, action-relevant reasoning is captured through three complementary supervision objectives: full-CoT state alignment preserves information across the reasoning process, action-distribution alignment encodes action preferences, and reasoning-to-action boundary alignment captures the representation immediately before action generation. In this case, our RISE effectively learns informative implicit representations, thereby improving the accuracy of next-action token prediction. RISE achieves 70.6\% accuracy on OSWorld-G, establishing state-of-the-art performance among compared 8B models with a 4.3-point gain over its backbone. Results across two distinct backbones and six diverse benchmarks further demonstrate its effectiveness in GUI grounding and action prediction. Our code is available on our project page.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Coronary computed tomography angiography (CCTA) depicts coronary anatomy and cardiac fat, but segmentation alone does not ensure anatomically consistent measurements. We propose an automated framework that combines deep learning with anatomical reasoning for quantitative cardiac phenotyping. Its anatomical measurement module uses graph geometry, branch identity, and surrounding structures to define vessel-specific pericoronary adipose tissue (PCAT) sampling and a surrogate epicardial adipose tissue (EAT) compartment. The latter also generates training targets without requiring expert pericardium contours. A companion module quantifies coronary calcium on non-contrast CT. Component-wise benchmarking was conducted on five public datasets: ImageCAS, ImageCAS-X, COCA, Visual Lab, and MultiD4CAD. On ImageCAS-X Test-160, lumen segmentation with validation-selected post-processing achieved a Dice score of 0.9133 and a clDice score of 0.940, exceeding the corresponding published CAS-Net point estimates. In the four-split ImageCAS evaluation, the four-member ensemble outperformed the single ResEnc-L model. Analysis of 800 paired scans showed that PCAT attenuation measurements differed between whole-vessel and lumen references. External EAT evaluation showed strong volume correlation and good spatial overlap. Explicit anatomical rules make the resulting measurements interpretable and inspectable. In practice, the framework provides a standardized pipeline for processing cardiac CT cohorts and quantifying coronary calcium, PCAT, and EAT for population-level analysis and comparison across studies. The source code is publicly available at https://github.com/HarryTRAN207/CardiacCT-Research

</details>

Abstract (英文): Switching scenes while preserving the original subject in streaming video generation exposes a conflict: cached history helps maintain the subject but also sustains old-scene objects that should disappear. Through controlled interventions on text conditioning, denoising steps, the local window, and the attention sink, we identify how cached visual history keeps this obsolete content. When the original frames remain in the cache, KV flush and sink recomputation still leave old-scene objects in the generated videos. Replacing this history with target-scene history largely removes those objects, but also changes the subject, reducing face similarity to the pre-switch subject. We then propose Molt Forcing, a training-free method that rebuilds scene history from a cold-start target scene while selectively retaining subject tokens from the original cache. Across 16 scene families, Molt Forcing removes old-scene objects at least as reliably as an Echo-Forcing hard cut while keeping the subject much closer to its pre-switch appearance.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Fine-grained text editing aims to accurately replace text in a specified region while preserving the source text's font, color, and texture, as well as visual consistency in unedited regions. We present EditText, a unified framework for fine-grained text editing that combines target character generation with visual transport from the source image. The model uses dual-condition Fill inputs: a masked image and a source text style reference, both sharing a spatial reference with the target image. The masked image hides the original text and retains unedited content as a source canvas; the style reference supplies appearance cues that guide new character generation together with the target text. We introduce an Attention Transport framework in which Source Weight describes the attention allocated to a condition and the Spatial Aggregation Kernel describes its internal spatial attention distribution. Accurate transfer of source details depends on attention strength, the retention of details in condition features, and spatially accurate aggregation. For selected non-edit queries, we therefore preserve the total attention weight assigned to the masked image while redistributing its attention toward spatially corresponding neighborhoods, enabling selective visual transport. Multi-stage training, combined with a Glyph-aware Structural Loss on local target character structure, teaches the model to generate accurate text, preserve source text appearance, and retain unedited details. We also introduce TBench, a fine-grained text editing benchmark covering Alphabetic, Logographic, and Arabic numerals, to evaluate character updates, appearance preservation, and the preservation of unedited details. On TBench, EditText achieves state-of-the-art non-edit-region SSIM (0.9769) and full-image LPIPS (0.0460).

</details>

Abstract (英文): Diffusion models are increasingly used for medical image reconstruction and super-resolution, but their samples may contain plausible fine-scale hallucinations that are difficult to identify from the reconstructed image alone. We propose Med-PODiff, a patch-wise proper orthogonal decomposition (POD) diffusion framework for brain MRI super-resolution that performs generative reconstruction in compact and interpretable local coefficient spaces. Unlike pixel-space or learned-latent diffusion models, Med-PODiff uses the POD structure to separate low-rank anatomical variation from fine-detail residual variation, enabling predictive uncertainty to be decomposed into total, coarse, and fine components. Building on this decomposition, we define a pixel-wise hallucination-risk score, $H_p$, that emphasizes regions where fine-detail uncertainty dominates coarse structural uncertainty. On brain MRI, Med-PODiff outperforms representative diffusion baselines in reconstruction quality while producing well-calibrated predictive intervals, with total uncertainty detecting high-error regions at AUROC 0.954. More importantly, the decomposed uncertainty is scale-selective: coarse and fine uncertainty preferentially predict errors at the corresponding scales, with $H_p$ localizing fine error at AUROC $0.906$. Controlled injections and retrospective Cartesian $k$-space undersampling further support fine-detail localization and degradation robustness. The discarded POD tail is only $3.8\%$ of the retained fine-error magnitude, and Med-PODiff improves downstream segmentation consistency while requiring substantially less inference time than pixel-space diffusion.

</details>

Abstract (英文): In embedding-based video anomaly detection (VAD), normal clips can have high similarity to anomalous descriptions, an anomaly bias that leads to false positives. Methods such as PRISM mitigate this bias by measuring deviations from a normality reference along anomaly directions. Both the normality reference and the anomaly directions are derived from text and shared across videos, although normal activity and the appearance of anomalies vary across scenes. The resulting scores are also sensitive to prompt selection. To address these limitations, we propose Visual Intrinsic Distribution Estimation (VIDE), a training-free framework that adapts text-derived scoring to each test video using frozen clip embeddings. VIDE estimates normal scene content from clips with low initial anomaly scores and uses this estimate to partially correct the textual reference. It then rescales category responses, penalizes clips with strong responses across categories, and refines the anomaly directions using high-scoring clips. VIDE averages scores across prompt views to reduce sensitivity to prompt selection and fuses scores from two frozen encoders. With both encoders, VIDE improves AUC over the best PRISM result by $1.5$, $1.9$, and $3.5$ points on UCF-Crime, XD-Violence, and MSAD, respectively, and AP by $6.5$ points on XD-Violence and $9.3$ points on MSAD. VIDE yields the highest AUC among the compared training-free methods on all three benchmarks and the highest reported AUC among all compared methods on XD-Violence and MSAD. Our code is included in the supplementary material and will be made publicly available.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Color Vision Deficiency (CVD) affects a large population worldwide, yet practical recoloring must run continuously on-device. Frame-wise video recoloring produces color flickering, while existing methods rely on costly cross-frame alignment in pixel or feature space. We observe that color flickering correlates with variations in channel-wise responses, with color-sensitive variations concentrated in a low-rank subspace. Based on this observation, we propose Lightweight Spatio-Temporal Adaptive Recoloring (LiteSTAR), built around LiCaD, a plug-and-play Lightweight Channel-Adaptive Decoder. LiCaD performs temporal filtering in a low-rank state space with learned process and observation uncertainties. It then modulates feature channels to achieve temporal consistency without explicit spatial alignment. LiteSTAR further employs adaptive patch routing to improve color discrimination for CVD users while avoiding unnecessary over-enhancement. We also introduce EgoCVDSet, the first egocentric video benchmark for CVD recoloring. Extensive quantitative and qualitative experiments demonstrate that LiteSTAR achieves superior performance with only 0.05M parameters. LiCaD further demonstrates strong generalization across different tasks and network architectures. The code, models, and dataset will be released after review.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Multi-contrast MRI super-resolution accelerates clinical protocols by reconstructing high-resolution (HR) target modalities from low-resolution (LR) inputs, guided by easily acquired reference contrasts. While purely data-driven deep neural networks achieve strong performance via rich representation capacity, they rely on heuristic fusion mechanisms (e.g., naive concatenation or black-box cross-attention) that lack theoretical grounding. Alternatively, model-based deep unfolding networks enforce principled cross-contrast fusion through explicit priors, yet their unrolled steps remain constrained to shallow convolutions, lacking modern deep blocks to capture global context. To bridge this gap, we propose CSC-MAMBA, unifying deep-unfolded multimodal convolutional sparse coding with the Vision Mamba state-space backbone. By fusing common structural priors from the guidance modality via unfolded sparse coding and globally refining contrast-specific representations with a Mamba back- bone, our approach benefits from interpretable, model-guided fusion and long-range spatial context. Experiments on the IXI dataset demonstrate that CSC-MAMBA outperforms state-of-the-art methods in reconstruction quality, achieving up to a 0.62 dB gain in PSNR while remaining computationally efficient.

</details>

Abstract (英文): First-order reinforcement learning, such as Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), is widely used to post-train Vision-Language Models (VLMs) for multimodal reasoning. However, it incurs heavy memory and system overhead from backpropagation, optimizer states, and a reference model. Forward-only zeroth-order (ZO) methods, such as Evolution Strategies (ES) and its top-k variant Augmented Random Search (ARS), avoid these overheads via reward-guided weight perturbations, yet remain unexplored for VLM reasoning. We find that directly applying standard ES or vanilla ARS to VLMs suffers from two fundamental failure modes: (i) patch-grid overfitting, fitting fragile grid-specific visual token artifacts while ignoring spatial invariants, and (ii) catastrophic forgetting, degrading general perception and out-of-distribution (OOD) tasks due to unbounded high-rank parameter and policy drift. We propose ARS-VLM, a visually aligned Augmented Random Search framework for forward-only VLM post-training. First, ARS-VLM extracts a contrastive visual basis from bilinearly paired cross-grid views, concentrating perturbations on shared visual signals while suppressing patch-shift artifacts. Second, ARS-VLM anchors shallow visual-fusion layers with a soft subspace prior while freeing deep reasoning layers, restraining policy drift without a reference model. Third, ARS-VLM scores perturbations on paired views to discard view-fragile directions during top-k selection. We evaluate ARS-VLM on Qwen3-VL-4B and Qwen3-VL-8B on multimodal reasoning tasks. On 8B, ARS-VLM reaches 61.0% validation accuracy in 20 steps versus 150 for GRPO (7.5x fewer optimization steps) and avoids the late OOD collapse of vanilla ARS and ES. Moreover, ARS-VLM achieves 75.41% single-view micro accuracy across seven OOD vision reasoning benchmarks, compared with 75.31% for full-parameter GRPO, using forward-only post-training.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Training pixel diffusion models remains less straightforward, owing to the ad hoc training recipes and architecture designs, along with the sensitivity to hyper-parameter choices. We show that strong denoisers can be built by refining existing pixel-native designs from the literature. Specifically, we revisit the long skip connection in U-Net and find that the optimization benefit commonly attributed to it is not necessary for its denoising benefit. We further find that the conventional linear combination of features from the skip and main backbone is inadequate for regimes of various noise levels. Motivated by these observations, we introduce two core modifications. First, we adopt asymmetric long skips, which propagate fine-grained information from input embeddings to decoder layers. Second, we introduce adaGLU, a SwiGLU-like MLP block, in decoder layers to adaptively filter information from the embeddings before being added to main-branch features. The above designs give a simple pixel diffusion transformer architecture. When trained from scratch, it scales smoothly from small-scale baselines up to 1.6B parameters, achieving 1.62 and 1.70 FID on ImageNet-256 and -512, respectively. Besides, it natively supports multimodal inputs, such as text and images, yielding competitive performance in text-to-image generation. We release our codes at https://github.com/AMAP-ML/PiT

</details>

Abstract (英文): Evaluating the physical plausibility of generated videos is critical for reliable robotic planning with world models. These videos inform action proposal and assessment before real-world execution, so undetected physical violations can lead to erroneous assessments of action feasibility and safety. Yet current video generators often produce physically implausible interactions. Detecting such violations directly with vision-language models (VLMs) remains challenging as physical verification requires evidence-grounded spatiotemporal reasoning beyond high-level semantic understanding. We develop five law-specific metrics for evaluating physical plausibility by combining spatiotemporal evidence from segmentation, tracking, 3D geometry, or appearance comparisons with targeted VLM reasoning: Contact fidelity, Object persistence, Solidity, Robot deformation, and Object deformation. Building on this metric suite, we introduce RoboProbe, an agentic evaluation framework that coordinates metric execution and selectively refines evaluations through constrained actions. To evaluate the physical plausibility of robot world models, we further introduce RP-Bench, with 761 human-labeled generated videos and 130 real demonstrations of 239 manipulation tasks from 12 robot datasets. On RP-Bench, all five metrics achieve AUROCs above 0.85 and outperform the evaluated learned judges, prior evaluators, and a frames-only Gemini baseline. Applying these metrics to five video generators reveals distinct patterns of physical violations across models, in both violation types and frequencies.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Existing clip-based soccer commentary models often produce delayed, repeated, or poorly synchronized narration on untrimmed full-match videos. Recent streaming video-language models make low-latency full-match narration feasible, but they often favor general descriptions rather than professional, event-grounded soccer commentary. They may describe nearby actions while failing to mention key events such as goals, cards, substitutions, and offsides correctly and on time. In this paper, we present SoccerNarrate to bridge low-latency streaming narration with event-grounded professional soccer commentary across data, training, and evaluation. First, we construct SoccerNarrate-Data, a large-scale time-anchored dataset with word-level ASR alignment, quality filtering, and entity calibration. Second, we perform macro-window preference alignment (MWPA), which aligns the model with complete event-level commentary semantics by comparing causal multi-step rollouts from the same streaming prefix while keeping second-level inference unchanged. We further use event-mismatched human commentaries as counterfactual negatives to emphasize event semantics over commentary style. Third, we introduce SoccerNarrate-Eval, an event-centric benchmark based on temporally constrained event entailment. Full-match experiments show that SoccerNarrate improves event coverage and event-aligned precision over strong offline and streaming baselines. Code and models are released at https://github.com/MCG-NJU/SoccerNarrate.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Geometric rotary position encodings (RoPE) incorporate 3D camera geometry into multi-view attention by rotating token features according to viewing rays, depths, and reprojected image coordinates. Each coordinate is rotated across a progression of frequencies--its frequency band--yet existing implementations leave this band to inherited defaults, either retaining standard 2D RoPE's top frequency or letting the frequency span grow exponentially with the attention head dimension. We show that this unstandardized setting can reverse benchmark rankings. In novel view synthesis on RealEstate10K, RayRoPE matches the strongest baseline in the 47M model (144-channel heads), yet trails by 1.2 dB in the 25M model (48-channel heads), and exhibits the same reversal when varying head size alone at fixed model width. Populating a 243x range with four frequencies instead of the 3x default at 48 channels recovers 0.9 dB of that gap. Across view synthesis and two-view depth estimation, overly narrow bands consistently blur correspondence, whereas all three projection-based encodings perform reliably when their top frequency lies within 0.3--3 rotations per 8-pixel patch (10--90 rotations across 256x256 RealEstate10K). At the opposite extreme, overly wide bands degrade RayRoPE through a forward-backward asymmetry: averaging rotations over a predicted depth interval damps high frequencies in the forward pass, yet leaves their gradients with respect to predicted depth undamped. Blocking only those high-frequency depth gradients recovers 90--93% of the lost PSNR, showing that forward smoothing does not guarantee backward stability. Guided by this analysis, we distill practical guidelines--setting bands in image coordinates, populating spans in small heads, and avoiding unconditional same-view damping--and advocate reporting and matching frequency bands in future multi-view benchmarks.

</details>

Abstract (英文): MediaStormBench is a multimodal benchmark for real-world film and television production, with each subset drawn from MediaStorm's day-to-day work. We release its first subset, MediaStormBench-CameraLog (CameraLogBench), on a common foundational task in footage ingest and initial color grading: identifying the Log curve of a clip without metadata. Cameras record Log footage by compressing a wide range of scene light into code values with a vendor-defined logarithmic curve, and color can be restored correctly only once that curve is known. The curve, however, is recorded only in file metadata, easily lost in transcoding and team hand-offs. A colorist then has to try candidate curves one by one against a waveform scope, and a wrong choice shifts the tones and colors of the whole clip. Recovering the curve from the image alone is challenging, because vendor curves cluster tightly in code value and are coupled with unknown exposure. CameraLogBench is built on professionally shot footage and tests whether multimodal models can identify the curve, with a probe that removes the correct option to expose guessing from priors. Because every candidate curve is strictly defined by a published vendor formula, we derive four controls from one implementation of these formulas: graded negatives, synthetic frames of identical scenes, in-context specification digests, and a training-free formula baseline, which together decouple semantic priors from photometric measurement. Across 11 models, real-footage accuracy barely exceeds always guessing the most frequent curve; when the correct option is removed, models almost never abstain, revealing heavy reliance on scene and brand priors. Supplying the vendor specification sharply improves recognition on calibrated charts but hardly helps on natural images without a photometric reference, exposing a gap between reading a specification and measuring pixels.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Scaling inference computation offers a way to improve diffusion transformers (DiTs), but taking more sampling steps leaves the depth of each denoising prediction unchanged. Recurrently reusing Transformer blocks increases computation within each step, allowing further refinement of hidden representations. Retrofitting pretrained models enables this capability without repeating pretraining, but requires blocks learned for fixed-depth computation to refine states produced by their own repeated application. The challenge is to use each additional pass to correct local errors without disrupting the structure established by earlier passes. We introduce LoopFit, which uses feedback from earlier passes to guide refinement at each image patch. Residual Attention Modulation (RAM) uses each patch's feature change in one pass to scale and shift its input to attention in the next. During training, Residual Relay Loss (RRL) gives greater weight to patches whose prediction errors persist across passes. A noise-dependent depth schedule speeds up sampling by using four passes at high noise and one at lower noise levels. At comparable cost, backbones trained with four passes solve more problems using recurrent depth than extra sampling steps. Across 12 visual puzzle settings, LoopFit solves 89.8% of boards, compared with 87.8% for the strongest baseline evaluated on all 12. Most of the gain comes from tracing long paths on mazes and frozen lakes.

</details>
