Abstract (英文): Reliable driving VLMs must do more than answer correctly or localize relevant scene actors: they must determine whether the available visual evidence is sufficient to support an answer. Existing evaluations largely treat grounding and answerability as separate measurements, making it difficult to distinguish well-supported predictions from those localized to relevant but insufficient evidence. We introduce DriveAnswer, a benchmark of 20,151 questions across seven reasoning families, that operationalizes this distinction using matched strong- and weak-evidence configurations. Each pair preserves the underlying scene fact and semantic answer while changing whether the observations support answering, making abstention the appropriate response under weak evidence. On the six-family evaluation, the strongest zero-shot baseline reaches 67.31% strong-evidence accuracy, yet only 21.55% of matched questions satisfy both answer-abstain behavior and complete grounding. With controlled adaptation, Gemma-4-12B reaches 63.29% on this joint capability, nearly tripling its zero-shot performance. Qwen3-VL-8B exhibits the same broad improvement in evidence-sensitive answering and grounding. These results show that semantic correctness, evidence sufficiency, and grounding are distinct capabilities that should be evaluated jointly.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Accelerating instruction-based image editing when one instruction requests several edits is challenging. Many training-free accelerators for flow-matching image editors skip tokens whose prediction has stopped changing, but decide this with a single threshold for the whole image. With several edits, such a threshold, tuned for speed, freezes small and slowly settling edits first, and requested edits disappear. We propose TIDE (Training-free Instruction-aware Drift-based Editing), which gives every instruction part its own compute budget. First, TIDE finds the region of each part in the editor's own attention by unbalanced optimal transport, without masks or external models. Second, it freezes a token once its drift is small relative to its own part and moves it along the straight path to its endpoint estimate. Third, a sparse engine computes only the remaining tokens. We prove that part-agnostic rules cannot guarantee per-part error budgets, characterize the optimal per-part threshold policy up to one tie class per part, and derive when our rule attains it. On FLUX.2 Klein, one global threshold breaks at least one edit's budget in 89% of MICE-Bench images, and TIDE drops up to 77% fewer edits at matched or higher speed (48% on MICE-Bench), a gap human raters confirm. On the many-step Klein Base, TIDE is the fastest compared method on six benchmarks (4.81-4.89x) and needs 5.3x fewer TFLOPs than vanilla, the largest saving we measure. With the same settings, it is also the fastest compared method on Qwen-Image-Edit-2511, a second model family (3.46-3.52x), and drops the fewest edits on all six benchmarks. Code will be made publicly available.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Recent advances in deep learning and the public availability of computed tomography (CT) datasets with paired reports have enabled many Text-to-CT generators. However, their evaluations almost always differ in preprocessing and metrics, which hinders comparison. Moreover, their protocols almost never test how editing the report changes the CT, not even at the same seed, so the report is often assumed to control the anatomy without evidence of how much and how consistently it does. We therefore propose CtrlCT, a toolkit that tests two properties of a generator, Credibility and Control. Credibility tests compare generated CTs, as a population, with real CTs and reports; Control tests are metamorphic and need no reference CT: at a fixed seed, editing one finding should change it as requested, keep negated findings absent and shift distant anatomy no more than a new seed, and the change should hold across seeds. A Report Card summarises all measures and insights for each generator. We benchmark five public generators on 940 chest CTs and 687 edits of pleural effusion and cardiomegaly, holding out a sixth to test the toolkit. Among them, the most credible generators barely respond to an added effusion, while the most responsive produce less realistic and less diverse CTs. The analyses proposed in the literature do not reveal this. We release CtrlCT, its edits, seeds and 30,525 synthetic CTs after acceptance.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Temporal localization in long videos is challenging because brief, question-relevant events are dispersed among extensive redundant content. Limited visual budgets increase the risk of omitting these events or their temporal context, undermining reliable reasoning. Existing frame selection methods emphasize semantic relevance or temporal coverage, often overlooking the complementary evidence needed to locate related events and establish their temporal relationships. To address this limitation, we propose TESS, a training-free framework for query-guided temporal event-structured frame selection. TESS selects frames that jointly show the reference event and the event asked about, in the required temporal order. Leveraging frozen vision-language representations, TESS integrates soft temporal gating, role-aware budget allocation, and within-role redundancy suppression to preserve complementary event evidence. Extensive experiments across four long-video understanding benchmarks and three multimodal backbones demonstrate that TESS consistently outperforms the strongest compared baseline by 4.0-5.5 percentage points under a fixed frame budget. Further analyses reveal greater improvements on longer videos and robust performance under tighter visual budgets, while independent evidence annotations confirm enhanced event and temporal-relation coverage. These findings establish explicit temporal evidence organization as an effective training-free strategy for preserving event relationships and supporting efficient temporal reasoning over long videos. Our code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/TESS_code.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Can a simple mirror expose the limitations of today's robotic manipulation policies? Inspired by the mirror self-recognition test in animal cognition, we investigate whether robot policies can reliably manipulate physical objects in scenes containing their reflections. We introduce RoboDojo-Mirror, a benchmark built on RoboDojo that preserves task objectives, success criteria, and policy checkpoints while comparing a mirror-free baseline with single-mirror, dual-mirror, and mirrored-tabletop conditions. Across five policies spanning Vision-Language-Action models, World Action Models, and agentic policies, every mirror condition reduces aggregate success rates: averaged over the three conditions, the drop ranges from 10.8 to 56.7 percentage points across policies, and it reaches 62.5 points on the mirrored tabletop. Because a mirror is also a physical obstacle, we compare it against geometrically matched non-reflective panels; 50--88\% of mirror-specific failures occur without recorded robot--panel contact, so direct collisions alone do not explain the degradation. Trajectory and contact analyses instead reveal recurring behaviors consistent with phantom targeting of reflected objects and spatial mislocalization of real ones. Targeted diagnostics further characterize grasping near reflections, policy-dependent pushing progress, and physical-target interaction under main-camera occlusion. Real-robot experiments with $π_{0.5}$ and FastWAM also show lower overall success rates under the upright-mirror and mirrored-tabletop conditions. These findings reveal a shared vulnerability to reflective environments across diverse robot policies and motivate reflection-aware evaluation of embodied intelligence. Code is available at https://github.com/iPPLab/robodojo-mirror.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Models read a book, an hour of video, or an image in one forward pass. As the context grows, this pass slows down, loses accuracy, and stops at the context window. Sparse attention is efficient, but keeps the other two limitations. Grid probing, a recent training-free method, recovers the accuracy and extends the reach by asking a frozen model the same question about short local and strided spans of the input, but every question repeats the whole computation, since each span is encoded again. Our insight is that grid probing is emulated attention, an attention pattern reproduced from outside the model through prompts, since each probe only restricts which part of the input the question can see. We introduce Factorized Context Attention (FCA), a training-free block-sparse attention that runs the same pattern natively, as the attention of the model itself. FCA encodes the input once into a KV cache, reads all spans as masked sequences in one batched forward, and uses a stair position schedule to keep every span inside the trained position range. Across long video understanding, long-context text inference and image grounding, FCA matches the accuracy and the reach of grid probing at a fraction of its cost. It gains about 10 points over the single pass on long video and 9 to 14 points on long novels, finds needles in videos of over a thousand frames and in texts four times the length of the window, and answers a later question about the same input in as little as a tenth of the time of grid probing. Native attention thus keeps what emulated attention gains without its cost, all with existing frozen language and vision-language models.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Event cameras capture asynchronous brightness changes at high temporal resolution, but scarce recordings constrain data-driven research. Sparse RGB observations cannot uniquely determine intervening events since identical frames may correspond to streams with different activity, polarities, and timing. Existing simulators largely rely on prescribed sensor dynamics and estimated brightness trajectories, whose assumptions limit the diversity and fidelity of plausible event streams. We introduce EventDiffusion, a hierarchical generative framework that directly learns the conditional distribution of native asynchronous event streams from real recordings. EventDiffusion decomposes generation into three probabilistic processes: Event Activity Diffusion (EAD) learns stochastic discrete event counts; Event Spatial Allocation (ESA) distributes these counts to individual pixels while preserving cardinality; and Event Timing Diffusion (ETD) learns continuous asynchronous timestamp distributions. We also introduce CEFS, an event-stream realism metric combining event activity and polarity with multiscale space-time structure and temporal fidelity. Across all datasets, EventDiffusion achieves double the accuracy of SOTA methods and demonstrates robustness across different cameras at any resolution.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Defect-free industrial samples can vary considerably in appearance, texture, and geometry. In few-shot anomaly detection, this variability has to be modeled from only a handful of images. SubspaceAD showed that a single principal subspace of foundation-model patch features is a strong training-free model of nominal appearance. However, a single subspace has to cover all of the nominal data's variability, so a deviation that is anomalous for one semantic region can be within the nominal variability of another. We propose LocalSubspaceAD, which extends SubspaceAD from a single subspace to a mixture of subspaces, assigning each group of patches with similar features its own subspace. The set of patch-features is partitioned using k-means, one subspace is fit per cluster, and each patch is scored by the minimum of re-projection residuals over all subspaces. The proposed method requires no training and no EM refinement. On MVTec AD, VisA and Real-IAD with 1, 2 and 4 shots, it improves over a single subspace (SubspaceAD) on both evaluated backbones: by 1.2 to 1.8 points image AUROC on DINOv2, and by 1.4 to 4.4 points in image AUROC, pixel AUPR and AUPRO on DINOv3. The gains are most pronounced on objects with heterogeneous nominal appearance, such as multi-instance categories and PCBs. Ablations show that the gains stem from a finer-grained model of variability through locally oriented subspaces, rather than from the subspace offsets (cluster means). We further show that the gains do not stem from added capacity alone: the local subspaces appear to capture the structure of coherent clusters in feature space, and the model does not merely act as a compressed memory bank.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Video foundation models have emerged as powerful generators of dynamic visual content, exhibiting remarkable capabilities in synthesizing complex actions and interactions. We argue that these models have also become excellent motion generators, with motion priors that are richer and more general than those learned by dedicated motion models. Their advantage extends beyond visual realism and is manifested in two important aspects: motion naturalness, including coherent body coordination, physical plausibility, and realistic interactions with objects and the environment; and motion scope, encompassing a much broader range of activities and semantic coherence, particularly uncommon, complex, and compound actions. To investigate this position, we introduce a common evaluation framework that compares video and dedicated motion models through three complementary settings: text-only motion generation, reconstruction of video-generated motion from sparse keyframes, and video-based enrichment of motion-model outputs. By converting all outputs into a shared 3D skeletal representation, we evaluate motion naturalness, semantic alignment, action coverage, physical motion characteristics, and fidelity to motion constraints. Our results suggest that video foundation models can generate more natural and semantically meaningful movements, particularly for complex and compound actions. Moreover, we investigate motion-conditioned generation through loose skeletal control, finding that dedicated motion models do not consistently improve video-generated motion, while video models can enrich skeletal animations using sparse motion guidance. Our findings suggest a shift in the role of specialized motion models, from generating complete animations to guiding and controlling the richer motion capabilities already embedded in video foundation models.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Long-horizon panoramic video wandering aims to continuously synthesize scene-consistent $360^\circ$ observations along user-specified camera trajectories from an initial equirectangular projection (ERP) panorama. Existing panoramic video models have achieved promising results in camera-controlled generation, while recent 3D-guided approaches further provide geometric guidance for improving scene consistency. However, stable long-horizon wandering remains challenging due to diverse motion--orientation relationships and imperfect memory guidance induced by accumulated generation artifacts and depth inaccuracies. To address these challenges, we propose \textbf{PVWander}, a 3D-memory-guided framework that accumulates generated observations into an online point-cloud memory and reprojects it to guide subsequent generation, with two complementary designs: \textit{Path-Facing canonicalization} for motion--orientation alignment and \textit{Timestep-aware gating modulation} for adaptive memory guidance. \textit{Path-Facing canonicalization} exploits the horizontal cyclic symmetry of ERP panoramas to uniformly align each clip's ERP orientation with its principal translation direction, yielding more consistent, forward-like relative motions while preserving the prescribed camera path. \textit{Timestep-aware gating modulation} exploits the coarse-to-fine nature of diffusion denoising to adaptively regulate reprojected memory guidance through timestep-dependent channel-wise gates, preserving its structural benefits while mitigating visual artifacts from imperfect projections. Extensive experiments demonstrate that PVWander achieves improved long-horizon generation quality, while ablations validate the effectiveness of both designs.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Video world models synthesize photorealistic observations in response to user actions but struggle to remain consistent over long interactions. Coding agents and text-to-3D models instead generate explicit 3D worlds whose geometry and state persist by construction, but whose appearance falls far short of photorealism. Recent methods combine the strengths of both by rendering such worlds into coarse proxies that guide a video model. However, since these proxies only specify where things are, the renderer must invent what they look like as the world is explored, and anything it forgets is reinvented when a surface reappears. Our key insight is that a mesh identifies which surface each pixel shows, across frames and views, even as objects move, allowing it to serve as an address for what the renderer generates. We propose M2R (Mesh to Remember), a few-step causal video renderer that stores its own features on the surfaces they depict, in each object's frame, and retrieves them by rasterizing the mesh whenever those surfaces come back into view. Appearance thus travels with objects wherever they move, persists across revisits, and is shared by every agent that observes it, while memory grows with the surfaces seen instead of the number of frames. Trained only on synthetic data, M2R transfers zero-shot to real indoor scans and to worlds authored by coding agents. It outperforms state-of-the-art methods in consistency across static, dynamic, and multi-agent benchmarks, more than doubling object permanence and nearly doubling cross-agent consistency, while rendering 720p video in real time (40 FPS).

Abstract (英文): Pedestrian forecasting from ego-view observations is challenging because occlusion, limited visibility, and tracking errors corrupt observed motion histories. Such errors are particularly consequential for autonomous robot navigation. Existing paradigms typically either impute noisy history positions before forecasting or jointly train an auxiliary history reconstruction branch. However, predictors trained on clean histories may be sensitive to residual errors in recovered histories at deployment. Meanwhile, auxiliary reconstruction still computes inter-agent interactions over raw, corrupted coordinates. We investigate conditioning future prediction on both recovered historical states and raw observation evidence, with progressive training to accommodate imperfect recovery. Building on this insight, we propose ObsBridge, a flow-based trajectory forecasting framework with observation-grounded historical bridging. Specifically, ObsBridge first employs a frozen estimator to recover historical motion from agent-relative and ego-relative positions, image-plane locations, and visibility states. During training, we introduce an agent-wise curriculum that gradually replaces clean teacher histories with complete estimated histories before context encoding. This progressively exposes the predictor to estimated histories during training. Furthermore, we design a residual geometry adapter to fuse the encoded motion with original observation evidence. We aggregate the resulting representations through social attention to condition the flow-based trajectory generator. Extensive experiments across multiple benchmarks demonstrate that ObsBridge outperforms state-of-the-art methods. On EgoTraj-TBD, it reduces minADE@20 by 6.5\% and minFDE@20 by 8.7\%. The code will be provided at https://github.com/shawnliang420/ObsBridge.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Pansharpening aims to fuse a single observed image pair, comprising a low-resolution multispectral (LRMS) image and a panchromatic (PAN) image, to obtain a high-resolution multispectral (HRMS) image. Recently, deep learning-based methods have achieved significant performance due to their strong capability of extracting nonlinear features. However, distribution shifts between training data and test images can degrade the performance of pretrained pansharpening networks, limiting their generalization in real-world scenarios. In this work, we propose an instance-specific test-time adaptation framework that uses the observed PAN-LRMS pair to select and update only a subset of the pretrained network's parameters. Specifically, the framework uses cross-scoring to identify parameter groups whose updates improve reconstruction across image patches. It then updates the selected groups by minimizing a loss that combines a spectral consistency term with a newly designed spatial consistency term. Experiments show that the adapted networks perform better than their pretrained counterparts and outperform representative state-of-the-art pansharpening methods on GaoFen-2, QuickBird, and WorldView series datasets.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Visual decision-making demands accurate judgments and timely responses. Yet for multiple questions sharing the same visual input, autoregressive multimodal large language models generate answers token by token, accumulating sequential decoding costs as question count grows and delaying completion of the decision set. Their predictions can also vary with question order. We introduce ViDM, which transforms multi-question visual inference into parallel decisions in a single forward pass, improving both accuracy and inference efficiency. ViDM organizes shared visual context, multiple questions, and question-specific decision slots into one sequence, reusing the native language-model head to produce all answer distributions simultaneously. A Decision-Query Mask aligns each decision query's direct attention with shared context and its corresponding question, while case-balanced multi-order training strengthens robustness to question reordering. Across nine benchmarks spanning image, multi-image, and video tasks, ViDM improves Qwen3.5-4B's average accuracy from 73.49% to 77.55% while achieving 8.4x the baseline's average end-to-end throughput. On GQA, where each evaluated image has 11.4 questions on average, the end-to-end speedup reaches 20.3x. ViDM also improves mean and worst-order accuracy while reducing order-induced variation across multiple benchmarks, with further validation on LLaVA-OneVision-7B and InternVL3.5-4B.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Multi-reference image generation requires a model to preserve the appearance of selected subjects while arranging them according to an instruction. Despite recent progress in reference-based generation and compositional control, reliably organizing selected subjects into the intended scene remains challenging. Although existing methods provide various forms of compositional guidance, the planned relationships are not always explicitly instantiated for inspection and refinement before image synthesis. To address this limitation, we introduce MAGPIE, which represents the intended composition as a simplified 3D scene defined by an executable Blender program. A coding agent links each scene instance to its reference, checks the geometry and rendered view against the instruction, and revises the program to correct discrepancies. The final proxy rendering serves as a visual plan for the image generator. Using MAGPIE-Instruct-3K, we train the generator to follow this plan while drawing each subject's appearance from its reference. MAGPIE improves both Qwen and FLUX on DreamOmni2 and SconeEval. With FLUX, it improves overall success on DreamOmni2 by 28.21 percentage points over the original backbone and achieves the highest overall score among the models evaluated on SconeEval.

Abstract (英文): Underwater degradation changes image appearance and can compromise the semantic cues used to guide enhancement. Identifying scene content alone does not explicitly specify the appearance to recover or the input-dependent changes associated with that target. We propose target-discrepancy knowledge distillation (TDD) for underwater image enhancement, which organizes guidance around these two aspects of the same restoration problem. For each degraded-reference training pair, reference features from an underwater vision-language teacher provide target-appearance supervision, while paired feature residuals describe the observation's discrepancy relative to the same target. Both representations are jointly distilled into a lightweight guidance network that predicts them from the degraded image alone; neither the teacher nor reference images are required at inference. To align their use with their supervisory roles, we introduce progressive knowledge fusion within a multi-scale enhancement network. The estimated discrepancy first conditions spatial feature updates, after which target-appearance guidance refines the updated features and channel interactions consolidate their responses. This correction-before-refinement design lets target-guided refinement build on discrepancy-conditioned features. Experiments on six underwater benchmarks demonstrate competitive performance across full-reference and no-reference metrics. Ablations and feature visualizations analyze the contributions of the knowledge source, joint distillation, and progressive fusion.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Vision-language models often rely on global context and language priors while overlooking answer-determining local details. Privileged visual on-policy self-distillation supervises full-image student rollouts using a frozen teacher that views high-resolution evidence crops. Yet our cross-scale diagnostics show that models retain over half of their full-image accuracy when local evidence is completely masked, indicating that single-image correctness does not guarantee reliance on local evidence, while the need for visual correction also varies across tokens with their spatial attention states. We propose STAR, a visual on-policy self-distillation framework combining independent counterfactual dual-world rollouts with spatial-attention-guided target reconstruction. Local image editing constructs paired worlds with identical questions and backgrounds but distinct answer-determining facts, where a shared student samples trajectories independently to expose contextual shortcuts under image-consistent prefixes. Within each world, the teacher contrasts real and mean-color crops to determine the visual correction direction, while the divergence between student image attention and a teacher-centroid spatial reference adaptively controls token-wise target log-probability reconstruction. Without auxiliary attention losses or test-time tool overhead, STAR improves Qwen3.5 across scales by up to 8.04 percentage points in average accuracy over initialization across six fine-grained benchmarks, reaches OPSD's final accuracy $4.7\times$ faster, and generalizes to out-of-domain reasoning tasks.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Action-conditioned world models play a vital role in robotics and autonomous driving, yet existing video diffusion models struggle to maintain dynamic object persistence. When the camera turns away and returns, out-of-view moving entities fail to evolve plausibly in latent space, often mutating or vanishing entirely due to bounded perspective views. To resolve this issue, we propose OmniPersist, a unified framework that couples perspective video generation with panoramic evolution within a single-stage diffusion model, without relying on auxiliary models or initial panoramic inputs. To maintain efficiency, we model the panoramic stream at a lower frame rate, bridging both views across space and time via Geometric Anchoring (GeoAnch) for spatial correspondence and Temporal Compensation (TemComp) for temporal aggregation. OmniPersist is agnostic to camera-control architectures, supporting both latent-level and attention-level conditioning. Extensive experiments on a newly constructed dynamic benchmark across image-to-video and video continuation tasks demonstrate consistent gains in visual quality, camera controllability, revisit consistency, and dynamic persistence.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Human and scene foundation models offer complementary strengths for monocular 4D reconstruction: human models recover metric-scale body meshes but lack scene grounding, whereas scene models estimate globally consistent 3D geometry and camera motion without reliable metric scale. Crucially, both models reconstruct the same foreground human surface. Yet existing methods do not directly exploit this shared surface: lacking reliable foreground human geometry in scene reconstructions, earlier approaches either resort to sparse contact constraints or learn data-driven alignment modules that generalize poorly to out-of-distribution scenes. We present COHERE4D, a training-free framework that directly registers the human surfaces predicted by independently pretrained human and scene foundation models to reconstruct a coherent, metric 4D world. COHERE4D establishes dense, pixel-aligned 3D correspondences over the visible human surface and employs RANSAC with cross-frame consensus to suppress outliers from occlusion, background contamination, and depth noise. This formulation solves for two complementary geometric quantities: a sequence-level shared scale that calibrates scene geometry and camera motion to metric human size, and per-frame human translations that align camera-space human meshes with the scaled scene geometry to enforce spatial and temporal consistency. Across diverse backbones and benchmarks, COHERE4D substantially outperforms prior methods in human--scene alignment, metric scene and camera pose reconstruction, and global human motion estimation, adding only 4,ms/frame, enabling overall inference at up to 14 FPS.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Composing an editable 3D scene from images requires an asset for each object and a layout that places the assets. 3D generation and CAD retrieval now supply the assets, but layout still lacks a general solution: scene generation methods couple it with generation, and pose estimators handle one object at a time and need true-size CAD models, depth, or iterative refinement. We present X-3DComposer, a feed-forward model that predicts the layout separately from asset generation. Given one or more images of a scene, the object masks, and an asset for each object from any source, it jointly predicts the rotation, translation, and per-axis scale of all objects in a single forward pass of about one second. The key idea is to treat objects as views: renders of the assets are processed together with the scene images by a multi-view geometry model adapted from VGGT-$Ω$, and each object pose follows in closed form from the cameras predicted for its renders. Camera intrinsics, camera poses, and depth are optional inputs. When the meshes produced by five scene generation methods are kept fixed and only their layout is replaced, the layouts of X-3DComposer are more accurate than the native layouts of these methods. Compared with the stand-alone layout model SceneMaker, it reduces the scene-level Chamfer distance by 52% on IndoorMix, our test set from six indoor datasets, and by 28% on 3D-FUTURE.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Analytical multi-view multi-object tracking (MV-MOT) systems rely on measurement covariance estimates to guide association, fusion, and state estimation. These covariance estimates are typically heuristic, requiring hand-tuning to the specific deployment scenario. We investigate how learned heteroscedastic measurement covariance affects calibration and robustness in analytical multi-camera tracking. To support this analysis, we introduce CalibTrack, a detector-side predictor that replaces uniform measurement covariance with per-detection covariance. We evaluate CalibTrack on the WildTrack and MultiviewX pedestrian tracking datasets with multiple analytical tracker cores, including an identity-informed GM-PHD, JIPDA, and pruned delta-GLMB filter. We additionally evaluate robustness by simulating adverse weather conditions with stable diffusion, Gaussian noise, camera browning, and camera dropout. CalibTrack improves average adverse tracking performance over confidence-scaled covariance, while producing better calibrated predictions, indicating the value of heteroscedastic covariance within analytical tracking. Comparisons with stronger heuristic covariance methods show that the benefits of learned covariance remain bounded by the quality of available observations and the downstream tracker choice. Together, these findings provide a principled exploration of learned covariance estimation and its potential for uncertainty-aware analytical tracking.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Consistency is essential to generative workflows: repeated processing should preserve information beyond the changes explicitly requested. In latent diffusion models, this requires stability both when moving between latent and pixel spaces and when applying successive edits. Autoencoding errors and unintended changes from the editing model can accumulate across these operations, progressively altering image structure, texture, and color. We introduce Déjà-VAE, a framework that combines exact autoencoding consistency with calibrated editing stabilization, without training or fine-tuning. Its autoencoding component converts a pretrained variational autoencoder into a surjective pseudo-invertible neural network, with a fixed gain balancing deviations from the pretrained encoder and decoder. The modified decoder $D'$ is an explicit nonlinear right inverse of the encoder $E'$, satisfying $E'(D'(z))=z$ by construction. Consequently, $D'$ is a reflexive generalized inverse: latent round trips preserve their input, up to numerical precision, and repeated image reconstruction is idempotent. To suppress editing drift, adaptive wavelet back-projection finds the closest latent to the editor's output in $\ell_2$, subject to matching the previous latent on wavelet coefficients whose changes fall below calibrated thresholds. This correction is applied once per turn, with no additional network evaluations after calibration. Together, these components support workflows that remain in latent space or interleave generation with pixel-space operations and constraints. Tests across models and editing benchmarks show less drift than stock pipelines and dedicated consistency methods.

</details>

Abstract (英文): This study integrates satellite remote sensing, groundwater monitoring, and machine learning to map leached-metal-associated hazards at four coal combustion residual (CCR) sites in Virginia, USA. Constituent-specific groundwater concentrations were expressed as Hazard Quotients (HQ) and combined into a composite Hazard Index (HI). Predictors included Sentinel-2 vegetation indices, biophysical parameters, and spatiotemporal metrics (2019-2025), alongside soil, climate, hydrology, topography, and land cover. Six supervised classifiers were evaluated using spatially separated test blocks, feature-set ablation, and leave-one-site-out testing. Higher HQ coincided with lower vegetation indices in 24 of 33 metal-site combinations near monitoring wells (sign test p = 0.007). Well HI was negatively correlated with CIRE and canopy chlorophyll content at three sites (Spearman rho as low as -0.71). Random Forest achieved within-site HI classification accuracies of 0.768-0.924 and higher-HI-class recall of 0.965-0.999. For 34 constituent-site combinations, HQ classification accuracies ranged from 0.775 to 0.989. Environmental covariates generally contributed more predictive information than vegetation features. Removing vegetation predictors did not reduce performance, and distance to the CCR unit alone performed best at one site. Zero-shot cross-site accuracy declined to 0.734-0.749, no better than the majority-class baseline. These findings demonstrate the potential of integrating satellite observations and environmental data with groundwater measurements for site-calibrated spatial hazard screening. The framework identifies indirect environmental associations rather than directly detecting dissolved metals, highlighting the importance of local calibration and independent validation.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Vision-language-action (VLA) planners inherit semantic knowledge from pretrained vision-language models, but their reasoning is rarely grounded in 3D scene structure and its evolution over time. We introduce GeoVLDrive, a geometry-grounded VLA framework for 3D spatiotemporal reasoning and planning. Multi-layer features from a 3D foundation model augment the vision-language backbone through gated cross-attention. A structured latent chain of thought (CoT) organizes future scene geometry, key-agent motion, and meta-actions into ordered, separately supervised latent states. An action expert generates trajectory proposals from these states and multi-layer scene features, and ranks them using scene-grounded, interacting score tokens. We also introduce NAVSIM 3DQA to train and evaluate metric 3D spatial and motion understanding. GeoVLDrive achieves 91.8 PDMS on NAVSIM v1 and 90.9 EPDMS on NAVSIM v2. On NAVSIM 3DQA, the geometry-grounded backbone scores 89.2% overall, 4.6 percentage points above the fine-tuned Qwen3-VL-2B baseline. On LingoQA, the backbone achieves 68.6% Lingo-Judge accuracy.

Abstract (英文): Accurate and efficient monocular 3D hand pose estimation is an important perception capability for embodied human interaction and dexterous manipulation. Recent diffusion-based methods model pose uncertainty by iteratively denoising 3D joint coordinates. However, directly diffusing the final joints requires the generative process to simultaneously explore plausible poses and recover precise coordinates, often relying on additional denoising steps or multiple hypotheses and consequently increasing inference cost. In this work, we propose DiffAnchor, a few-step anchor-space diffusion framework that diffuses anchors rather than joints. Our key insight is that stochastic exploration does not need to operate directly on the final output. Instead, diffusion searches for informative intermediate anchors that serve as local 3D regressors, while anchor-to-joint offset prediction and weighted aggregation complete precise joint localization. Specifically, fixed context anchors provide stable global references, whereas noisy dynamic anchors are iteratively refined toward informative joint regions. An importance-guided anchor selection and replenishment mechanism further retains useful anchors, removes ineffective ones, and explores insufficiently covered regions. This design reduces the precision burden of the diffusion process and enables accurate prediction using only a few denoising steps and a single hypothesis. Extensive experiments on HO-3D V2, NYU, ICVL, and InterHand2.6M demonstrate state-of-the-art performance on the first three benchmarks and competitive performance on challenging interacting-hand estimation, while maintaining inference efficiency comparable to deterministic regression baselines.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Open-vocabulary semantic segmentation (OVSS) enables flexible, text-driven mapping of remote sensing (RS) imagery beyond predefined categories. Despite advances in segmentation foundation models, RS-OVSS remains constrained by the scarcity of large-scale, semantically diverse datasets with accurate pixel-level annotations. Meanwhile, existing RS datasets contain abundant scene- and object-level annotations that offer valuable semantic information but lack precise pixel-wise supervision. To exploit these complementary sources, we propose HS-OVSeg, a unified training framework that integrates heterogeneous scene-, object-, and pixel-level supervision for RS-OVSS. Specifically, we generate pixel-wise pseudo-labels using SAM3, conditioned on scene-level class names and object-level class names with bounding boxes. Combined with ground-truth semantic masks, these pseudo-labels enable joint training across all three annotation granularities through LoRA-based fine-tuning of SAM3. Our unified training spans 20 RS datasets, including 3 scene classification, 4 object detection, and 13 semantic segmentation datasets, with 1,000 images from the 7 classification and detection datasets manually annotated for pixel-level evaluation. HS-OVSeg substantially outperforms existing RS-OVSS models across both the 20 training-source and 5 held-out datasets, demonstrating strong multi-granularity segmentation capabilities under heterogeneous supervision. The code will be publicly available upon publication.

</details>

Abstract (英文): The field of semi-supervised learning (SSL) has seen substantial progress, driven by ongoing refinements within the pseudo-label SSL paradigm. The main difficulty lies in reducing the adverse impact of confirmation bias. The predominant approach is to apply a confidence threshold during training to discard low-confidence samples. Nevertheless, while this mitigates the bias, the discarded samples are mostly situated near class boundaries, and consequently, this strategy also loses valuable boundary information. To address this problem, we propose Boundary Refinement and Correction (BRC), an add-on for confidence-thresholded SSL that rescues useful discarded samples without changing how the base SSL method selects pseudo-labels. BRC keeps the original confidence-based selection rule and operates only on the rejected set. It first identifies discarded samples that may still be useful for training, then replaces their original pseudo-labels with corrected soft pseudo-labels and returns them to the unsupervised loss. Extensive experiments on CIFAR-100, STL-10, and TissueMNIST demonstrate the flexibility and broad applicability of BRC across diverse datasets and base SSL methods, reducing error rates by up to 4.97 percentage points. On boundary-near samples, BRC further improves Top-1 accuracy by 12.80 percentage points, directly supporting its ability to refine the model's decision boundary. An exploratory extension to semantic segmentation also yields consistent gains of 1.03-2.39 mIoU, indicating that BRC generalizes beyond image classification.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Geospatial reasoning segmentation aims to localize geographic targets from language requiring semantic inference beyond direct visual perception. However, existing benchmarks often operationalize reasoning as category omission while overlooking attribute mixing, which neglects explicit-category reasoning, introduces perceptual shortcuts, and fails to precisely evaluate reasoning capability. To diagnose and address these limitations, we first reformulate geospatial reasoning segmentation along category expression (explicit vs. implicit) and attribute nature (perceptual vs. inferential), and audit 90,708 queries across existing benchmarks to reveal that pure explicit-inferential queries account for only 0.61% whereas 31.98% mix both attribute types. We then develop a three-stage pipeline that harnesses OpenStreetMap attributes while mitigating geometric and semantic noise to construct RSReasoner, a large-scale dataset of 106,610 images with 213,220 reasoning queries, and RSReasoner-Bench, a diagnostic benchmark of 75,400 queries across four canonical types and controlled variants. Extensive experiments demonstrate that RSReasoner-Bench precisely evaluates model capability across multiple dimensions while exposing shortcut behaviors, and fine-tuning on RSReasoner boosts both reasoning and cross-type generalization. Our findings offer key insights for future geospatial reasoning research and benchmark design. Datasets and code will be released at https://github.com/Ricardo-XZ/RSReasoner.

</details>

Abstract (英文): In the realm of generative AI, models such as GANs, MidJourney, DALL-E, and others have been continuously evolving to generate increasingly lifelike images. This rapid advancement has created an urgent need for robust methods to distinguish between real and AI-generated images. This paper proposes a novel approach for the detection of synthetic images by employing deep learning techniques for feature analysis. The proposed architecture classifies images by identifying variations that generative models struggle to replicate, particularly fine-grained textures and complex object features. Deformations in complexes are examined with the help of the CBAM architecture along with ResNet50. We have performed a selective patch-based analysis using Discrete Cosine Transform (DCT) scoring, extracting the regions with the most and least complexity for further processing with an SRM Module and ResNet50 module. Experimental results show that our approach achieves high accuracy in classifying real-world images from synthetic ones, contributing to the development of more reliable detection frameworks.

Abstract (英文): View-graph calibration jointly estimates camera intrinsics from multiple pairwise fundamental matrices. The correct intrinsics transform each fundamental matrix into an essential matrix, casting the calibration problem as minimizing the distance to the essential manifold. We study how to represent this objective for nonlinear least squares. The conventional singular-value formulation collapses two independent essential-matrix constraints into one scalar residual. Its per-edge Gauss-Newton model is therefore rank deficient, which leads to poor convergence. We instead derive an isometric reduction to 2x2 matrices, where each branch of the nonlinear essential-matrix constraint becomes a linear subspace. This reduction yields a smooth two-dimensional residual whose squared norm is exactly the original manifold distance, whose Gauss-Newton approximation is generically full rank, and whose evaluation requires no per-edge singular value decomposition. Experiments show that the new residual retains the estimation accuracy of the geometric objective while converging in fewer, computationally cheaper iterations. Combined with robust initialization and optimization, it achieves state-of-the-art view-graph calibration accuracy at a runtime comparable to inexpensive algebraic objectives.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Vision foundation backbones are often treated as drop-in upgrades for improving region proposals in open-vocabulary panoptic segmentation (OVPS). Yet, improvements in backbones often fail to translate well into downstream cross-domain performance. Using ODISE, a diffusion-based OVPS model with a CLIP-based recognition branch, as a testbed, we replace its diffusion visual extractor with DINOv2, ConvNeXt, or DINOv3, and find that backbone upgrades can introduce a grouping-recognition mismatch: an alignment gap between dense-region features and CLIP text embeddings. Across source and near-domain, the newer DINOv3 variant improves source-domain performance but collapses on several cross-domain targets, whereas other baselines, interestingly, show the opposite. Stage-wise diagnostics, linear probes, and region-text alignment analyses indicate that DINOv3 embeddings retain semantic information but are geometrically incompatible with CLIP. We test adaptations, including text-side adapters, conformal mask retention (CMR), and a lean 2.7M-parameter visual-side LoRA, and find that text-side adaptation is more sensitive to vocabulary overlap. CMR provides only limited and target-specific gains, indicating that retention alone cannot resolve the full cross-domain failure. To understand why ADE20K resists improvement across all interventions, we evaluate OVSeg, CAT-Seg, and MaskCLIP. This separates two failure axes: a recoverable visual-to-CLIP interface gap that visual-side LoRA largely repairs, and a vocabulary-boundary limitation shared by the evaluated CLIP-based recognition systems that requires different remedies.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Aligning inertial measurement unit (IMU) signals with text enables zero-shot activity recognition across wearable devices. A worn device's signal depends on its setup, the combination of body site and mounting. Leading IMU-language models make their encoders invariant to the mounting, which also discards the device's orientation relative to the body, such as the direction of gravity that separates sitting, standing and lying. We introduce SetCon, which replaces full invariance with selective invariance. It estimates each device's mounting on its known body site from a few unlabeled windows and conditions its encoder on this estimate. Because the mounting stays nearly fixed while the device is worn, the moving body serves as a calibration target. Both calibration and representation learning are supervised by 3D human motion, since one movement can be simulated under many known sensor setups. At test time, a class is represented by its name or by an anchor, which averages the text embeddings of the name and of activity descriptions and needs no labeled window. A language model built on the frozen encoder also answers how and in what order a movement is performed. Trained only on IMU simulated from the same Nymeria motion capture as AnyMo, SetCon reaches 56.6% mean zero-shot accuracy on 13 real datasets with class names and 65.3% with anchors, against 41.8% and 46.7% for AnyMo.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Text-to-motion generation has made great progress in capturing what action to perform, yet it remains limited in controlling how that action is executed. For example, when only the height word of a kick request changes, none of the tested state-of-the-art methods reaches all three of knee, waist and head height. The other words of a caption usually imply an attribute such as height, so neither the training loss nor the standard metrics require a model to use the word that states it. A model has to use this word only when it alone separates two otherwise identical captions. We introduce MinPair, which creates such caption pairs from real motion clips. It starts from rule-based motion descriptors that measure the 3D body motion of each clip. From these descriptors a language model writes MinPair captions, timed body-part captions that say how each body part moves. The same descriptors show in which attributes two clips of the same action differ. A measured minimal pair joins the true caption of one clip with an altered copy in which only one attribute takes the value measured in the other clip. A contrastive pair loss then requires a diffusion denoiser to reconstruct the clip more accurately from its true caption than from the altered copy. Training needs no synthetic motion and inference stays unchanged. Control is tested on 22 target tasks and on caption edits that each change one attribute. MinPair captions alone help motions reach their targets and improve the standard metrics M2M, R@3 and FID, yet a model trained on them still ignores most single-word edits. The pair loss raises the share of followed template edits from 11% to 60%, while the standard metrics do not improve further. With body-part captions, MinPair reaches more targets than state-of-the-art methods that were trained on up to 99 times more motion data.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Both single-modal and multi-modal anomaly detection aim to identify deviations from normal patterns, providing a common foundation for unified detection. However, existing methods rely on modality-specific modeling and scoring, making it difficult to obtain comparable anomaly evidence with limited normal references. To that end, we propose UnifiedAD, which unifies single-modal and multi-modal anomaly detection through anomaly evidence calibration, requiring only one normal reference per target category and no gradient updates. Specifically, we first derive neuron-level discriminative priors from normal and anomalous samples in an independent auxiliary dataset and combine them with neuron response consistency under normality-preserving transformations of the target reference to select reliable neurons, termed anomaly neurons, and identify anomaly directions. Next, we construct a low-rank normal subspace for each layer and modality from the selected neurons' spatial responses to the reference and quantify query feature deviations from normal structure using projection residuals. Finally, we separately calibrate projection residuals and anomaly-direction responses using the response distributions obtained from the normal reference and its transformations, converting them into comparable anomaly evidence for aggregation across layers and modalities. Experiments on multiple anomaly detection benchmarks demonstrate that UnifiedAD achieves strong performance in both image-level anomaly detection and pixel-level anomaly localization.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Segmenting a 3D object into semantically meaningful parts requires joint reasoning about geometric structure and semantic context. Pretrained 3D experts provide spatially grounded region proposals, while 2D experts provide semantic cues for identifying object parts. The challenge is to integrate these complementary capabilities into a framework that can both produce meaningful part segmentations and progressively refine them. We present PartAgent, a training-free agentic framework for 3D part segmentation built upon two complementary modules: (i) Structural-Semantic Expert Integration combines regions predicted by the 3D expert with multiview semantic evidence from the 2D expert through image-to-surface correspondences, constructing compositional part partitions at multiple granularities. (ii) Observation-Driven Closed-Loop Partition Refinement observes the current partition and its supporting evidence to select targeted inspection, evidence repair, composition, or reselection actions. It verifies proposed changes and uses the updated state and action outcomes to guide subsequent decisions. On PartObjaverse-Tiny-WT, PartAgent achieves 68.41 mIoU, outperforming the strongest competing method by 8.53 points. Across 3DCoMPaT200, PartNeXt, PartNetE, and PartObjaverse-Tiny, PartAgent achieves the highest mIoU on each dataset, averaging 69.36 across the four datasets, 7.28 points above the highest mean among competing methods.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Sol-Attn (Sparsifying online attention) combines sparse exact computation with low-cost representative approximation to accelerate training-free inference in diffusion video models. However, intra-block attention can focus on only a few tokens. While preserving these key interactions exactly, the block-level decision incurs the cost of evaluating many low-response members. We propose Sol$^{3}$, a training-free hierarchical sparse attention method that further refines how exact computations are allocated. First, we introduce Gaussian-guided hierarchical routing, which determines the scope of exact computation step by step, from blocks to tokens, using statistical thresholds at each level. Only a subset of members within Mixed blocks that require refinement is selected for exact computation, while representative terms approximate the remaining members. We further design heterogeneous routing statistics that combine block-center correlations with variations in Key and Value within blocks to identify blocks that are difficult to approximate. Among these Mixed blocks, we uniformly compare raw token relevance to select members requiring exact computation. Experiments on video generation, audio-video co-generation, and world simulation tasks demonstrate that Sol$^{3}$ achieves 86% attention sparsity and delivers a $3.07\times$ attention speedup while preserving quality. Our code is available at https://github.com/FofGofx/sol3.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Multi-vehicle collaborative perception can use observations from different perspectives to alleviate the problems of occlusion and limited sensing range. However, missing echoes and false echoes caused by bad weather will reduce the quality of single-vehicle features and affect collaborative detection. LiDAR provides richer geometric information, and 4D Radar has strong weather adaptability. The complementarity of the two provides the possibility to improve perception under degraded observations. However, how to adjust cross-modal repair according to the current observation conditions and how to reasonably integrate multi-vehicle features whose reliability still differs after repair are still problems that need to be solved. To this end, we propose CoReQ, a condition-aware repair and quality-guided collaborative perception framework. For the former, the condition-guided bidirectional cross-modal repair module (CBCR) extracts vehicle-level observation conditions from point cloud occupancy, density and feature statistics, and adjusts the bidirectional correction of LiDAR and Radar features accordingly. For the latter, we combine local reliability assessment with collaborative weighting: first, the task-aware quality estimation module (TaQE) uses the classification and positioning information of the EMA teacher to construct a supervision target and predict the spatial quality of the repaired features; then, the condition and quality guided fusion module (CoQF) matches the quality information with the content, and the observation conditions of both parties are jointly used for multi-vehicle feature weighted fusion. Experiments on V2X-R and V2X-Radar-C show that the framework is competitive under a variety of observation conditions, achieving significant gains in AP@0.7 on V2X-R under snowy conditions, and achieving the highest AP among the compared V2X-Radar-C methods.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Virtual staining synthesizes special-stain images from routine hematoxylin and eosin (H&E), extending the information available from an existing digital slide. However, a fixed inference rule can leave available real target-stain references unused, limiting query-specific correction of stain appearance at deployment. Test-time adaptation (TTA) provides a mechanism for this reference-guided, query-specific refinement without retraining the generator. We therefore propose FewRef-5, a reference-assisted TTA method for virtual staining built on StainPromptInversion. We extract tissue-masked optical-density means and standard deviations from five real Masson's trichrome (MAS) training patches and average them into a fixed stain target. A scheduled statistic-matching loss introduces this target into per-query visual-prompt optimization alongside our edge/Gram consistency penalties and the inherited reconstruction objectives. The target remains unchanged across queries, avoiding recursive dependence on earlier generated outputs while the pretrained generator stays fixed. On recorded H&E-to-MAS outputs for 1,989 queries, FewRef-5 reduces target-set FID from 183.201 to 166.904 and increases source-referenced SSIM from 0.92787 to 0.94996 relative to our Edge+Gram variant. Source PSNR and MS-SSIM decrease, showing an endpoint trade-off rather than uniformly better reconstruction.

</details>

Abstract (英文): We present VenusOCR2.0, a compact 1B-parameter vision-language recognizer designed for both high-accuracy and high-throughput document parsing across text, tables, and formulas. To continuously improve recognition quality without self-training collapse, we build a bounded recursive self-improvement (RSI) data engine where the model acts strictly as a weakness probe, while external multi-expert medoid consensus, VLM visual verification, and factorized synthesis supply reliable supervision across pretraining, curated SFT, and RLVR. VenusOCR2.0 couples a dynamic-resolution SigLIP2 encoder and 2x2 spatial token merging with a Qwen3-0.6B decoder, adopting compact atomic OTSL tokens to shorten table sequences and reduce structural errors. To further eliminate the autoregressive decoding bottleneck, we introduce PTPv2, a lightweight ($\sim$67M) decoupled parallel draft module that combines frozen multi-layer verifier conditioning, a standalone learned mask, and block-causal all-position next-token prediction. On OmniDocBenchv1.6, VenusOCR2.0 achieves an overall score of 96.45 and a state-of-the-art 98.57 formula CDM, substantially outperforming massive general-purpose VLMs such as Gemini-3-Pro (92.91) and Qwen3-VL-235B (89.78). Deployed in vLLM on a single H20 GPU, PTPv2 delivers a 2.96x single-request speedup and up to 2.36x higher batched throughput while guaranteeing lossless, token-for-token equivalence with standard autoregressive decoding.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Given a reference asset and an instruction such as "remove one armrest," a retriever must find variants that make the edit and preserve the other parts. This search is well established for images but remains underexplored for individual 3D assets. To study this problem, we introduce ShapeQuest and ShapeQuester, a new benchmark and model for edit-conditioned 3D asset retrieval. ShapeQuest's open-source pipeline edits textured meshes through part additions, removals, and material and colour changes. It controls edit granularity across five levels, defined by the fraction of the object's surface area each edit affects. It constructs multiple valid targets when an instruction leaves the choice of part open. A vision-language model judges each edit from views chosen to expose the edited parts, and human raters audit its judgments. ShapeQuest has 87,824 queries in 50 categories and a 50,919-shape gallery with competing edits. To distinguish these variants, ShapeQuester learns retrieval alongside reconstruction of the edited point cloud, with part masks supervising changed and preserved regions. With a frozen pretrained point encoder, ShapeQuester reaches 50.4 operation-macro mAP@10 on the test split, against 47.2 for the fusion baseline with the same inputs and 48.6 for the strongest baseline. Reconstruction adds 6.5 points on the validation split when the point encoder is trained from scratch, including 3.3 from part masks.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Recent advances in vision-language foundation models have enabled open-vocabulary semantic segmentation (OVSS) without task-specific training, offering new opportunities for Earth Observation (EO) applications. However, their capabilities for cloud detection remain insufficiently explored. In this work, we evaluate twelve OVSS approaches across six publicly available EO datasets in a zero-shot setting, i.e., without fine-tuning or adaptation to the target data. We investigate both binary cloud detection and multiclass segmentation, distinguishing between thick clouds, thin clouds, cloud shadows, and background. Our results show differences in performance across models and datasets, with SegEarth-OV3 achieving the highest binary cloud detection performance on four of the six datasets. While several approaches demonstrate promising performance in binary cloud detection, multiclass segmentation remains more challenging, particularly when distinguishing between fine-grained cloud categories. Furthermore, our controlled prompt sensitivity analysis reveals model-dependent responses to alternative textual descriptions, with appearance-oriented prompts benefiting certain approaches while degrading the performance of others. Overall, our findings demonstrate both the potential and current limitations of zero-shot OVSS for cloud detection, establishing a comparative benchmark for future research on generalisable cloud segmentation. Code will be available at: https://github.com/josesosajs/zero-shot-cloudovss.

</details>

Abstract (英文): 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) enables high-quality novel-view rendering, but its large scene representations are costly to transmit. Pruning and compression reduce file sizes, yet progressive delivery also requires usable reconstructions before the full scene arrives. Existing progressive representations provide partial scenes, but level-based updates are limited by predefined coding boundaries. This paper presents a progressive 3DGS codec that balances attribute fidelity with the image benefit of making Gaussians available earlier. Its single compact stream supports partial 3D reconstruction and adds newly decoded Gaussians as more bytes arrive. The stream supports fine-grained updates at arbitrary received-byte budgets, without separate encodings or later attribute refinement. At matched complete-file sizes and received bytes, completion-aware allocation improves 1/8 PSNR over attribute-only allocation by 0.48-1.43 dB. The proposed codec compresses original 3DGS representations by 62-88 times while retaining near-reference Full PSNR. Under the evaluated 50-Mbps delivery model, partial reconstruction becomes available in approximately 0.2 s.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Real-world single-image dehazing suffers from severe domain shifts, as paired real data is scarce and models trained on synthetic haze generalize poorly to real-world haze. While pre-trained generative editing models offer powerful image priors to bridge this generalization gap, applying them to image dehazing poses two major challenges: standard generative models prioritize distribution alignment over strict pixel-level fidelity, and operating in compressed latent spaces inherently discards fine-grained structural details. In this paper, we propose a novel two-stage framework that adapts the generative prior of a few-step flow-matching backbone to real-world dehazing. In the first stage, we fine-tune the latent backbone using Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA), supervised not only on a flow-matching objective, but also on pixel-level supervision, thus enabling direct hazy-to-clear translation. In the second stage, we introduce structure-preserving skip connections directly to the VAE decoder, bypassing latent compression artifacts to recover precise spatial edges. Experiments on four realworld benchmarks demonstrate that our approach significantly outperforms existing dehazing methods in real conditions.

</details>

Abstract (英文): We were surprised to find that leading end-to-end driving models across major paradigms, despite scoring close to human drivers on NAVSIM, cannot reliably make a simple 90-degree turn in photorealistic closed-loop simulators. To investigate why, we build LaWanle, a benchmark of turns drawn from three such simulators. Driving each turn from several starts, we find that the same model passes it from one start and fails it from another, and that the two drives look alike until they enter the junction. Rotating the car by a few degrees at the junction entry can fail a turn it otherwise passes. Conversely, a slight heading correction to the plans can rescue about half of the failed turns we tested. Such errors arise when a model drives by itself, so we post-train an open-source lane-keeping model with closed-loop DAgger on its lateral path. Training 5% of its parameters is enough to raise its turn pass rate from 19% to 76%. The gain transfers to the two other benchmarks, where the policy also scores among the leading models. Further fine-tuning on recovery data repairs the deviations it is trained on but, against a control trained equally long, improves turns on none of the three benchmarks, and every variant stays far below a human driver.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Modern video object segmentation (VOS) relies on a two-stage inference paradigm: target initialization and memory-based propagation. However, existing adversarial attacks on VOS often overlook this structure. To bridge this gap, we propose AUV, an adversarial framework that Aligns with the Universal two-stage paradigm of VOS models. Specifically, AUV optimizes a universal adversarial perturbation (UAP) through decoupled training strategies tailored for both initialization and propagation stages. By disrupting the shared memory space, it achieves prompt-agnostic attacks across different modalities, including masks, points, boxes, and language. Extensive experiments show that AUV establishes state-of-the-art VOS attack performance, such as degrading mask-based SAM3 to only 8.4 J&F on DAVIS17-val (compared to 55.4 for the previous SOTA under the same training setup). It also exhibits superior temporal dynamics with the fastest onset, the most enduring persistence, and the support of any-time mid-video attacks. The AUV optimization framework is applicable to different VOS models, revealing vulnerabilities in real-world streaming VOS. Code is available at https://github.com/FudanCVL/AUV.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Few-shot adaptation of vision-language models has produced increasingly elaborate methods built on CLIP and on one shared principle: the zero-shot text classifier is the anchor, and labeled images only correct it. We argue that this principle reflects CLIP's visual features rather than a property of the task. We provide a simple Bayesian interpretation, in which a class name is worth exactly kappa = sigma^2 / tau^2 labeled images, the ratio between the visual spread of a class and the misalignment of its name. The Bayes classifier of this model counts text as kappa more samples. With modern frozen encoders, few-shot adaptation then reduces to a well-regularised convex probe. Specifically, we introduce TASP, a simple convex logistic regression on frozen features that incorporates class-name embeddings as weighted training examples and as a regularization prior. On the standard 11-dataset benchmark, TASP with frozen PE-Core-L features achieves 87.1% accuracy at 1 shot and 91.4% at 16 shots, outperforming state-of-the-art few-shot vision-language adaptation methods by 4.6 to 11.0 percentage points. While stronger representations explain much of this improvement, controlled comparisons on matched features show that existing frozen-encoder adaptation methods do not materially improve on this probe. We further find that the weight assigned to text matters more than how it is incorporated, and that combining self-supervised visual features with vision-language representations is most beneficial for weaker visual encoders. Together, these findings suggest that progress in few-shot vision-language adaptation depends more on representation quality and the appropriate use of text supervision than on increasingly complex adaptation mechanisms.

</details>

Abstract (英文): High-resolution video matting requires accurate recovery of continuous alpha values, including fine hair, soft boundaries, and motion blur, yet dense alpha supervision remains scarce. Pretrained text-to-video diffusion transformers (DiTs) offer rich spatio-temporal priors, whereas existing generative video matting approaches typically route alpha predictions through RGB-trained VAE latents, which can attenuate fine details and introduce additional decoding overhead. We present TokenMatte, which adapts a pretrained video DiT into a single-pass, auxiliary-free RGB-to-alpha predictor with a pixel-native output interface. At its core is a single shared linear readout that maps each spatio-temporal token directly to its corresponding ($t \times p \times p$) block of alpha pixels, predicting spatial details and temporal variations within the same local video block. Tiling these blocks produces the full-resolution alpha sequence without a convolutional decoder or learned upsampling. The predicted alpha is supervised directly in pixel space, while the ground-truth alpha never enters the VAE, eliminating target-side alpha encoding and output-side VAE decoding. Leveraging the pretrained video prior, TokenMatte is trained using only existing public matting datasets, without additional data curation or auxiliary inputs at inference. Compared with a matched latent-output baseline, our pixel-native interface improves matting accuracy while achieving a 1.51$\times$ inference speedup. TokenMatte establishes a new auxiliary-free state of the art on VideoMatte, YouTubeMatte, and V-HIM60.

</details>

Abstract (英文): In object pose estimation methods, predicted poses are expressed with respect to a canonical frame, defined by a reference model of that object. In learning-based methods, an arbitrarily chosen modeling convention defines this canonical frame, which is then used to generate the ground-truth pose-annotations for training. Object pose estimation methods are therefore bound to such arbitrary conventions, restricting generalization. In this work, we propose that such a canonical frame can instead be derived for any object, and present RestPose, an approach towards open object pose estimation that does not require reference models or canonical frame conventions during training. From a single input image, we generate an object mesh and canonicalize it through a geometric and physical analysis of the object, then use foundation model-based dense matching to estimate the pose. We choose category-level benchmarks for our experiments, which contain instances across various object categories and enable us to measure the effectiveness of our canonicalization. Across HouseCat6D, NOCS, and Omni6DPose datasets, we improve on the supervised state-of-the-art methods by 18.3 points in absolute metrics and 13.8 points in scale-agnostic metrics, without requiring task-specific training. Code will be published.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Vision-Language-Action models (VLAs) enable simultaneous processing of egocentric video and textual target descriptions to produce action commands for embodied tracking platforms. However, these platforms rarely move exactly as commanded, in part due to unexpected environmental disturbances such as wind, unleveled terrain, and road bumps. Here we study how such models handle uncommanded egomotion. First, we explore performance across diverse disturbances by benchmarking three tracking VLAs on three datasets. We find that models differ in which environmental disturbances hurt them most, but all are sensitive to sudden target loss. Tracing egomotion from pixels to emitted actions, we identify two gaps. In representation, motion is reliably recovered through explicit relations between frames, but aggressive compression of past frames weakens small-motion readouts. In action, large rotations do reach the decision state, yet the decoded direction carries only a few percent of a mirrored rotation's effect on the turn. Targeting both gaps, we fine-tune one model, Uni-NaVid, on data amounting to just 1.7% of its original training set in a ladder of analysis-motivated variants. These gain 5-22 points after target-loss without degrading clean success; after a smooth loss, the best one improves success by 72% (relative), recovering 76% of its gap to clean. We also show that targeted fine-tuning improves recovery partly via a text-mediated action dependency, without new architecture, large-scale data collection or training from scratch.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Open-vocabulary 3D segmentation methods rely on 2D foundation models applied to registered images captured alongside the 3D point clouds. However, such images may not be available in many cases, while direct 3D point cloud segmentation methods require semantic class supervision and are challenging to deploy in a zero-shot manner. To address these issues, we present SplatSeg3D, which segments a bare colored point cloud in an open-vocabulary manner without requiring the accompanying images, camera poses, class supervision, or instance mask proposals. To achieve this, we first surfelize the input point cloud and obtain oriented 2D Gaussians. We then render views of the scene by splatting, with a view selection strategy that maximizes scene coverage with as few views as possible. Next, we apply text-prompted segmentation on the rendered views, giving each 3D point a set of multi-view semantic cues, fused to produce a 3D segmentation of the scene. We evaluate SplatSeg3D on semantic segmentation and novel-class instance segmentation on 4 standard benchmarks: Scannet, Scannet++, Replica, and S3DIS. Our pipeline not only performs better or on-par with the state-of-the-art, but we also achieve these results efficiently across runtime and compute requirements. Code will be published.

</details>

Abstract (英文): 6D pose estimation of rigid objects is a fundamental and challenging task in computer vision. Recent dense semantic keypoint-based methods employ binary encoding for efficient global keypoint localization, delivering strong performance in object pose estimation. Nevertheless, the discontinuous nature of binary encoding hinders effective training at high resolutions, while its inherent discrete and quantization errors lead to significant localization uncertainty. We propose GHLAPose, a global-to-local framework that synergizes hybrid Gray-binary (HGB) encoding with local-aware refinement (LAR). Specifically, HGB encoding first alleviates discontinuity to achieve accurate initial global keypoint localization. To further address intrinsic encoding errors, the LAR module estimates uncertainty and refines keypoint localization. This synergy enables a precise global-to-local strategy by aggregating global and local features. Extensive experiments on LM, LM-O, YCB-V, and TUD-L datasets demonstrate that GHLAPose surpasses state-of-the-art methods, particularly on fine-grained metrics. On LM-O, it outperforms the second-best method by 1.4% and 1.9% on ADD(-S) 0.02d and 0.05d respectively, and on YCB-V by 1% on ADD(-S) 0.1d. The code will be available at GHLAPose.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Feedforward 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) pipelines reconstruct a scene from images in a single forward pass, representing it as real-time-renderable 3D Gaussians. However, the resulting representations contain far more Gaussians than are needed to render the scene faithfully, especially when there are many input views. This drastically increases memory and storage requirements. Existing pruning methods either require costly re-optimization, which defeats the purpose of a single forward pass, or rely on simple strategies that do not exploit all the available information. We present AnyPrune, a transformer-based, optimization-free pruning module designed to work on top of any feedforward 3DGS pipeline. To remove unnecessary Gaussians, AnyPrune learns and exploits pipeline-specific redundancy patterns through a short single-GPU training on the frozen target feedforward pipeline. AnyPrune achieves better rendering quality than existing optimization-free pruning methods in most settings and generalizes to unseen datasets, as shown through extensive experiments across multiple feedforward pipelines, datasets, and input view counts. It prunes a 1.6M-Gaussian field in about $5\,\mathrm{s}$ on a consumer GPU, faster than every method that merges Gaussians or re-optimizes the field. It can also be combined with the pruning built into feedforward pipelines, pruning further at moderate ratios while preserving or improving rendering quality. Finally, it can even suggest a scene-specific pruning ratio that retains most of the quality.

</details>
