Abstract (英文): Fine-tuning a generator on a complete high-resolution canvas requires long image sequences and substantial computation, yet local regions can still exhibit unnatural repetitive stripes when training resources are limited. We introduce Qwen-Image-HD, which adapts Qwen-Image 2.0 to high-resolution synthesis through local training and blockwise autoregressive generation. Each prediction covers a target tile at 1K scale, conditioned on high-resolution upper and left neighbors and a compressed historical thumbnail. These conditions provide adjacent fine-scale structures and an overview of the scene generated so far. To integrate these complementary cues, we map both forms of history and the target to a shared canvas coordinate system to express their spatial relationships and scale differences. On Qwen-Image-Bench at 4K, Qwen-Image-HD achieves stronger overall evaluation performance than direct 4K generation with FLUX-3-Image-Klein. Compared with full-image 4K supervised fine-tuning of the same backbone, it achieves better generation quality with lower training compute and higher inference efficiency, while exhibiting fewer unnatural repetitive stripes in original-resolution comparisons. We further introduce a Wavefront-AutoRegressive inference schedule that generates tiles on the same anti-diagonal concurrently across GPUs to reduce single-image latency.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Lithography is a critical step in the manufacturing of chips that works by projecting light through a patterned mask and subsequently focusing it onto a photosensitive layer on top of a wafer. The task of finding a mask that prints a desired pattern on the wafer is called inverse lithography technology (ILT). The design of the mask is complicated by the fact that it must satisfy a set of constraints in order to be manufacturable. In this paper we provide a mathematical characterization of these constraints. Next, we develop a scoring function that allows us to measure whether a given mask satisfies the manufacturing constraints and if not, how badly the constraints are violated. Since our scoring function is (sub-)differentiable it can be used as the regularizer in a gradient-based inverse problem solver. We show that the resulting MorphILT algorithm that we developed can generate manufacturable masks that have nearly the same print quality as the (unmanufacturable) masks obtained from unconstrained inverse lithography.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Sparse world models for 4D semantic occupancy forecasting represent scenes with a small set of evolving queries to efficiently predict future geometry and semantics for autonomous driving. However, existing sparse world models face a bottleneck in using historical temporal evidence for long-horizon forecasting. They rely on scene states to carry historical information implicitly, allowing errors to propagate and accumulate across successive forecasting steps and causing geometric and semantic deviations. To address this limitation, we present MemAnchorWorld, a sparse occupancy world model that explicitly retrieves relevant historical geometric and semantic evidence for 4D semantic occupancy forecasting. Inspired by the von Restorff effect, we introduce a forecast-time-conditioned hierarchical dual-path memory-anchor retrieval mechanism. Guided by the effective age and semantic compatibility of historical information, it combines query-level candidate selection with point-level memory-anchor retrieval to provide historical evidence for sparse points at different forecast times. To better use memory anchors and existing features, a point-level residual adapter jointly gated by the task and forecast time separately controls residual corrections to semantics, occupancy confidence, and spatial positions. On Occ3D-nuScenes, MemAnchorWorld achieves an average mIoU of 13.55% and an average IoU of 22.84% across the 1-3 s forecasting horizons, attaining state-of-the-art performance on both metrics.Project page: https://memanchorworld.github.io/

</details>

Abstract (英文): Instance-level building damage assessment (IBDA) from bi-temporal remote sensing imagery remains challenging, as the same damage grade may exhibit diverse visual manifestations across individual buildings. Natural language offers explicit descriptions of physical damage, providing complementary semantic cues beyond discrete category labels. However, existing vision--language approaches have not fully addressed how damage semantics should adapt to local bi-temporal evidence and subsequently serve as instance-specific references for severity discrimination. To bridge this gap, we propose LAMIA-IBDA, a unified vision--language framework that progressively grounds shared damage semantics in local observations and individual buildings. Specifically, Adaptive Text-Visual Alignment (ATVA) enables evidence-adaptive semantic interaction by jointly contextualizing multiple damage descriptions and bi-temporal visual features under reliability-aware attention supervision. Dynamic Instance-Prototype Grounding (DIPG) further associates these contextualized semantics with individual building regions to construct instance-adaptive prototypes for fine-grained damage classification. Instance-level description preferences additionally supervise fusion-stage attention, linking semantic adaptation with instance discrimination. Extensive experiments on xBD and BRIGHT demonstrate state-of-the-art performance across optical--optical and optical--SAR settings. The source code is publicly available https://github.com/Cutensil/LAMIA-IBDA.

</details>

Abstract (英文): As generative models tackle larger and more complex datasets, learned codecs enable efficient generation through compact latent representations, but may lose details and cannot guarantee exact preservation of given conditions. Generating directly in the original data space avoids errors introduced by the codec. Recent advances in image generation demonstrate that pixel diffusion can produce high-quality samples at practical computational costs. Inspired by these advances, we conduct a preliminary study of pixel diffusion, demonstrating its potential for adaptation to motion generation. Building on these findings, we present RawMo, a framework that brings text-to-motion generation to raw motion space. Our Motion Diffusion Transformer (MiT) models local skeletal structures and whole-body dynamics, while refining motion across frames to improve temporal consistency. Working directly in raw motion space further allows our high-noise text-motion alignment loss (HiNCE) to improve text consistency without intermediate motion decoding. RawMo supports multiple motion-conditioning tasks with a single conditional model while preserving given motion conditions exactly. Experiments on HumanML3D and KIT-ML show the best text alignment among compared methods and competitive motion quality, with ablations confirming the benefits of MiT and HiNCE.

</details>

Abstract (英文): 3D perception is shifting from task-specific pipelines to geometric foundation models: monocular depth networks, feed-forward 3D/4D reconstruction models, and vision--language models that predict geometry. However, their progress cannot be measured reliably. Published numbers are not comparable, because every paper samples frames, sets resolutions, and selects test splits differently. They are not robust because the alignment required for every up-to-scale prediction can outweigh the gap between models: fitting depth in the wrong space nearly triples its error, and standard trajectory alignment understates the error of failing methods by up to $2.6\times$. And they rarely cover long recordings, dynamic scenes, or aerial views. We present SpatialArena, an open arena that scores geometric models with the same metrics and rules, with robust and deterministic alignment. SpatialArena spans five tasks, from monocular depth to SLAM. It covers 23 public datasets with 418,929 images across indoor, outdoor, aerial, object-centric, and dynamic scenes, together with Spatial Arena, our own captures with accurate poses and depth. Evaluating 52 models, we find that single-image depth is close to saturation on public benchmarks but not on large, uncontrolled scenes, and that progress now comes from consistency across frames and views, where feed-forward 3D/4D models match or surpass monocular specialists and overtake video depth models. The benchmark, toolbox, and leaderboard will be publicly released.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Image-text alignment serves as a foundation for modern vision-language models (VLMs), yet prevailing training paradigms provide limited supervision for learning how semantic changes correspond across modalities. To address this limitation, we propose a post-training method that learns cross-modal semantic change correspondences through controlled visual and textual variations. Specifically, we introduce a Semantic Difference Alignment (SDA) loss which can align the directions of corresponding visual and textual representation changes. We combine our proposed SDA with pairwise sigmoid matching to jointly learn image-text correspondences and cross-modal semantic changes, encouraging more fine-grained and discriminative visual representations. To support this learning, we construct VarFamily-40K, a dataset of image-text pairs with controlled cross-modal semantic variations. Experiments demonstrate consistent improvements in compositional understanding and downstream classification performance, alongside further gains on multimodal tasks when the adapted visual encoder is integrated into VLMs.

Abstract (英文): Video generative models synthesize realistic scenes, yet their interactions often violate physics. A ball dropped onto sand should displace grains and settle into a crater, whereas a generated video may show it passing through the surface or leaving the sand flat. Post-hoc methods typically score generated videos with a vision-language model (VLM), but such scores offer limited guidance on which object or parameter to modify, and each correction costs another synthesis. Simulation-based pipelines instead run fixed stages before synthesis, so simulation errors reach the video. To address these limitations, we present PhysMend, a training-free framework for verification-guided program refinement. PhysMend describes the target event as an editable physical program of grounded objects, events, and required relations, paired with tunable execution parameters. Our key insight is to verify and repair the event on its simulated rollout and on the depth video that conditions the generator (depth control) rather than on generated videos, enabling quantitative violation measurement, targeted editing, and fast re-verification before synthesis. A hybrid verifier evaluates semantic event consistency, physical plausibility, solver stability, and visibility in the depth control. VLM judgments are confined to event semantics and cannot override failed physical measurements, which limits the impact of VLM hallucinations. Each failed check localizes the program fields eligible for repair, and its measured violation scales a bounded update of their parameters. Only accepted depth control reaches the generator, which runs once per input. Experiments on the commonly used benchmarks Physics-IQ, PhyGenBench, and VBench-2.0 demonstrate substantial gains across different generator backbones; for example, PhysMend raises the overall Physics-IQ score of Wan-VACE from 27.33 to 39.40.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Brain encoding models predict neural responses from audiovisual input, but typically overlook how ongoing brain activity shapes an individual's response. We introduce EEG-World, a recurrent world model that forecasts 10 seconds of EEG band-power dynamics from 6 seconds of preceding EEG and the subsequent audiovisual stimulus. Pretrained on continuous EEG, EEG-World learns shared neural dynamics and adapts to unseen individuals through lightweight subject-specific readouts. Using 2,584 hours of EEG from 78 participants, we show that nonlinear recurrent forecasting surpasses strong history-aware linear baselines at scale. EEG-World achieves a forecasting correlation of 0.261 versus 0.233 for the strongest history-and-video linear model, with a 3.3-second forecasting-horizon advantage. Its learned dynamics transfer to unseen participants, outperforming the linear baseline in 23 of 24 individuals after calibration. While ongoing EEG history accounts for most predictive performance, controlled stimulus analyses reveal an additional video-dependent component. This component also enables evaluation of visual representations, with V-JEPA 2 improving clip-locked prediction by 35% over combined CLIP and DINOv2 features. Our results demonstrate the value of transferable neural dynamics for individual EEG forecasting and highlight the importance of separating state-driven from stimulus-driven predictability.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Independently pretrained vision and language models may learn shared structures of the same underlying world, providing a foundation for combining them to build multimodal capabilities. However, a key challenge remains in aligning cross-modal feature representations while preserving access to visual information before and after language adaptation. To this end, we propose sparse alignment with visual evidence-guided residual adaptation (SAVERA), a framework that combines frozen vision and language models through bidirectional alignment in a shared sparse space and prompt-guided residual fusion. Specifically, we first present shared-space projection, activation reconstruction and encoding (SPARE), which represents original visual, adapted visual, and textual features through shared soft-gated sparse encoding, with branch-specific reconstruction to preserve their respective information. Second, we devise bridge--text embedding and activation matching (BEAM), a bidirectional cross-modal alignment module that aligns adapted visual and textual features at both the sample and neuron levels, establishing fine-grained cross-modal correspondence. Finally, we introduce prompt-conditioned residual integration of vision and adapted features (PRIVA), a prompt-guided residual fusion module that models vision--language interactions through gating and attention and integrates complementary information from original and adapted representations through residual fusion. Extensive experiments show that SAVERA achieves competitive performance on image captioning and visual question answering while reducing object hallucination.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Recovering editable computer-aided design (CAD) models from underwater stereo images requires accurate and complete geometry, yet stereo reconstruction produces displaced points and missing regions. The original views retain cross-view disagreement and ray evidence lost during point-cloud fusion. Aqua2CAD exploits this evidence in two stages: view-conditioned correction and quality filtering establish reliable geometric support, while multi-view observations condition a learned shape prior to infer missing surfaces in the original metric frame. The recovered points support surface fitting and solid assembly with manually revised face groups. On an OceanSim-based synthetic benchmark, Aqua2CAD reduces point-cloud Chamfer distance by 60% relative to the stereo reconstruction. Compared with a matched point-only completion backbone, it improves missing-region recovery at 5,mm from 16.27% to 24.16%. With a shared CAD backend, exterior acceptance increases from 6/19 to 14/19. Abstract (英文): We present DOPER, an RGB pose estimation framework that shares a visual backbone across object detection, segmentation, and keypoint prediction to recover the 6D pose of known objects through Perspective-n-Point. Starting from synthetic training, DOPER adapts to real observations using partial supervision while retaining synthetic keypoint labels. Calibrated multi-view observations provide geometric constraints for keypoint supervision. During inference, optional heatmap refinement fits a rigid pose directly to the predicted keypoint distributions, resolving ambiguous correspondences without depth input or a learned pose refiner. Starting from underspecified CAD models, DOPER progressively refines the object representation and pose estimator. Results on SPEED+ and SHIRT further demonstrate effective adaptation to illumination changes and accurate temporal pose estimation. Across the evaluated single-view and multi-view configurations, DOPER achieves state-of-the-art detection AP results on HANDAL, HOPEv2, HOT3D, and T-LESS while using only RGB and running in real time (35 Hz per object model on one GB300). These results demonstrate the effectiveness of jointly learning detection and pose keypoints, with real observations and geometric constraints.

Abstract (英文): Satellite-based 3D reconstruction is limited by sparse observations and changes across acquisition dates. Existing methods use generative priors to recover missing visual detail, but ghosting, streaking, and blur persist in their Gaussian scenes. These artifacts become particularly apparent in low-elevation renderings. We introduce CityRecast to suppress these artifacts through Gaussian scene repair and supervision calibration. To correct the Gaussian distributions that produce these artifacts, we first use multi-angle warm-up to constrain their spatial extent. This reduces the initial ghosting carried into progressive refinement. Surface-guided replacement then continues to repair the Gaussian distribution while preserving existing appearance. To provide more reliable supervision for this process, observation calibration mitigates interference from multi-temporal observations. Joint refinement further reduces conflicts among generated views. Experiments on DFC2019 and IARPA demonstrate reduced artifacts in low-elevation renderings while maintaining satellite-view fidelity. Compared with Skyfall-GS, CityRecast reduces FID-CLIP by 25.3% on DFC2019 and 51.8% on IARPA. MUSIQ increases by 14.54 and 24.95 points, respectively.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Recovering the continuous world-space motion of both hands from egocentric video is essential for applications such as human-to-robot policy transfer and augmented reality. Achieving this goes beyond reconstructing the hands from each individual frame, as the context that a frame needs is often spread over time, especially around occlusions or when a hand moves out of sight. Recent works have explored how to exploit temporal context, yet they mostly draw it from fixed-length windows. Such windows cannot guarantee that every frame has the context required to reconstruct the hands in world space with accurate poses and metric scale. To address this limitation, we introduce MemHand, a memory-enhanced framework for world-space hand motion reconstruction. MemHand maintains two complementary forms of memory. The Short-Term Event Memory keeps the context of the well-observed hand around each occlusion or out-of-sight event and supplies it to frames with weak or missing visual evidence. In addition, since the metric scale holds over the whole recording, the Long-Term Scale Memory retains the most reliable observations across the video as scale anchors within a bounded state. Across benchmarks featuring severe occlusions and frequent out-of-sight intervals, MemHand achieves state-of-the-art accuracy and generalizes well to unseen domains. Benefiting from the synergy of both memories, it cuts W-MPJPE by 57\% on ARCTIC and 70\% on HOT3D relative to StableHand, delivering continuous and metrically accurate world-space hand poses and trajectories. We will release our code and models at the project page: https://irmvlab.github.io/memhand.github.io

</details>

Abstract (英文): Self-attention's quadratic complexity constitutes a core bottleneck for Vision Transformers (ViTs). Existing efficiency-oriented optimizations, including softmax-based and linear attention (LA) variants, generally compromise model representational capacity, leading to inevitable performance degradation. We propose a novel linear attention which alleviates this efficiency-capability dilemma. For the first time, we embed an Adaptive Channel Compression and Activation Purification (ACCAP) mechanism into the QKV projection of linear attention, which enables the model to retain high-dimensional feature information while performing computations with reduced channels. We further fuse a focused kernel function and a self-gating module into ACCAP to construct a lightweight linear attention module (ACCAP-LA). Our theoretical analysis shows that, compared to standard linear attention, ACCAP-LA reduces the attention module's parameter count by 79.17% and FLOPs by 77.5%. Extensive experiments demonstrate ACCAP-LA's effectiveness: when applied to ViT, it achieves 79.4% Top-1 accuracy on ImageNet-1K with only 5.8M parameters, and more notably, it boasts strong cross-architecture compatibility, boosting EfficientViT-b1 and PVT-T by 3.3% and 3.4% respectively.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Tracking a fast-moving point in 3D is prohibitive when RGB frames are blurred or too far apart to reveal its motion. Event cameras record brightness changes between frames, providing measurements inside these temporal gaps. We introduce TrackAnyMotion, which combines monocular RGB frames and events to track arbitrary points in 2D and 3D, including at times when no RGB frame is available. The motion expected for a static point, given scene geometry and camera motion, provides a reference for identifying independently moving objects. An independent-motion decoder combines geometric motion cues with event guidance and 2D tracks, feeding its predictions back into iterative 2D and 3D refinement. We also introduce HyperEvent, a synthetic RGB--Event dataset with high-frequency 3D trajectories, geometry, and independent-motion labels. At RGB frame timestamps on this benchmark, TrackAnyMotion reduces 3D and 2D endpoint errors by 70\% and 32\%, respectively.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Early identification of cerebral palsy (CP) relies on observational assessments of spontaneous infant movement, such as the General Movement Assessment (GMA) and the Baby Observational Selective Control AppRaisal (BabyOSCAR), which require trained raters and substantial scoring time. Existing automated approaches derive hand-engineered features from two-dimensional keypoints, which describe image-plane motion rather than the three-dimensional behavior of individual joints. We present Baby Biomechanical 3D Body (BB3D), an infant-adapted, biomechanical pipeline that estimates joint-angle trajectories from single-camera video. Against a multi-view markerless motion capture reference in 17 infants, BB3D's median Procrustes-aligned error was 17.5~mm for joint centers and 20.4~mm for marker sites. A spatio-temporal transformer, pretrained on BB3D joint-angle trajectories from 1k+ single-camera recordings, and fine-tuned on 550 videos from 350 infants, predicted each individual BabyOSCAR item with a mean within-site AUC of 0.79 and the BabyOSCAR total with a Spearman correlation of 0.86 on held-out recordings. Without being trained on diagnosis, the predicted total separated infants with CP from those without CP with an AUC of 0.86, exceeding a replicated two-dimensional feature classifier (0.67) but below the expert clinician rater's total (0.97). Predicted scores also carried information about functional ability and body distribution, though less than expert clinician scores. These results show that biomechanically grounded representations of monocular video can support scalable, joint-level assessment of selective motor control in infancy

Abstract (英文): Spectral imaging captures information that ordinary RGB sensors cannot, but dedicated spectral cameras can be expensive and bulky. Recovering dense spectra from RGB images offers an accessible alternative, but distinct spectra can produce the same camera response, generating an ambiguity that even more advanced recovery algorithms cannot always resolve. Therefore, increasing the spectral information captured at acquisition is essential. We propose DualSpec, a stereo acquisition framework that jointly optimizes two external optical filters and a reconstruction network. Each filter is placed in front of a standard RGB camera, and the two are designed to produce six responses that provide complementary information for the network. Experiments across reconstruction architectures and spectral ranges show that DualSpec reduces mean spectral angle error by 36-72% relative to a single RGB camera and by 11-29% relative to stereo pairs selected from commercial filters. Cross-dataset evaluations, including a new benchmark of spectral images captured from displaced viewpoints, show that the method generalizes and remains robust to viewpoint misalignment, while analyses of thin-film approximations support optical feasibility.

</details>

Abstract (英文): A dataset whose diagnostic label can be recovered from non-diagnostic information yields high, reproducible and uninformative model performance. We applied pre-training controls that see only image geometry, global texture, a sub-collection identifier or one covariate to six public mammography datasets, against a stop threshold of AUC 0.60. In KAU-BCMD, geometry alone predicted the BI-RADS class at AUC 1.0000 in a local copy and at a mean of 0.979 in a public Kaggle archive; after resizing, texture alone gave 0.847. CBIS-DDSM exceeded the stop on geometry (pooled mean 0.654 over 20 splits), a verdict statistic chosen post hoc; its official mass and calcification splits did not. M-SYNTH, INbreast and a partial VinDr-Mammo copy passed the few controls run. We release the code and derived tables. There is no quantum component in the inference path.

Abstract (英文): Computed tomography (CT), cone-beam CT (CBCT), and magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) provide complementary representations of the same underlying anatomy, yet substantial differences in image appearance challenge consistent segmentation and deformable registration in adaptive radiotherapy. We investigate whether dense anatomical segmentation supervision can promote cross-modality correspondence without explicit feature alignment. We introduce IMPACT-Seg, which segments 126 structures across CT, CBCT, and MRI using shared network parameters. A collection of 8,290 volumes was assembled from public cohorts through label harmonization, model-assisted pseudo-label completion, iterative refinement, and 2,864 physics-based CBCT simulations with voxel-aligned CT labels. Unpaired images were used to jointly train 2.5D and 3D residual-encoder networks.
  On 139 real CBCTs, IMPACT-Seg 3D achieved a mean Dice of 0.842 versus 0.671 for the strongest comparator. On 497 voxel-aligned CT-simulated-CBCT pairs, the CT-to-CBCT Dice decrease was 0.022 versus 0.153-0.178 for competing methods. On independent pancreatic radiotherapy CBCTs, gastrointestinal Dice was 0.735 versus 0.634 for TotalSegmentator. Prediction agreement reached 0.881 on registered CT-CBCT and 0.790 on CT-MRI pairs. Adding MRI training data reduced CT-MRI encoder feature retrieval error from 36.8 to 3.2 mm. Registration pipelines incorporating IMPACT-Seg features ranked first on two multimodal Learn2Reg benchmarks. The 3D model segmented a whole-body CT in 15 s.
  These findings show that dense anatomical segmentation supervision can support consistent anatomical representations across modalities, with applications to both segmentation and registration. Code, datasets, and pretrained models are available at https://github.com/HEMONCedric/IMPACTSeg.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Reasoning about human-object interaction from RGB cameras is important for gaming, VR environments, and robot learning. Yet 4D reconstruction from a monocular RGB camera is challenging, especially when articulated hands are involved. Prior full-body works neglect hands during reconstruction, causing them to penetrate into, or float from the object. We present HARI4D, a hand-articulation-aware method for reconstructing spatially and temporally consistent 4D human-object interaction from monocular RGB video. We introduce a history-aware object tracker that provides robust and stable tracking. Based on these tracks, we improve the interaction configuration through coarse-to-fine reasoning that follows the order of object manipulation: ReachNet first brings the full body and arms to the object during contact, and TouchNet then finalizes hand articulation according to temporal context and local object geometry. On ARCTIC, unseen in training, our method reduces the object error by 56% and improves hand contact F1 from 0.866 to 0.921 over prior art. Our method recovers accurate full body-object interaction with coherent hand articulations and applies to online videos. Our code and pretrained models will be released.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Few-shot medical anomaly detection (MAD) has largely followed a dataset-specific "one-for-one" paradigm, leaving unified "one-for-many" modeling underexplored. Naively extending existing methods to this setting causes substantial performance degradation, especially at the pixel level. We attribute this to their detection mechanisms: a shared decision boundary becomes unreliable across heterogeneous lesion definitions, whereas a normal-only memory bank cannot adequately cover diverse normal distributions with few references. We propose ProX-MAD, a proxy-based framework that aggregates spatial context into compact, learnable, state-specific proxies for unified few-shot MAD. Its Masked Proxy Infusion Attention (MPIA) module cross-attends proxy tokens to reference patch features and applies proxy-wise masking to retain high-confidence correspondences, mitigating proxy collapse and encouraging diverse proxies that summarize both normal and abnormal references. Across six medical datasets, ProX-MAD exhibits only a marginal performance drop from one-for-one to one-for-many training. Despite being trained only once as a unified model, it also outperforms prior state-of-the-art methods on average even when they use their more favorable one-for-one protocol, demonstrating the practicality of unified multi-dataset MAD.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Recent advances in harness-based recursive self-improvement (RSI) have improved video understanding by evolving executable agent workflows without updating foundation model parameters. However, extending this paradigm to omnimodal understanding remains challenging, as the heterogeneous and interdependent nature of multimodal perception and reasoning creates a complex evolution space, making targeted harness optimization difficult. To address these challenges, we propose OmniHarness-RSI, a modality-aware framework for evolving agent harnesses. Specifically, we factorize the evolution space into three capability dimensions: modality routing and collaboration, modality-aware evidence localization, and modality-specific perception. Based on this factorization, we construct a Factorized-Harness Agent integrating specialized perception experts and audio-video retrieval tools. We further introduce modality-guided harness evolution, which identifies deficiencies through structured modality-capability attribution and refines corresponding harness strategies through interactive code revisions and execution feedback. Validation-guided selection and held-out evaluation assess generalization within and across datasets. Experiments on WorldSense, Daily-Omni, and OmniVideoBench demonstrate test accuracy improvements of 8.86, 8.13, and 6.23 percentage points, respectively, consistently outperforming modality-agnostic RSI. The evolved harnesses also demonstrate cross-dataset transfer without adaptation, while reducing planner calls and viewed frames.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Joint brain tumor segmentation and classification models are computationally intensive, limiting their training on resource-constrained edge platforms and restricting privacy-preserving Federated Learning FL in the Internet of Medical Things IoMT. Existing approaches either perform both tasks on GPU-equipped workstations or support only classification on edge devices. To address this gap, this work proposes a lightweight multitask FL framework for joint segmentation and classification through local edge training. A shared pretrained LiteMedSAM encoder with TinyViT extracts features for a coordinate-attention-based segmentation decoder and a segmentation-guided classification head, eliminating a separate feature extractor. Selective encoder freezing retains 95\% trainable parameters while reducing on-device training costs. The proposed framework is experimentally validated on a laboratory testbed of five resource-constrained edge clients using 1,200 T1-weighted MRI samples. It achieves 94.20\% classification accuracy, 88.53\% DSC, 83.68\% IoU, and 99.87\% ROC-AUC. Its classification accuracy remains within 0.6\% of the GPU-trained baseline of 95.21\% across five FL rounds. The attention and feature refinement modules improve DSC and IoU by 3.82\% and 4.60\%, respectively, with 0.74 GFLOPs of additional computation. On edge hardware, the framework achieves 46.54 s inference latency, 52.8\% CPU utilization, and a peak temperature of 65$^\circ$C without thermal throttling. Its inference latency is observed to be 6.4--10.5 times lower than that of existing frameworks. These results demonstrate the feasibility of privacy-preserving joint brain tumor segmentation and classification on resource-constrained edge platforms.

</details>

Abstract (英文): People can anticipate how an object moves and the effort an interaction may require by watching others. How can embodied agents acquire this understanding from video? Force measurements provide direct sensory supervision, but require specialized equipment and remain limited in quantity. We introduce VidForce, which estimates an interaction trajectory together with a force profile from a third-person RGB video; 2D contact clicks and textual object-action prompts are optional. Our approach makes limited instrumented measurements useful for interpreting human interactions through learned physical prototypes as a prior. The prototypes share learned force levels and temporal profiles across interactions of comparable effort, with visual and semantic cues determining their contribution to each observation. Geometric and vision-language features adapt this prior to the observed interaction, while a joint conditional flow refines an initial tracked trajectory together with the force profile. Paired human and instrumented-gripper recordings provide measured force references for supervising human video, and a separately trained torque model demonstrates that the formulation extends to other sensory quantities. We evaluate trajectory recovery and force estimation through baseline comparisons and controlled ablations on held-out interactions from the Hoi! dataset. On this evaluation subset, VidForce achieves 6.6 cm ATE, peak-force MAE and RMSE of 4.81 N and 5.92 N, respectively, and force-profile RMSE of 5.52 N. On real-world videos, VidForce infers motion and force reliably and consistently despite the variability in human execution. We further deploy VidForce on a Spot robot, where trajectories and force profiles estimated from human demonstrations drive real-world articulated-object manipulation, and report success rates across repeated trials. Project page: https://rafaymhddn.github.io/vidforce

</details>

Abstract (英文): Existing depth-from-focus methods can struggle with weak focus cues, new cameras, or fewer focal slices. We present FocusAnchor, a recurrent model that uses focus-volume lookups to refine metric depth and repeatedly aligns a relative depth prior from a frozen foundation model to the current depth estimate. The aligned prior guides subsequent refinement in a feedback loop, carrying metric scale recovered from textured regions into regions where focus cues are weak while preserving scene structure. To support transfer across cameras, we synthesize training stacks with randomized camera parameters and focus distances. However, stack synthesis blurs existing image noise differently across slices, creating artificial depth cues even on textureless surfaces. We add independent per-slice noise to suppress these cues. We also introduce variable-stack training to improve robustness with fewer focal slices and support more practical mobile capture. Compared with FOSSA ViT-B, FocusAnchor reduces AbsRel by 36% on NYU Depth V2 and by 9% on DDFF-12 after fine-tuning. In zero-shot evaluation, FocusAnchor and its variable-stack variant, FocusAnchor-flex, achieve the two lowest AbsRel values on all five benchmarks. On ZEDD test, FocusAnchor achieves 9% lower AbsRel than the best competing method, FOSSA ViT-B, while FocusAnchor-flex achieves lower AbsRel with 40% fewer focal slices on ZEDD validation.

</details>

Abstract (英文): In text-to-video retrieval, MLLM-based embedding models do well when the text describes the scene (general retrieval), but poorly when it describes actions and their direction (time-sensitive retrieval). TARA, a pioneering work, fine-tunes such a model on text triplets with temporal hard negatives; it improves time-sensitive retrieval but lowers general retrieval (mean R@1 over six benchmarks in our rerun on Qwen3-VL-Embedding-8B: 58.4->44.8), and its released model keeps general retrieval only when ensembled with Qwen3-VL-Embedding. Our goal is strong general and time-sensitive retrieval with one model and one vector per clip. Three findings lead to three simple components. Keep loss: general retrieval follows how far the output embeddings drift from the base model, not how far the weights move; we therefore keep them near the base model, which keeps most general retrieval even without general-domain data. Pair loss: the text embeddings of an action description and its reversed-direction version are well separated, but the video embeddings of a clip and its reversed copy are close; we therefore widen the gap between the video embeddings of each clip and its reversed copy, in the direction of the difference between their two text embeddings. Middle-layer LoRA: frames are combined in the middle layers, and temporal information peaks in or just after them; we therefore train LoRA only on these layers, which nearly matches training all weights on most time-sensitive benchmarks with 0.15% of the parameters and keeps general retrieval better. General R@1 stays at 58.4, while R@1 with each clip's reversed copy in the gallery rises from 55.5 to 63.2; the gains carry over to unseen benchmarks and InternVL2-8B-RA. The keep loss is also a remedy for the TARA recipe (general R@1: 44.8->57.8), and ensembling TARA with Ours gives the highest scores on RTime and unseen CiA among the compared models.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Open-world object detectors aim to localize objects of classes not covered by their training annotations, but most label them only as "unknown". This label tells a system that an object is present but not what it is, while its broader category would allow a more informed decision. We therefore address both uncovering unknown objects and assigning them to the broader categories of a given class taxonomy. For uncovering, we find that most unknown objects are already localized and labeled "unknown" in our base detector, but are discarded during final top-k selection because background receives higher scores. To mitigate this, we propose a class-agnostic ranker that estimates how well each predicted box fits an object and uses these scores to select the final predictions. Across three benchmarks, our detector improves unknown recall by 3.8% to 195.1% relative to the strongest baseline in each task, while maintaining competitive known-class detection. For categorizing, we draw on Euler diagrams, in which the region of a category contains the regions of its members. Our categorizer builds each category's region from the known classes and learns how much room to leave for members it has never seen. On the same detections, it categorizes unknown objects more accurately than existing hierarchical methods. Code is available at https://github.com/TUM-AVS/NEST.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Fine-tuning a vision-language model on its own failures is a direct way to repair it, but accuracy on held-out failures, the obvious measure of success, misleads. Many mined failures are not model errors: a human audit of 100 failures of Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct on questions generated from COCO and GQA found label errors in 37. And mined failures share wording regularities: question-only classifiers get both answers right on 73.2% of held-out failure pairs. We study both traps with verified failure-driven repair (VFR): typed minimal-pair failure mining, verification by three LLM judges that answer without seeing the stored labels, answer-first corrective targets, and LoRA fine-tuning with replay. VFR repairs 82.8% of held-out failures (base model: 10.0%), and on independently labeled, unconditioned pairs it raises overall accuracy from 87.4% to 92.4% through spatial relations, whose accuracy rises from 66.7% to 96.8% even where the wording points to the wrong answer. Yet on negation, of which it repairs 88.9% of held-out failures, its accuracy on unconditioned pairs falls from 88.7% to 79.7%: it breaks negations about absent objects, rare in its training set. Without verification, accuracy on unconditioned pairs falls to 80.8%, below the base model's 87.4%, through a negation shortcut our development benchmarks miss; without failure selection, it stays at 92.0% (one seed) while repair falls to 73.4%. Training also lowers two-image relation accuracy (74.3% to 49.6%; replay data alone: 48.4%); multi-image replay partly and unevenly reverses this. Paraphrasing the spatial questions keeps most of the gain (87.3% vs. the base model's 62.0%), though rewording lowers spatial repair on held-out failures from 92.9% to 76.8%. On What'sUp's independent photos of objects under others, caption choice stays perfect while accuracy on our yes/no pairs falls from 84.5% to 26.4%.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Structured clinical decisions from volumetric computed tomography (CT) require anatomically grounded predictions and calibrated confidence estimates. However, existing 3D medical multimodal models often suffer from uncalibrated autoregressive decoding and shortcut reliance on irrelevant anatomical regions. We introduce Radev, a decision-native multimodal model that integrates anatomy-guided visual compression, direct candidate prediction, and counterfactual confidence supervision. To bridge global context with fine-grained slice details, a frozen 3D encoder extracts reusable organ-level features, which are dynamically selected by the query to attend to 2D slice features within predicted anatomical bounds. The resulting compact visual tokens enable the language backbone to estimate candidate probabilities in a single forward pass without autoregressive decoding. We further propose Counterfactual-Calibrated Training (CCT) to compare predictions under intact and intervened organ representations. CCT encourages confidence to decrease when removing target-organ evidence degrades decision quality, while penalizing excessive confidence drops under unrelated-organ removal. To facilitate rigorous evaluation, we construct CT-Verdict, a comprehensive benchmark spanning binary finding presence, multiple-choice questions, and ordinal severity assessment across both in-distribution and external cohorts. Extensive experiments demonstrate that Radev significantly outperforms state-of-the-art methods in both decision accuracy and confidence calibration, while generalizing robustly under distribution shifts. Project page at https://juntaojianggavin.github.io/projects/Radev/

</details>

Abstract (英文): Egocentric video question answering enables blind and low-vision (BLV) users to query their immediate surroundings through natural language. However, existing benchmarks and models primarily optimize for semantic agreement with reference answers, reducing visual assistance to a purely factual matching task. In real-world scenarios, a factually correct response remains practically unhelpful if it lacks the spatial orientation, actionable detail, or conciseness required by the immediate task of the user. Drawing upon real BLV egocentric videos and authentic user queries from EgoBlind, we construct the PA-EgoQA, a preference-aligned dataset that reconstructs reference answers according to empirical BLV communication criteria. Based on this dataset, we introduce Preference-Aligned Visual Egocentric Learning (PAVE), a unified learning and inference framework that couples structured answer-policy fine-tuning with deterministic question-conditioned routing to adapt response formulation directly to the surface form of the query at inference time. We further align the model via Filter-GRPO, which pairs a hierarchical correctness-quality reward with contrast-based candidate-group filtering to extract learning signals from both factual accuracy contrasts and fine-grained quality variations among correct answers. Extensive experiments across three vision-language backbones demonstrate consistent improvements in factual accuracy alongside substantial gains in assistive response quality. These results establish a concrete paradigm for translating human-centered response requirements into egocentric supervision, generation policies, and post-training objectives. Code will be provided at https://github.com/shawnliang420/PAVE.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Recent neural implicit and Gaussian splatting approaches have achieved impressive results in multi-view surface reconstruction. However, jointly optimizing geometry and appearance introduces ambiguities. A parametric appearance model can compensate for geometric errors, reproducing the input images accurately without an accurate surface. We demonstrate accurate neural implicit surface reconstruction using only multi-view patch photoconsistency with geometry-based visibility, without volumetric rendering or parametric appearance models. For each reference ray, sphere tracing computes the first surface intersection. Its position and normal define a local patch whose photoconsistency is evaluated across visible neighboring views using colors sampled directly from the input images. For efficient sphere tracing, we use a 1-Lipschitz multi-layer perceptron. We use a single photoconsistency loss, without foreground masks or auxiliary geometric regularization. Experiments show that our method achieves the lowest mean Chamfer distance on BlendedMVS (BMVS) among compared methods and matches the geometric accuracy of leading reconstruction methods on DTU.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Visual token compaction reduces inference cost by passing only selected image patches or video tokens to later layers. In sequence models with local convolutions, each removal changes which tokens are neighbors. Learning which tokens to keep therefore requires a training signal that accounts for the new neighborhoods. We introduce ProbConv, which computes the exact expected convolution before activation at a kept position under independent keep decisions. The operator is differentiable in the keep probabilities. With binary decisions, ProbConv and step gating reproduce the compacted Mamba block. Across image, vision--language and video backbones, ProbConv is strongest under strong compaction: its advantage over matched training controls grows as more tokens are removed. In Cobra, it also outperforms adaptations of published vision--language pruning methods at matched inference budgets. ProbConv also improves over matched zero-mask training in video frame selection and speech frame dropping. Our code is available at: https://github.com/gmum/probconv

</details>

Abstract (英文): Physics-based soccer characters must perform diverse motor skills and transition smoothly between them, such as dribbling into a shot. However, skills that perform well in isolation may fail when executed sequentially, as preceding behaviors can leave the character and ball in unfamiliar states. We present MESSI (Multi-Expert Soccer Skill Integration), a framework that enables multiple soccer skill transitions through shared adaptation while preserving the original skill policy outside transitions. MESSI first trains eight task-competent experts using motion priors and task rewards, then distills them into a unified, skill-conditioned Transformer. Rather than retraining individual experts for different transitions, we train a shared, context-conditioned low-rank adapter on the frozen student through online reinforcement learning. Activated upon skill switching, the adapter helps the target skill handle unfamiliar entry states, while a learned exit mechanism determines when to return control to the original student. By disabling the adapter during native skill execution, MESSI preserves the original student policy by construction. Experiments across individual skills, skill transitions, and continuous skill chains demonstrate substantial improvements over direct switching, including an increase in dribbling-to-shooting success from 19.8% to 61.8%.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Scaling embodied simulation requires diverse objects with executable physical representations. We present RFW-W, a physical asset universe connecting rigid, articulated, and deformable asset sources to task-conditioned scene composition. Its reported inventories comprise 1,010,386 asset records: 524,611 rigid assets in 1,212 categories (RFW-R), 402,410 articulated assets in 531 categories (RFW-A), and 83,365 jointly verified deformables in 485 categories (RFW-S). These taxonomies contribute 2,228 dataset--category entries. RFW-R couples category-guided generation with collision geometry, composed physical properties, and interaction annotations; RFW-A uses reusable agent-authored procedural generators with explicit part and joint structure; RFW-S uses structure-aware simulation representations, calibration, and independent testing. A common interface preserves physical requirements specific to each asset type for retrieval and scene assembly. Quantitative evaluations demonstrate that RFW-R provides broader asset coverage and stronger scene-fulfillment capabilities than existing datasets. Across five RoboTwin tasks, using 100 demonstrations per task for each asset source, policies trained on RFW-R achieve macro success rates of 71.2% in clean settings and 41.8% under randomization, exceeding the strongest baseline by 15.8 and 10.6 percentage points, respectively. RFW-W provides a scalable and unified asset foundation for generating physically grounded, task-conditioned embodied worlds.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Model-generated labels offer a scalable route to training lightweight vision models, but what makes a model a good teacher, and can we tell before training a student? We study this in gaze target estimation, where interactive robots need models that are both accurate and efficient. In a controlled comparison of eleven supervision conditions, we vary only the source of gaze-point labels, keeping the student, training protocol and 361k training samples fixed, across three video datasets, two student sizes and three seeds; every student is evaluated against human annotation. Three findings emerge. First, imperfect model-generated labels can replace human annotation: Gaze-LLE, never trained on the target datasets' gaze annotation, trains students that match annotation-trained ones on ViT-Tiny and are significantly more accurate on ViT-Small (average distance error 0.187 vs 0.196), and the ViT-Tiny student runs 32$\times$ faster than its teacher on a laptop CPU. Second, apparent competence can conceal unreliable supervision: given another person's face centre in the prompt, InternVL3 and Qwen3-VL return it on 93.4% and 81.1% of test samples, and a copy-the-coordinate baseline matches InternVL3's labels and students. Without that coordinate, both models fall far behind the specialist. Third, good teachers can be identified before training: validation label error ranks student test error (Spearman's $ρ= 0.91$), and ten annotated clips single out the two specialist teachers in 95% of draws. These findings give practical principles for assessing supervision, exposing shortcuts and selecting teachers for lightweight models.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Multi-frame video frame interpolation (VFI) aims to reconstruct dense video from sparse keyframes, requiring accurate intermediate motion and faithful appearance. As temporal gaps grow, flow-based methods can retain observed textures but misplace them under ambiguous motion, whereas iterative latent-diffusion models can synthesize plausible content but incur substantial sampling costs and may alter details during reconstruction with a variational autoencoder (VAE). We present RePix-VFI, named for Re-rendering observed Pixels, a multi-frame VFI framework that couples draft-conditioned one-step video diffusion with reconstruction from observed keyframes. A pretrained video diffusion model refines a flow-rendered draft of the whole window in a single forward pass. For each target frame, our generative readback matches its two bounding keyframes to the generated prediction using an optical-flow estimator. The resulting correspondences guide re-rendering of original keyframe RGB pixels outside the VAE. A lightweight appearance fusion head combines these renderings with generated appearance. Experiments on benchmark datasets demonstrate significant improvements in reconstruction and perceptual quality. On SNU-FILM at $\times$8 and $\times$16, RePix-VFI achieves the highest PSNR and the lowest LPIPS, FloLPIPS, and tOF among the compared methods, exceeding the best evaluated diffusion-based baseline by 1.71 and 1.48dB in PSNR, respectively. It also runs 32--42$\times$ faster than the state-of-the-art multi-frame VFI diffusion model.

</details>

Abstract (英文): RGB-based video object segmentation (VOS) remains brittle under fast motion and adverse illumination. Event cameras preserve signal under blur and challenging exposure, but provide little evidence when motion slows. Interaction can restore object state for subsequent propagation, yet promptable event methods merely address single windows. We formalize event-based Promptable Video Segmentation (ePVS), where prompts at arbitrary windows initialize, correct, or introduce objects and predictions. We present Event Memory-As-Prompt Segmentation (E-MAPS), which transfers a pretrained promptable segmenter to event streams and learns propagation and correction. Event-to-Token (E2T) aligns recurrent event features with pretrained representations while preserving prompt-conditioning priors. Memory-Aware Prompting (MAP) converts object-conditioned history into sparse prompts, allowing memory and user inputs to share one decoder pathway efficiently. We construct Event-View Masklets (EViM) from 206K aligned RGB-event pairs and 620K masks for two-stage representation transfer and native event-video learning, and establish three training-disjoint benchmarks. E-MAPS leads prompt-once and interactive ePVS across all three benchmarks while being more efficient. It further demonstrates strong performance on zero-shot object tracking benchmarks. Project page: https://github.com/adonaydem/emaps-wacv

</details>

Abstract (英文): Despite strong performance on standard benchmarks, video reasoning models remain vulnerable to visual degradation, raising concerns about their reliability in safety-critical scenarios. Existing benchmarks address this concern in part by simulating visual degradation or probing model reasoning with challenging questions, leaving open how well models handle naturally degraded video and whether they can make the simpler judgments that matter for safe driving. To this end, we introduce GRAPE-Bench, which jointly evaluates video reasoning across visual degradation conditions and question difficulty. It combines a synthetic set spanning twelve corruption types across daily-life and driving videos with real-world footage of driving at night or in rain, snow, or fog. Each video is paired with three progressively challenging questions, from fundamental judgments with potential safety implications to demanding reasoning stress tests. Across open-weight and frontier models, we observe ranking reversals between synthetic and real-world conditions and level-specific weaknesses obscured by overall accuracy. Synthetic-data fine-tuning improves Qwen3.5-9B's real-world accuracy from 76.7% to 81.1%, while clean-privileged on-policy distillation reaches 80.4% in a separate reasoning-enabled setting. Through preference steering, we further demonstrate continuous control over a frozen model's trade-off between safety-relevant judgments and challenging reasoning, with little change in overall accuracy. Together, GRAPE-Bench provides a unified testbed for evaluating, improving, and controlling video reasoning under degraded visual conditions.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Affordable snapshot hyperspectral cameras capture spectral images in a single exposure but suffer from limited spatial resolution and sensor noise. Low-cost RGB cameras provide fine visible details to guide NIR reconstruction, but introduce spatially varying cross-camera misalignment. Supervised fusion methods require relatively large datasets of clean, high-resolution hyperspectral images, are sensitive to sensor noise, and generalize poorly across new sensors. We introduce SHARPnP, an interpretable, flexible framework coupling registration and reconstruction for RGB-guided hyperspectral super-resolution from noisy, unaligned measurements without clean references. We adapt a pretrained registration model via a cross-view loss backpropagated through a fixed plug-and-play fusion module. Temporal and recorrupted pairs enable reference-free self-supervision on noisy inputs. At test time, we calibrate cross-camera radiometry and refine alignment. Plug-and-play fusion employs sequential three-band denoising. On the REAL benchmark, SHARPnP outperforms supervised fusion baselines at $4\times$, $8\times$, and $16\times$ on clean inputs and at $4\times$ under Gaussian noise ($σ=5/255, 10/255$). On native RGB-HSI snapshot measurements, it reconstructs all 51 VIS-NIR bands, beyond the conventional 31-band VIS setting, without high-resolution references.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Representation-space generative models, such as Representation Autoencoders (RAEs), leverage pretrained vision foundation models (VFMs) primarily to accelerate training and improve sample fidelity. Yet VFMs are more than reconstruction tokenizers, as they feature rich representation spaces that are highly organized and linearly readable. We observe that this removes a key obstacle of post-hoc guidance: by linearity of expectation, a clean probe applied to an ideal denoiser's prediction yields the exact posterior mean of the probe scores at any noise level. This regime, which we call Semantic Mean-Space, turns semantic guidance into a Bayesian linear inverse problem, bypassing the need for training-time conditioning. With this foundation, we propose LiSM, a unified framework for Linear Guidance in Semantic Mean-Space. To validate the framework, we developed and adapted different solvers, and characterized them based on how they estimate probe-space uncertainty and propagate the correction through the representation. In addition, we propose Fit-Path, a solver designed to achieve strong global and local control. Our results in four control setups (class, attributes, foreground, and keypoints) show that LiSM methods, and Fit-Path in particular, provide effective, lightweight control across global and local tasks, outperforming noise-aware and pixel-space guidance.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Hyperspectral images (HSIs) record the full spectrum of light at every pixel, so projecting an HSI through a camera's spectral sensitivities gives that camera's RAW image, and one HSI can supply training data for hyperspectral reconstruction and camera-pipeline methods. Real HSIs, however, are slow and expensive to capture, and the largest datasets hold only about 1,000 images. High-level vision commonly replaces scarce real data with images rendered from 3D scenes. Low-level tasks, however, need physically correct spectra, which requires spectral rendering with a measured spectrum for every material in a scene, and existing spectral renderings cover only a handful of scenes, mostly with a simplified image formation model. We present Spectrify, a framework that converts 3D scene datasets into HSIs, together with a library of reflectance spectra labeled by material and compiled from close-range hyperspectral datasets and spectrophotometer measurements. In Spectrify, spectra follow the material: each object receives a physically based optical model and measured reflectance spectra or optical constants from its material category, each lamp an emission spectrum of its type and the sky a modeled daylight spectrum, and Mitsuba 3 path-traces the full light transport of each scene per wavelength. We show that a hyperspectral reconstruction network trained only on our synthetic HSIs generalizes to unseen real datasets better than networks trained on the largest real HSI datasets on most test sets, and that assigning spectra by material category instead of by color alone improves this transfer on every test set. RAW images projected from our HSIs are also useful training data for color constancy, performing on par with existing synthetic RAW generators. We release the 4,500 rendered HSIs, the code of Spectrify and its material-labeled reflectance library.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Amyloid positron emission tomography (PET) quantifies brain amyloid-$β$ as a standardized uptake value ratio (SUVR), but is costly and not widely available. Structural magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) is routine, and plasma biomarkers such as phosphorylated tau 217 (p-tau217) are becoming practical. Synthesizing amyloid PET from MRI alone is ill-posed, because brain anatomy reveals little about amyloid burden. We present SUPRA (SUVR prior-guided amyloid synthesis), which divides this task between MRI and plasma biomarkers. A regression maps the plasma panel to regional SUVR levels, painted onto the subject's MRI segmentation as an explicit prior. A 3D network then adds a residual correction guided by MRI. The output is in absolute SUVR, and the model also runs on p-tau217 alone. On the Alzheimer's Disease Neuroimaging Initiative (ADNI) cohort (111 test pairs), SUPRA reaches a peak signal-to-noise ratio (PSNR) of 23.4 dB, against at most 20.7 dB for four published models, and rank correlations of 0.56 to 0.67 with true SUVR in the composite cortex, an early-accumulation region and the striatum, against at most 0.24. In a second, cognitively unimpaired cohort with other plasma platforms (143 test pairs), it reaches 22.8 dB and 0.32 to 0.61, against at most 21.4 dB and 0.20. Its area under the curve for amyloid positivity is 0.91 and 0.82, against at most 0.63. Ablations show that plasma supplies the amyloid information and the network the image quality. Combining MRI with a blood test yields synthetic amyloid PET that is realistic and tracks burden across regions.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Monitoring Activities of Daily Living (ADLs) supports safety and well-being for people living at home, especially older adults, and radar sensing is gaining increasing attention in the field of activity recognition for its ability to ensure privacy in the home while also surpassing technical limitations of other sensing modalities. However, radar-based activity recognition remains constrained by the limited availability of large-scale datasets. We introduce the Radar-based Activities of Daily Living (R-ADL) dataset to addresses the gaps identified in existing work: 1) it expands the scale of available data by providing over eight hours of annotated recordings in which a person performs eight distinct activities; 2) it overcomes room configuration limitations by recording from five different locations in a two-bedroom, two-bathroom apartment; and 3) it extends beyond primitive actions to focus on high-level ADLs. In addition to the dataset, this paper introduces practical guidance for future ADL dataset construction. We conducted a detailed analysis of how ADLs are prioritized across academic research, health survey tools, and clinical trials to identify clinically significant ADLs. Furthermore, we provide our experimental design, data collection and annotation strategies, post-processing pipeline for forming the R-ADL dataset from raw data, and baseline results on standard machine learning architectures. The R-ADL dataset serves as a valuable resource for advancing ADL recognition systems as we continue building larger and more diverse datasets.

</details>

Abstract (英文): We present RenGen, a latent diffusion model that generates simple scenes from a few tokens and busy scenes from many, saving time and compute on simple scenes without losing realism. Most latent generators represent every image as a fixed grid of patch tokens, so a simple image costs as much to generate as a complex one. Prior methods that vary the token count through content-adaptive tokenization lose image quality as the count shrinks. RenGen instead trades scene complexity, rather than image quality, for fewer tokens. It does so by generating in the latent space of a pretrained region encoder, where each token represents one region of the image, and a decoder renders these region tokens back into pixels. Unlike patch tokens, region tokens cover areas of varying shape and position, so each token must capture both what its region contains and where it lies on the image canvas. We encode this spatial extent by adding positional embeddings to the encoder features before pooling, and we generate it by anchoring each token to a single randomly sampled point, from which the generator infers the region's shape, size, and content. At sampling time, the number of anchor points sets the trade-off between scene complexity and compute. On ImageNet $256{\times}256$, RenGen reaches a gFID of 1.34 with 88 tokens per image on average, the best among methods that vary their token count. It approaches the quality of RAEv2, a state-of-the-art patch-based model, at $2.5{\times}$ lower latency, advancing the Pareto frontier of generation quality versus compute. RenGen's gFID stays flat across scene complexity while latency varies by $2.4{\times}$ between simplest and busiest scenes, and its autoencoder reconstructs nearly as well as RAEv2 (rFID 0.32 vs. 0.30) with $2.9{\times}$ fewer tokens.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Language models can generate LEGO$^\circledR$ assemblies from text, but their builds often contain parts that collide or attach to nothing. The usual approach of rejecting and resampling invalid placements discards decoded tokens without guaranteeing a valid build. We present BriConstrain, a constrained decoding method that restricts a pretrained language model's next-token probabilities before sampling, steering it towards valid placements. Because the validity of a brick placement depends on the non-predetermined build state, it cannot be compiled into a fixed grammar and must be checked dynamically. However, checking the validity of a brick before sampling is challenging as the space of valid placements is combinatorially large and cannot be enumerated exhaustively. Our key idea is to narrow the search space by constraining how parts attach rather than which parts attach to each other. Concretely, given the model's predictions of the currently placed part, and the previously placed parent it wants to connect to, we constrain the model's choice of how they attach. This allows us to span the space of part attachments, using a precomputed geometric oracle, and to effectively filter out colliding options. With valid attachment candidates in hand, we bias the model's next-token distribution towards the remaining valid candidates. We evaluate our method on two LEGO$^\circledR$ formats and models: BrickNet, which annotates connectivity explicitly, and our proposed Implicit Connectivity LDraw Format (ICLF), which uses numeric values for placements and leaves connectivity implicit. Our method raises collision-free builds from 31% to 91% on BrickNet and from 15% to 65% on ICLF, and outperforms rejection sampling on both formats at about 60% of its decoding cost.

</details>

Abstract (英文): We present Physicl Scenes, a high-quality artist-created and curated dataset of 6,499 3D scenes spanning household rooms, public interiors, and outdoor spaces. The dataset combines detailed geometry, physically based materials, and carefully composed environments with object- and part-level semantic annotations, providing rich, joint supervision for visual and embodied learning. We demonstrate its value as training data across inverse rendering, 3D reconstruction, scene understanding, and mobile manipulation. Across these tasks, training state-of-the-art methods with our scenes improves performance over alternative synthetic datasets. In addition, we introduce a mobile manipulation benchmark, evaluating vision-language-action models performing physical pick and place in diverse 3D scenes with realistic layouts, and using our part annotations to also score where each object is grasped. Our dataset will be released publicly to facilitate research in visual and embodied AI.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Embodied Spatial Reasoning (ESR) is central to deploying Vision-Language Models (VLMs) in real-world embodied tasks, yet remains inadequately evaluated. Existing benchmarks primarily assess spatial reasoning in static images or synthetic indoor environments, where semantic shortcuts often suffice, making it difficult to faithfully evaluate spatial reasoning capabilities in the wild. In light of this, we present Lost on Campus, a benchmark for evaluating ESR in large-scale real-world outdoor 3D environments reconstructed by 3D Gaussian Splatting. Our benchmark introduces a unified reasoning-action evaluation framework that seamlessly integrates diagnostic QA for isolated reasoning with closed-loop interactive navigation for active reasoning under multimodal instructions. To enable fine-grained diagnosis, we evaluate six capability aspects of ESR: action grounding, spatial foresight, metric awareness, goal-directed planning, ego-allocentric localization, and spatio-temporal consistency. Extensive experiments reveal that: (i) Compared to indoor settings, real-world outdoor environments impose substantially higher demands on spatial reasoning, where existing models exhibit poor performance; (ii) VLMs still struggle with fine-grained visual alignment, with failures in spatial foresight and ego-allocentric localization emerging as primary bottlenecks; (iii) Improving multimodal interaction capabilities and long-term spatial reasoning is crucial for advancing embodied intelligence. These findings highlight that faithful evaluation of ESR demands benchmarks tightly coupling perception, reasoning and action within realistic and continuous environments.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Creating a dialogue scene typically requires cutting between multiple views (e.g., close-ups, medium shots, and over-the-shoulder shots) of the actors. Yet capturing such coverage is costly and in many settings unavailable. We study dialogue re-cinematography: synthesizing additional shots from a single wide-angle dialogue video. Existing video generators can synthesize new shots, but rely on visual cues alone, so when the source video barely shows the lips, the generated lip motion fails to match the speech. Our key observation is that the speech audio is view-invariant: it constrains lip motion in any target view. Building on this insight, we adapt a pretrained audio-visual diffusion model to generate the new shot from a target frame that specifies the view, a source video that contains the performance, and the speech audio that drives lip motion. In multi-person scenes, however, pretrained audio-visual models drive every visible face with the same speech, causing speech leakage, where silent listeners mouth the speaker's words. We therefore introduce speaker-aware audio gating, a training-free module that lets the audio drive only the faces that speak in the target shot. On a cinematic paired-view benchmark spanning diverse viewpoints and framings, our method lowers perceptual error by 59%, landmark error by 56%, and speech leakage by 97% relative to the strongest baseline, Wan 3.0. Our method also remains robust on in-the-wild scenes, highlighting its potential for film post-production.

</details>

Abstract (英文): The development of biomedical vision-language models (VLMs) with contrastive image-text pretraining has been driven by scaling to increasingly large and diverse image-text datasets. Beyond scaling, further progress can come from learning richer representations from existing data. We hypothesize that the structure of scientific literature itself provides an additional source of supervision: figures, panels, captions, and accompanying article text encode rich relationships across modalities. Yet, treating these as independent image-text pairs overlooks their relationships. To address this limitation, we incorporate these structural relationships into contrastive image-text supervision. We preserve exact image-text matches while introducing weaker positive supervision for related pairs, such as a panel and the caption of a sibling panel. We upweight cross-article negatives of the same content type to encourage finer discrimination. We also enrich panel descriptions using visual content, original figure captions, and mentioning paragraphs. Pairing panels with these descriptions and whole figures with their original captions yields Loom52M, a dataset of 52.4 million image-text pairs spanning scientific charts and biomedical images. Using Loom52M, BioLoom (DFN-2B) achieves 56.82% domain-balanced mean accuracy across 67 zero-shot classification tasks from 43 datasets in 10 domains, compared with 48.77% for MedPMC-CLIP. On BioLoomBench with concise captions and BioMedFlickr, it achieves bidirectional mean Recall@1 of 63.20% and 10.59%, versus 46.37% and 7.55% for the respective strongest evaluated baselines, BiomedCLIP and MedPMC-CLIP. Code, datasets, benchmarks, and models are available at https://bioloom-clip.github.io/.

</details>

Abstract (英文): We present DiaCap, an audiovisual LLM for comprehensive dialogue captioning, which produces the transcript, timestamps, and a visual description of the speaker for every utterance in a video. Considering that utterance timing and audiovisual diarization are tightly coupled, DiaCap unifies the tasks in a single model, trained in two complementary stages. First, we train DiaCap on 225K curated clips, using synthetic captions that densely label every utterance in detail. Second, we further refine the model via reinforcement learning on 2.9K human-corrected clips, using fine-grained feedback that directly evaluates utterance timing, speaker grouping, and speaker binding. On DiaDemBench, which we extend with utterance-level timestamps and subtitle redaction, both DiaCap with off-the-shelf transcripts and its end-to-end variant DiaCap-E2E achieve strong results. Specifically, DiaCap performs on par with Gemini 4 Argon in audiovisual diarization while clearly outperforming it in timestamp accuracy (76.59 vs. 42.76 TIME). It also maintains strong diarization on multilingual and minute-long videos, and transfers to audiovisual question answering.

Abstract (英文): The appearance of a scene is determined by the albedo, roughness, metallicity and normal of every point on its surface, and by the light that reaches it, its irradiance. Recovering these intrinsics from images is ambiguous, and methods that predict them per pixel give the same surface point a different decomposition in every view. We introduce PointX, a simple feed-forward model that predicts intrinsics per 3D surface point instead. A multi-view transformer encodes a set of unposed images, and for any queried surface point, PointX pools the point's tokens across the views in which it appears and decodes the point's intrinsics once. Each point of a 3D reconstruction of the scene, such as a point cloud or a mesh, thus receives a single prediction, which is exactly consistent across views by construction and enables view-consistent relighting and editing. The same model also predicts per-view maps, from one image or many, by querying the point seen at every pixel. These maps agree across views up to the precision of the estimated geometry and correspondences. PointX is significantly more consistent than existing methods, while remaining competitive in accuracy from a single image and improving as views are added. Project page: https://pointx-paper.github.io/

</details>

Abstract (英文): Tenuous atmospheric layers play an important part in applications ranging from modeling atmospheric heat to transportation safety, disaster and hazard response to wildfires and volcanic eruptions. The height and optical properties of atmospheric layers are usually provided by satellite lidar observations. A known challenge in the identification of atmospheric layers is the detection of tenuous clouds and aerosols, which often escape existing layer detection schemes. The problem lies in the discrimination of faint signals from background, especially in situations where stronger reflectors exist within the same regions, during nighttime and daytime conditions. The Density-Dimension Algorithm (DDA) is an auto-adaptive algorithm which has had wide application from the detection of crevasses on ice sheets in ICESat-2 Advanced Topographic Laser Altimeter System (ATLAS) data, to the detection of atmospheric layers in ICESat-2 and CALIPSO lidar data. This paper focuses on the implementation of the DDA for atmospheric lidar data collected by the Cloud-Aerosol Transport System (CATS) aboard the International Space Station (ISS). We aim to demonstrate the versatility of the DDA in detecting a variety of atmospheric layers, with an emphasis on tenuous clouds and aerosols. Layer detection facilitated by the new CATS-DDA is evaluated using airborne data collected with the Cloud Physics Lidar (CPL). Comparisons of CATS-DDA results with detections in the CATS Level 2 Feature Mask product indicate an increased detection capability for tenuous layers and a refined, more realistic representation of atmospheric layers afforded by the high-resolution capabilities of the DDA, which retains the native resolution of the recorded data. Beyond the functionality of the DDA for layer detection in CATS lidar data, results from this paper serve to demonstrate the ability of the DDA to overcome some of the limitations of the CATS instrument. These findings may aid in developing instruments and algorithms for future atmospheric satellite missions, such as NASA FALCON (Fleet for the Atmosphere Linking Commercial Observations with NASA).

</details>
