Abstract (英文): Type 1 diabetes (T1D) requires lifelong insulin therapy, and reinforcement learning (RL) has shown promise for automated dosing in the UVA/Padova simulator. However, the decisions of RL controllers remain difficult to interpret. We present XRL4T1D, a post-hoc explainability framework for evaluating pretrained insulin-dosing policies without retraining. Each policy uses 60 minutes of continuous glucose monitor (CGM) and administered-insulin history, sampled every 5 minutes, to recommend insulin. We use SHapley Additive exPlanations (SHAP) to compare the importance of CGM and insulin-history inputs and summarize insulin-history attributions over three 20-minute windows motivated by insulin-on-board. We assess whether these attributions correspond to policy-output sensitivity through targeted input replacement and compare temporal rankings with TimeSHAP. In all 80 patient-policy-mask-size cases, replacing the highest-attribution inputs changes the normalized insulin recommendation more than replacing the lowest-attribution or random inputs. The highest-attribution insulin-history window is also the most output-sensitive in 115 of 120 patient-policy-scenario configurations, and KernelSHAP and TimeSHAP select the same top window in 111 of 120. Applying XRL4T1D to PPO and G2P2C across 20 virtual patients, we find that both controllers rely predominantly on CGM history, especially G2P2C. However, the distribution of insulin-history importance across recent, middle, and older windows varies by cohort, explainer, and analysis settings, without a consistent separation between controllers. Global feature-attribution rankings remain stable under the tested observation corruptions. These results show why attribution fidelity, temporal reproducibility, and controller-comparison robustness should be assessed separately.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Agents that search patient charts must classify claims from retrieved records, including cases with missing or conflicting evidence. In Fast Healthcare Interoperability Resources (FHIR), status fields can change how those records should be interpreted. PatientFlowSynth provides 18,000 tasks from 225 synthetic patients and 18 templates that hold the patient, question, and retrieval program fixed across chart variants representing four evidence states. Re-executing the program verifies each variant's reference answer, evidence sets, and search requirements. Strict success requires a completed response with correct labels and citations exactly matching an accepted evidence set retrieved during the episode. Where required, a tool response must also show a complete search at the specified scope. In a study using GPT-5.6 Luna and 225 synthetic patients (3,600 matched pairs), explicit Encounter-status guidance increased strict success from 40.7% to 51.3% (+10.6 percentage points; 95% patient-bootstrap confidence interval, 8.4 to 12.8). Among cases whose primary reference label was insufficient evidence, label agreement fell from 49.2% to 29.3%. A prespecified two-model replication reproduced both changes. Failed retrieval can mask interpretation errors by producing a correct insufficient-evidence label without examining the relevant records. Additional model comparisons showed that the benefit of guidance was model-dependent. Retrieval, citation, and classification should be evaluated separately.

</details>

Abstract (英文): GUI agents can choose correct actions yet fail as interfaces change during deliberation. Latency alone cannot explain these failures because actions may become invalid at different interaction stages. We model four events: environment change, observation availability, decision commitment, and action receipt (EODA). We introduce ChronotopeGym to study these failures under Paused, Replay, and Live execution. Across five tasks, agents can fail in Live despite succeeding when responses remain valid, even when actions meet deadlines. On Moving Balloon, updating a pending click raises success from 0% to 100% without changing response time. Our accelerator-resident GUI controller, Chronotope, maintains compact interaction history and selectively keeps, updates, or redecides pending actions. Compared with Agent S3, Chronotope reduces median end-to-end latency by 85.5% for a 27B model, expanding its performance envelope to 100% Live success at an 8-second deadline versus 0%. Chronotope enables real-time GUI control through compact interaction history, knowing when to preserve, revise, or replace actions, and an overall efficient architectural design that obviates verbose reflection, reasoning, and grounding calls.

</details>

Abstract (英文): The correctness of actions taken by agents is typically evaluated against known, externally verified ground truth labels, such as whether a proof is correct or whether code compiles and passes unit tests. However, as coding and science agents are entrusted with more end-to-end autonomy -- proposing interventions, running experiments, and deciding what to deploy and investigate next -- whether an agent's actions genuinely improve an outcome must be inferred from noisy, often inconclusive experiments. To evaluate agents in this setting, we define agent quality as a weighted sum of correct decisions along its trajectory, using sequences of $e$-values as statistical evidence regarding correctness in place of ground-truth labels. Our framework accommodates arbitrarily dependent decisions, data-dependent trajectory termination, dynamically generated hypotheses, and quality measures defined after the trajectory concludes. We construct upper and lower bound estimators and confidence sets for agent quality with statistical guarantees that hold uniformly over agent trajectory distributions and establish instance-wise sharpness of our estimator class. We demonstrate our methods with a case study comparing the success of Claude Opus and Claude Haiku in improving the runtime of an open-source Python package.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Code-executing LLM agents submit blocks of code that issue several API writes. If a block fails after some of its writes have taken effect, the agent rewrites the block and continues. It is unknown how often such a recovery harms the external state when the failure occurs naturally in the code written by the agent. In this paper, we measure it on recorded trajectories of AppWorld agents. In a stratified random sample of 200 candidate episodes across ten models, 145 are partial executions confirmed by the database state. In the sample, 30 of 143 confirmed partial executions with a determinable outcome are followed by a harmful recovery, and its violation remains at the end of the run, which gives a stratification-weighted estimate of 24.6%. Two thirds of the harmful recoveries duplicate an effect, and the rest delete user data or make a wrong first-time write, e.g., an order of a wrong product. A rule that searches for repeated calls finds 75% of duplications but only 41% of all harm. We also examine two sources of operation identity for an idempotency contract. In a small pilot (17 episodes, one local model), identifiers supplied by the model do not detectably reduce harm, because the model issues a new identifier for the same operation. Identity derived from the call content, evaluated counterfactually on the sample, would have matched every harmful write in 14 to 18 of the 30 harmful recoveries, but in none of those with a wrong first-time write, and it would also have matched writes required by the task in 30 episodes. Based on these results, we outline design requirements for safer agent recovery.

</details>

Abstract (英文): LLM-based user simulation can support interface design, accessibility research, and interactive-agent evaluation, but standard computer-use agents are optimized for task completion rather than reproducing how people perceive, reason, and act. We introduce COGENT (COGnitive agENT), a modular cognitive architecture for simulating user interaction with desktop interfaces. COGENT draws on established cognitive theories, such as Norman's action cycle and ACT-R, to enable explainable, intervenable, and modular user simulation by decomposing the simulation process into cognitive modules with explicit representations of perception, memory, user state, intentions, and action selection. We begin by presenting six intervenability demonstrations, each tracing how a single edit to memory, a user attribute, or a processing module propagates through the cognitive pipeline to produce a behavioral change. We evaluate COGENT's behavioral and attributional fidelity across three benchmarks -- AgentNetBench, A11y-CUA, and SENSE-42 -- finding that it achieves statistically significant fidelity gains over computer-use baselines in reproducing recorded human behavior and recovers the relational structure among human self-reported psychological states. Together, these demos and evaluations demonstrate COGENT's utility toward more faithful, explainable, intervenable, and modular LLM-based cognitive simulations.

Abstract (英文): In playtesting games, a human operator is typically required to test most gameplay elements. In this work we present Buddy, an autonomous game solving agent which can solve long time horizon objectives in an unseen 3D role-playing game using off-the-shelf language model systems. Buddy will take actions, receive feedback on those actions, and pursue a goal across many turns. No written walkthrough or model training of any kind is required, and we show that this paradigm holds in a novel environment where the agent must discover solutions via exploration rather than recalling them from pretraining. We demonstrate three downstream capabilities: game solving with no provided walkthrough, logging bug tickets for encountered difficulties, and conversion of successful agent traces into scripted hierarchical task networks for regression testing. Unlike existing playtesting solutions, our framework is fully modular: language models and harnesses can be easily swapped out as capabilities improve over time.

Abstract (英文): Autoformalization translates a natural-language statement into a formula that captures its intent, which depends on the statement's context: the background that disambiguates it. LLMs make autoformalization practical, but their probabilistic output carries no formal guarantees. We propose techniques for safe autoformalization that certify one-sided logical adequacy: a formula sufficient for the intent is safe to enforce, and a formula necessary for it is safe to rely on. We use LLMs to generate candidate formulas and order them by logical strength in an Entailment Graph (EG), an ordering a solver decides. Each edge carries a residual and a co-residual, the two adjoints of the entailment order, all fixed by the candidate pool alone. In a context, two searches return the sufficient frontier, the weakest candidates that entail the statement, and the necessary frontier, the strongest candidates the statement entails; each step is one local check against an edge's residual or co-residual. The frontiers bracket the statement and yield the ambiguity residue, which holds exactly of the situations the candidates certify neither as satisfying nor as violating it; the statement is ambiguous precisely when the residue is satisfiable. For natural-language statements and contexts, an LLM entailment oracle decides the checks; it is sound if it accepts only checks that hold, and complete if it accepts every check that holds. We prove that a sound oracle makes the search safe regardless of pool quality, and that a complete one makes the frontiers tight. On security-critical AWS properties, EGs are safe in all 20 cases of an expert-adjudicated study, where majority vote over equivalence clusters and Universal Self-Consistency are unsafe in 25% and 10%. An ablation with the same oracle but no graph is unsafe in 25% and rejects every candidate in another 30%. We machine-checked our proofs in Lean.

</details>

Abstract (英文): This paper investigates Product Quantization (PQ) Optimization with Gaussian data. It offers three key contributions (i) a proof that SVD is the optimal preconditioning rotation for PQ; (ii) a new Extended Zador (EZ) estimator of the $k$-means quantization cost that is accurate in low bit rates and low dimensions; and (iii) a novel segmentation algorithm based on 1-d bin packing of global bit allocations. The result is a simple algorithm (EZPQ) with strong approximation guarantees. It matches OPQ's empirical accuracy while providing a per-instance distortion lower bound certificate under the Gaussian EZ cost model. On typical real embedding spectra, EZPQ with 2 bits per coordinate is often certifiably within 1% of the optimum in this model. It also consistently achieves a lower reconstruction error than all random-rotation-based algorithms such as EDEN, E-RaBitQ, and TurboQuant.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language models are increasingly fine-tuned on sensitive data, where the same person's information may appear multiple times across records. This can cause models to memorize sensitive information, but memorization does not necessarily mean that the information can be recovered. A more serious privacy risk arises when the model begins to reproduce that information during generation. In this paper, we study how often sensitive information must appear in the fine-tuning data before it becomes recoverable. Across four language models, we vary the frequency of email addresses and phone numbers from $1\times$ to $50\times$ and compare standard fine-tuning, gradient clipping, and differentially private training. We find that the number of repetitions required before sensitive information becomes recoverable varies substantially across models. Gradient clipping can delay extraction, but it does not consistently prevent leakage at high repetition. DP-SGD at $\varepsilon=5$ produces zero observed extraction through $50\times$ repetition across all four models under the attacks we test, although the utility cost varies by model. These findings characterize how observed extraction varies across sensitive-data frequencies, model families, and training mechanisms, and highlight substantial differences in extraction behavior across models. Code and data are publicly available.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Frontier models like GPT-6 Astra are increasingly able to do substantial reasoning without a monitorable chain of thought, chains of thought are becoming less legible, and are often monitored asynchronously to reduce cost. We propose several variants of this architecture designed to balance tradeoffs and discuss other benefits, such as capability removal.

Abstract (英文): Designing linear solvers for discretized partial differential equations requires problem-specific expertise. We present a multi-agent framework, in which language-model agents propose, critique, implement, and evaluate solver configurations assembled from Krylov methods, preconditioners, block factorizations, and neural operators. Inside the proposing agent, a non-dominated sorting genetic evolutionary algorithm evolves the population of meta-solvers toward the Pareto front of six measured quantities, so that one run returns a front of trade-offs rather than a single tuned solver. The system runs on small open-weight language models served on a single laptop, and we compare it with a single agent on four problems. For three-dimensional acoustic scattering and elasticity, it delivers 47 and 48 convergent hybrid solvers out of 60 candidates. Abstract (英文): Reasoning models degrade as their context grows, so longer reasoning traces do not by themselves give longer effective horizons. One way to extend the horizon is to compress the reasoning history into a compact state from time to time and continue from the state. Existing methods train the state with the final-answer reward or distill it from a stronger model. Neither signal is tailored to measure the contribution of the state alone. We observe that a reasoning model that degrades over long trajectories still behaves reliably in short contexts. We aim to preserve this short-context behavior when compressing the reasoning history. Motivated by this, we train a separate summarizer with Delta-KL, a reward computed from the frozen reasoning model's token-level predictions on sampled continuations, and run inference by truncating the trace, summarizing it, and continuing from the summary (TruSuCo). Training uses a few thousand questions with 4k-token reasoning prefixes and does not need answers or external teachers. Under a fixed 32k-token budget, the trained summarizer improves on vanilla reasoning and on an untrained summarizer in all nine settings across mathematical benchmarks and Reasoning Gym puzzles, by up to 7.7 points and 12 points, respectively. The improvement over vanilla reasoning grows with the budget. At 128k tokens, our method outperforms it by 10 points on AIME 2024--2026. Analyses show that truncation prevents the collapse of vanilla reasoning and that the trained summaries retain more of the reasoning history.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Speculative decoding accelerates LLM inference by letting a cheap drafter propose tokens that the target model verifies in parallel. Parallel block drafters such as DFlash and P-EAGLE propose a whole block in one forward pass, but they lose the dependence between tokens in the block; DSpark restores part of it with a lightweight correction head that runs sequentially. Measuring DSpark, we find that this head overrides the base prediction on most slots, so the expensive base readout through the target's full lm_head mostly provides a seed. We therefore propose Wavefront, which follows a simple principle: shrink the base and strengthen the correction head. Wavefront replaces the full lm_head with a learned low-rank base, conditions the correction head on the whole drafted prefix within the same block, and resolves it with a few parallel Jacobi passes. A fixed pass budget makes the draft step free of data-dependent control flow, so we capture it as a single CUDA graph in vLLM with fused kernels. On Qwen3-8B, Wavefront improves throughput over xPress, DSpark and DFlash by 2.6%, 10.7% and 23.6% on average across math, code, chat and agentic benchmarks, and by 9.6%, 14.3% and 28.9% on SPEED-Bench long-context prompts. On Qwen3.8-27B it leads DFlash2, DSpark and xPress by 7.6%, 10.6% and 5.0%, and by 7.4%, 5.8% and 6.0% on the same long-context prompts. The gains persist as serving load grows to concurrency 32.

</details>

Abstract (英文): AI agents are increasingly expected to support users over long periods, across work, and daily life. This requires modeling not only who users are, but also how their goals, interests, activities, and interactions evolve over time. A challenge in longitudinal user simulation is maintaining consistency between these evolving states and the consequences of actions in a shared environment. We introduce E2SIM, a longitudinal user simulation framework that jointly models evolving actor states and a dynamic shared environment. E2SIM connects actor-state updates with shared-world changes through a feedback loop linking immediate actions, delayed outcomes, and actor-specific observations across work and life. This allows the consequences of earlier actions to shape later behavior, while preserving differences in what each actor knows. We further propose a suite of longitudinal trajectory diagnostics, measuring dynamic consistency, coherent event progression and collaboration, and non-degenerate trajectory development rather than static consistency alone. In one 60-day simulation, E2SIM produced more extracted event threads judged meaningful by our evaluator and fewer near-duplicate actions than four direct-generation baselines. The framework and evaluation methods provide a basis for studying longitudinal user simulation, with potential applications in training and evaluating AI agents that assist users over extended periods.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Mechanisms recur across the sciences: the equation for heat in a solid also describes molecular diffusion or a Brownian particle. A mechanism is rarely written in one equation; its operation, boundaries, parameters and closures are stated at different steps of a derivation, where methods that read text or fit formulas miss it. We built an equation-native system that reads the equations of all 2.5 million arXiv manuscripts, and show that mechanisms can be compiled from them and constructed by deterministic calculation. A constructor detaches, transfers, unfolds, composes and joins their parts across disciplines; where parts do not fit, the failure is an equation for the missing term. Constructions turn a threshold reactor into a memory that a weak signal writes, an oscillator into a switch through two changes that each fail alone, and a rhythm into the regulator of a chemical amount, each with conditions for an experimental test.

</details>

Abstract (英文): AI inference infrastructure is commonly analyzed as an energy business. Under full total cost accounting it is not: in the reference deployment modeled here, capital recovery is 87.3% of fully loaded cost per token and electricity 3.7%. Because capital accrues per unit time irrespective of use, accelerator value is destroyed by non-utilization rather than by consumption. Yet, SLO qualified goodput reaches only 59.6% of nameplate throughput, and the decomposition of the loss chain shows that 25.2 of the 40.4 lost percentage points arise from scheduling idleness and stranded burst reserve. Both are properties of a tenant's demand portfolio rather than of its silicon, and neither is diversifiable by a single tenant acting alone. Modelling reserve and idle capacity as diversifiable risk, pooling demand across eight imperfectly correlated tenants (rho = 0.3) recovers 7.3 pp of goodput, approximately $0.75M per annum per megawatt of IT capacity, or between 2.8x and 4.9x the entire annual interconnection spend of the deployment that produced it. The implication for neutral colocation and interconnection providers is that the switching and interconnection layer aggregating tenant demand functions as an instrument of capital productivity while being priced as transport.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) ranks memory chunks by semantic similarity to the query. Many methods improve on this by learning from feedback on what was retrieved, but each is a separate heuristic, with no account of when it is the right choice. We introduce BRAG, a generative model of retrieval with use. Each chunk generates the queries it answers from a mixture of von Mises-Fisher components anchored at its original embedding, and each chunk has a latent utility that drifts over time. The model gives the optimal inference, a closed-form filter that stores a few keys and two scalars per chunk and updates them online from the labels of a curator, and it frames retrieval as a control problem. It also shows when existing methods are optimal. Query averaging, Rocchio-style feedback and frequency reranking are special cases with fixed parameters, NUDGE is the case of one key per chunk with no dynamics, and LADER the limit of one key per past query. On two public long-term memory benchmarks, BRAG matches these methods on queries asked before, as expected, because when nothing changes over time the assumptions behind these special cases hold. On harder problems, where a new chunk makes an old one obsolete, BRAG outscores them. They all stay at plain-search level (Recall@5 0.67-0.71), while BRAG retrieves the new chunk 0.80-0.86 of the time. We also ran BRAG on three of NUDGE's seven text benchmarks, and it matches or beats NUDGE on all three. On nine months of a personal agent's memory (20,640 chunks), BRAG raises Recall@5 on held-out queries from 0.75 to 0.98 and Recall@1 from 0.44 to 0.97, with little change on chunks never queried.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Diffusion multimodal large language models rely on confidence to decide when to commit candidate tokens during generation. In multimodal tasks, however, confidence cannot distinguish whether a candidate originates from image evidence or the language prior. We perform paired forward ablation and find that nearly nine out of ten high-confidence commits are insensitive to the image, while degenerate and hallucination samples exhibit significant increases in the entropy of deep-layer attention organization. Based on this diagnosis, we propose CD-APC, a training-free decoding method. CD-APC performs evidence-adjusted scoring within a base-supported pool and uses dual gating based on model acceptability and contrastive concentration to decide whether to commit, together with an inverse-window mechanism that adapts to inter-step difficulty. Across two backbones and five benchmarks, CD-APC consistently improves performance, indicating that stable visual evidence improves model outputs.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language models (LLMs) have transformed how humans interact with information, enabling natural-language interfaces across various domains. They are increasingly deployed for geospatial applications including map-based question answering, location-based services, and natural-language interfaces to spatial databases. But are LLMs truly location-aware, able to reliably interpret and precisely answer location queries involving real-world coordinates, points of interest (POIs), and geospatial distances? To answer this concretely, we present LimoBench, a street-level benchmark of 82 natural-language location queries over seven canonical spatial operations: geocoding, reverse geocoding, routing, directionality, range search, $k$-nearest-neighbor search, and spatial join. The queries are grounded in real map data of West Lafayette, Indiana, and are graded by location and semantic complexity. They are designed to probe LLMs' street-level spatial awareness, from simple lookups to multi-step spatial reasoning. We also present Limo, an approach based on the Model Context Protocol (MCP) that enhances the location awareness of LLMs. Limo grounds LLMs in PostGIS-backed spatial data through a compact set of tools. We find that ungrounded models mostly achieve single-digit accuracy (3.1% for GPT-6 Luna) across different spatial operations. In contrast, the same models achieve near-perfect accuracy (99.3% for GPT-6 Luna) when given access to Limo's spatial tools through MCP. Current LLMs thus have limited and unreliable street-level spatial knowledge, while grounding provides accurate answers at modest overhead (+0.2 s latency, 7.7$\times$ tokens, and 2.2$\times$ cost for GPT-6 Luna). We release the benchmark, Limo toolset, and evaluation code to encourage the community to evaluate their own location-aware LLM approaches.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Ultrasound imaging provides views of internal tissue in real time for medical diagnosis. However, ultrasound signals may be affected by interference, and correcting errors and removing transient interference while preserving valid echoes remains challenging. We propose CROSS-Calib, a channel recovery method that uses predictions from other channels as references for amplitude and phase calibration, while a gate using context selectively combines calibrated measurements with predicted echoes to retain usable samples and repair unreliable ones. On the in vivo test set, CROSS-Calib outperforms all baseline models under gain errors and transient interference, while phantom tests show that the model restores cyst contrast with echo widths of wire targets close to the clean reference. CROSS-Calib can perform channel calibration and selective repair before beamforming to improve echo usability and image quality.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Macrocyclic peptides are a growing therapeutic modality, and co-folding models are increasingly used to predict their structures. Yet published evaluations rely on small, loosely curated target sets that differ from paper to paper, so results are not comparable, and most report pose RMSD alone, without checking whether the predicted molecule is chemically correct. We introduce PRETZEL (Prediction of Ring-Enclosed Topology: Zero-shot Evaluation of cycLic peptides), a benchmark of 285 curated cyclic peptide-protein complexes spanning three cyclization chemistries, all post-dating the training cutoff of every model evaluated. PRETZEL has three components: PRETZEL-SET, an evaluation set annotated by cyclization chemistry and chemical novelty; PRETZEL-TWIST, a specification layer that compiles a single canonical macrocycle description into each engine's native input format and verifies the returned structure; and PRETZEL-SALT, an evaluation suite pairing standard pose metrics with validity checks for bond distances, bond angles, dihedrals, and ring closure. Using these, we evaluate AlphaFold3, Boltz-2, Protenix-v2, and HighFold3. Protenix-v2 achieves the best median RMSD at 9.05 A, with 19.3% of predictions under 2 A and 37.5% under 5 A. Accuracy varies by cyclization chemistry: models handle head-to-tail macrocycles relatively well but degrade on stapled targets. Chemical validity is weaker still: at most 8.1% of predictions have a fully valid set of inter-residue bonds, and no engine closes every macrocycle in more than 80.7% of its predictions, so pose accuracy alone is an insufficient criterion. These failures share a cause: co-folding models place macrocycles without representing them, treating cyclization and non-canonical chemistry as annotations layered onto a residue sequence rather than as first-class structure. PRETZEL is released with all inputs, predictions, and evaluation code.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language models can now write simulation programs of operational processes and workflows. These programs can be reused to answer what-if questions on new records and policies. However, a program that reproduces recorded history can still give wrong answers to what-if questions; requested changes may be applied wrongly, or they may be applied to records other than the ones the question is about, or the program may stop being valid once its policy is revised. Current checks return a pass-or-fail verdict for the whole program, which says little about whether any single answer can be trusted. We propose Vouch, a library that judges whether an answer from a generated simulator can be trusted for the change being asked about. Each what-if question is turned into a structured change, which is checked against the program and the current records. The library also keeps a record of the kinds of change each program has passed or failed, and repeats the relevant tests whenever the program is reused. We apply it to three program-generation platforms, with programs built by five language models, across four domains: manufacturing, IT operations, and pharmacy and loan-application workflows, using independently checked reference answers. Our baselines are the platforms' own checks where such checks exist, an LLM-as-a-judge, and a ReAct agent that can run the program. We measure how many correct answers are trusted, how many are wrongly rejected, how many wrong answers are accepted, and what the checking costs.
  On programs written by Haiku and Sonnet in one platform, Vouch removes 97.6% of the wrong answers, while still trusting 80.1% of all answers. At equal coverage, it accepts fewer wrong answers than LLM judges built on Claude Haiku. We disclose code and results for reproducibility.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Sustained Large Language Model (LLM) inference generates significant heat that exacerbates silicon aging and reduces GPU reliability. Because GPU temperature fluctuates continuously with workload intensity and ambient conditions, keeping it in a safe range requires real-time frequency scaling, which inevitably comes at the cost of either throughput or accuracy. To address this tightly coupled challenge, we propose GreenDynaMix, the first runtime system to jointly integrate a DVFS-based thermal manager with a dynamic precision-tuning throughput compensator. A sensitivity-aware allocator leverages intrinsic importance signals from the Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) router to assign higher precision to critical experts, preserving accuracy. Experiments on server, desktop, and embedded platforms demonstrate that GreenDynaMix reduces the temperature-induced aging rate of GPUs during LLM inference by 25-33%. Without offline profiling or model retraining, it recovers throughput at a perplexity cost of 0.9-1.6% with 8-bit/4-bit mixed precision, rising to 9.4% where memory forces 4-bit/2-bit.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Expert pruning reduces the memory footprint of mixture-of-experts language models, but retaining model quality depends on which experts are removed. Frequency- and magnitude-based scores measure the expert in isolation and overlook how normalization consumes its contribution: subtracting one expert changes both the direction of the residual stream and the scale applied to what remains. We propose FN-Prune, a training-free criterion that compares the next RMSNorm outputs before and after subtracting each router-weighted expert contribution, holding original routes and weights fixed. Squared differences are averaged over all calibration tokens in one pass; low-scoring experts are physically deleted at uniform per-layer ratios, and inference reroutes among survivors. At 50% expert removal, FN-Prune achieves lower WikiText-2 perplexity than the best published methods on Qwen3, ERNIE, and OLMoE, including a 32% reduction on OLMoE. At the same budget, GSM8K accuracy improves over the best published methods by 7.5 and 7.0 percentage points on Qwen3 and ERNIE, respectively, while multiple-choice gains remain task-dependent.

</details>

Abstract (英文): The growing autonomy of AI agents in using external tools and making decisions has created an urgent need for comprehensive safety benchmarks. Existing benchmarks test compromised tool channels or adaptive multi-turn attacks, though they may not capture the unique capabilities of policy-governed tool-use agents, which must resist unauthorized requests without abandoning the legitimate work embedded in the same interaction. To fill this gap, we introduce TACT-Bench, a stateful benchmark built around scenario-specific stories, personas, manipulation tactics, and response-adaptive escalation. Each adversarial task targets an action explicitly prohibited by the policy provided to the assistant, while the policy remains hidden from the attacking user. TACT pairs each adversarial task with at least one benign counterpart, and its adversarial tasks retain authorized ground-truth actions when a compliant resolution remains available. We define these base tasks as TACT-Base. We additionally introduce TACT-TrustPrime, which places an independent benign task before an unrelated attack in the same conversation. We also propose Paired Selective Trustworthiness (PST), which combines explicit safe refusal with demonstrated capability on matched benign tasks, to analyze an agent's overcautiousness and practicality. Averaged across 6 different models as assistants, TACT-Base raises Attack-Induced Failure Rate (AFR) by 17.53% over four compatible attack baselines, and TACT-TrustPrime raises it by a further 17.01%. The two evaluated defense methods reduce AFR by only 8.35%--9.41% on average and do not consistently improve PST, underscoring the need for stronger policy-enforcement methods.

</details>

Abstract (英文): We refactor existing Lean 4 proofs to reduce source length and checking cost while preserving theorem statements and compatibility across Lean versions. A locally served Qwen model uses the open-source Codex CLI to inspect source, retrieve declarations, and test proof replacements. The host keeps a replacement only if it improves the objective and Lean accepts it on every required version. A deterministic CPU pass then refines the verified proofs. On the 60 problems in Lean Refactor Arena, model search achieves a local mean combined score of 82.07, and cleanup raises it to 82.18. The final proofs pass all 127 required proof--version checks. Our system placed first in the open-source LLM track of Lean Refactor Arena at VeriCodeGen.

Abstract (英文): Scientific measurements reported in scholarly articles are often embedded in compound figures rather than available as machine-readable data. Recovering these measurements at literature scale requires an integrated workflow for figure localization, panel decomposition, chart identification, numerical reconstruction, and experimental context association. We present \textit{SciFig2Data}, an automated, high-throughput workflow for harvesting structured experimental data from scientific PDF repositories. The system integrates multi-scale document element detection, adaptive compound figure decomposition, two-stage OCR-based semantic filtering, Swin-based chart classification, and instruction-guided multimodal large language model (MLLM) reconstruction. It further incorporates directory-level batch execution, caching, and structured logging to support large-scale processing. We demonstrate the workflow on two biomedical literature corpora comprising 605 toxicology and pharmacology articles and 551 lipid nanoparticle (LNP) articles. The pipeline successfully reconstructed 468 and 242 tabular outputs from the respective corpora, illustrating its practical applicability to literature-scale scientific data harvesting. By associating reconstructed measurements with caption-derived experimental metadata, SciFig2Data provides a workflow for transforming graphical evidence in scholarly publications into structured, context-enriched data records for downstream AI4Science applications.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Frontier models are increasingly used through harnesses as agents, with growing autonomy to do useful work. Agent performance can often be enhanced by the use of skills: reusable workflows, code, and prompts that provide task-related information or guide the behaviour of the model. Skills, being textual in nature, present themselves as another editable surface to be optimized; current methods have improved on handcrafting and one-shot generation by treating skill development as an iterative process, using score-driven search to tweak and improve performance. In this paper, we demonstrate the efficacy of the agents themselves as optimizers: across five benchmarks, a coding agent without guidance substantially improves every target and outperforms published skill-optimization methods. More broadly, this shows the capability of agents to do such work and to refine their own behaviour to provide task-specific context, as long as they are given a metric to measure against.

Abstract (英文): Existing frameworks in causal abstraction first propose a high-level causal model, and then verify whether its interventional effects agree with those of a given micro-level model - the two models are declared causally consistent when the discrepancy is zero. However, proposing a consistent macro causal model is difficult, because macro causal relations are almost never well-defined, since distinct micro-level interventions associated with the same apparent macro intervention can produce different effects. Our central insight is that causally consistent macro-level models can be obtained constructively: ill-defined macro interventions can be interpreted as missing variables on the macro level, and can be accounted for by introducing a complementary variable q that retains omitted causally relevant state. In the linear case, q has a unique form and we provide sufficient conditions for valid implementations to exist; in the nonlinear case, we provide an ODE-based algorithm for constructing implementations. We call this framework Constructive Causal Abstraction (CCA), and use CCA to formulate the difference between machine-learning model auditing and editing. With the Bias-in-Bios biographies dataset, we demonstrate that CCA allows practitioners to articulate assumptions when detecting the model's gender bias. Finally, CCA resolves a well-known paradox known as "interpretability illusion" which points to a mismatch between the subspace for editing and the one where the model implements a signal-to-output pathway.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Realistic modeling of human-driven vehicle (HDV) interactions is essential for automated driving safety testing in urban areas. Existing methods struggle to reproduce realistic safety-critical interactions involving high collision risk for the following reasons: they often assume full observability, overlooking interactions arising from occlusion. Driving styles are mainly characterized by vehicle maneuvers, limiting the diversity of safety-critical interaction behaviors. Moreover, learning signals from safety-critical interactions can be diluted by non-critical driving behaviors. To address these issues, we propose a generative AI agent to emulate interactive HDVs. The agent incorporates field-of-view modeling to account for driver visibility under occlusion and characterizes the interaction process using five metrics spanning its initial, intermediate, and final stages. It automatically learns diverse interaction styles through a dynamic mixture-of-experts network. Through residual reinforcement learning, the agent further refines generated trajectories to match the real-world distributions of safety-critical interaction characteristics. Experiments on the INTERACTION dataset show that our method reduces Jensen--Shannon divergence (JSD) by 39.9\%--87.1\% across five interaction characteristics compared with a state-of-the-art baseline. Our method achieves greater improvements of up to 87.1\% in final-stage interaction metrics, suggesting that it can reduce error accumulation in long-horizon trajectory generation. Ablation studies confirm the effectiveness of each proposed component, particularly highlighting the importance of residual refinement. Generalization experiments show that our method approaches the performance of full training on unseen intersections with only one epoch of fine-tuning.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Deep networks embed inputs in high-dimensional representations, but how much of this capacity actually drives behavior? We introduce a framework for identifying, at each layer, the subspace of the representation that is functionally sensitive to the task. Using a matrix analogous to the Fisher information but defined over the hidden representation, we rank directions by their influence on downstream computation and project onto the most task-sensitive subspace. Across pretrained vision networks, representations are remarkably compressible: a small fraction of the task-sensitive subspace preserves most performance, often requiring far fewer dimensions than geometric alternatives. We find that this compressed representation is also more robust to adversarial attacks generated on the full network. We additionally show that this decomposition recovers functionally distinct subspaces within the same representation: on cue-conflict stimuli, shape-sensitive and texture-sensitive directions are largely non-overlapping, and ablating one selectively impairs the corresponding task while leaving the other intact. Task-sensitive subspaces also provide a principled foundation for continual learning: constraining gradient updates to lie orthogonal to the task-sensitive subspaces of prior tasks outperforms standard variance-based gradient projection methods. Finally, restricting representational comparisons to task-sensitive subspaces reveals that inter-model alignment is higher in task-sensitive directions than in task-insensitive directions at later layers, and that conditioning on different tasks over the same stimuli uncovers asymmetries that full-space similarity measures cannot detect. Together, these results establish that deep networks compute through a low-dimensional functional spine embedded within a much larger representational space.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Generating Chinese Gaokao mathematics finale problems requires controlling not only the included skills, but also the underlying mathematical kernel and solution path. Existing LLM-based methods mainly rely on surface-level specifications, while the realized difficulty of generated problems cannot be determined directly from prompted targets. Therefore, we propose GaokaoMathGen, a framework for Chinese Gaokao mathematics finale problem generation controlled by latent reasoning structures. It extracts standardized latent reasoning structures from seed problems and performs constrained synthesis using skills, methods, a core identity, and a pivot step as structural controls. We further estimate realized difficulty through logical-quality filtering, fixed-solver decoding effort, structural features, and solver-output search signals. Experiments show that semantic normalization improves cross-annotator comparability and that 78.0% of 609 scored candidates pass the logical-quality filter. In a teacher audit of the 60 highest-effort candidates, all were judged to reach finale or penultimate problem difficulty, and only one exhibited a severe logical issue. These results support latent reasoning structures as effective control signals for generating high-difficulty mathematical problems.

</details>

Abstract (英文): As AI transitions to sociotechnical deployments, uninterpretable models pose serious threats to reliability, public trust and contestability. However, not all interpretation methods are created equal: for interpretability to enhance deployment safety and effectiveness, interpretation methods must be evaluated in a tiered system to differentiate evidence levels of trustworthiness, rather than validated through isolated interpretation instances. Our core vision is that elevating the evaluation of interpretation methods to a core AI assessment metric will drive robust knowledge discovery from data (KDD) and ensure safe, contestable sociotechnical deployments of AI. To this end, we propose TRUST, a tiered evaluation framework that builds upon the Predictability-Computability-Stability (PCS) principles for Veridical Data Science (VDS) and the Predictive, Descriptive and Relevant (PDR) foundations. To differentiate interpretation methods effectively and systematically, TRUST grades interpretation across five tiers: Task-Aligned PDR Foundations, Rigorous Epistemic Grounding, Unifying Semantic Understanding, Scope Transferability, and Targeted Prospective Validation. We present a start-to-finish case study of the iterative Random Forest (iRF) family under TRUST and contrast it with other LLM interpretation methods that struggle to clear specific tiers. Finally, we conclude with brief initial thoughts on how TRUST principles can extend in autonomous agentic frameworks. Ultimately, the TRUST framework fosters contestability and epistemic grounding as foundational evaluation principles for human-machine workflows and, eventually, autonomous agentic workflows.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Emotions are central drivers of human behavior, yet most behavioral models fail to capture their role in shaping perception, decision-making, and collective action. Application areas such as emergency response and public health are especially constrained by the lack of emotion-aware modeling. While large language models (LLMs) are increasingly integrated into agent-based models (ABMs), little work has examined how to endow LLM agents with emotional reasoning or validated their ability to reproduce empirically observed emotional patterns. This study addresses this gap by introducing a hierarchical prompting framework that conditions LLM agents with demographic, psychological, and contextual cues to generate event-conditioned emotional responses to real-world events. We validate the resulting event-conditioned emotional responses against large-scale social media reaction data derived from the Social Science One Facebook URL Shares Dataset, complemented by demographic and personality benchmarks. Our simulation results reproduce both polarized and consensus-driven emotional patterns with significant fidelity, supporting the application of LLM agents for emotion-aware modeling in behavioral simulations. We also find, however, that systematic gaps in emotional expression of LLM agents and human populations exist, such as the underexpression of anger relative to human baselines. As such, these findings highlight the need for continued calibration if LLM agents are to serve as proxies for human populations in behavioral modeling. By quantifying their divergence from human populations in terms of emotional expression, we hope that our methods can aid in this effort.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Species-level differentiation of Plasmodium falciparum (PF) and P. vivax (PV) determines treatment, yet deep learning detectors rarely test whether their explanations are reliable. In this pilot feasibility study of TRACE-Mal, a research prototype combining YOLOv8n-seg detection and a six-model soft-voting ensemble, we examined whether agreement among three post-hoc explanation methods (Grad-CAM, LIME and SHAP) is associated with the correctness of species calls. the study analysed 2,847 parasite crops from 250 thick smears within publicly available Lacuna Malaria Datasets. The model achieved an mAP50 score of 0.79 and the ensemble had a macro F1 of 0.84 (PF recall 0.86; PV recall 0.74). Unadjusted analyses of crops revealed that predictions explained by greather than or equal to 2 methods were more accurate (86.2% vs 78.9%; difference 7.3 percentage points; 95% CI 3.5-11.1) compared to those explained by less than 2 methods. As the number of explanatory methods increased so did prediction accuracy (from 73.8% for no methods to 89.5% for all three methods). When performing a cluster-bootstrap analysis of 60 test images, again there was a positive consensus difference (7.3 percentage points; 95% CI 2.9-11.8). Further, incorporating explanation information into a model's prediction confidence enhanced its error differentiation ability (AUROC 0.024; 95% CI 0.008-0.041). PF and PV parasite crops have distinct morphologies. Although based on one unvalidated public data source, these findings demonstrate proof-of-concept and should not be taken as a measure of diagnostic capability.

</details>

Abstract (英文): The numeric settings of a multi-stage LLM pipeline (a compound AI system) are the usual target of tuning. We tuned all of them for an LLM pipeline for schema matching and mapping, with accuracy and cost as joint objectives, and we show what this buys, what it cannot buy, and where the remaining accuracy is. First, tuning buys cost and a little accuracy. On 35 tasks, with whole groups of tasks held out from tuning, one tuned configuration gains 2.9 points of top-1 accuracy (HR@1) over the default at 18% more cost, and another keeps the accuracy of the default at 32% lower cost. Second, the accuracy depends on a choice that numeric tuning cannot reach. Restricting the search for each source column to one assigned target table changes HR@1 by +30 points on a MIMIC-III to OMOP task and by -52 points on a task built from database views, more than numeric tuning gains on nine of ten test tasks. Neither setting is right for all tasks: on the larger set of 41 tasks used for the remaining results, mean HR@1 is 75.7% with the restriction (the default) and 83.1% without it. Third, choosing the setting per task is not enough either: even an oracle that picks the better setting for each task reaches only 87.8% on the 41 tasks, because the better setting differs between the columns of one task. The pipeline therefore runs both settings, and an LLM judge chooses among their candidate answers on the 26% of columns on which they disagree. This reaches 91.5% HR@1 on the 41 tasks, at 5.9 times the cost of the default, about one cent per column.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Real-world software engineering (SWE) tasks require agents to understand repository structures, cross-module dependencies, and runtime behaviors, posing significant challenges for constructing high-quality training and evaluation data. To address this, we propose RepoEviAgent, a scalable SWE data construction framework that integrates quality-aware data curation and role-specialized multi-agent task synthesis. Starting from 8,477 open-source repositories and approximately 2.48 million pull request records, we employ semantic alignment, complexity constraints, and multidimensional quality assessment to identify valuable SWE data. We further develop a sandbox-based multi-agent framework that integrates automated environment construction, execution validation, repository exploration, and evidence-grounded Q&A synthesis. Based on this framework, we construct RepoEviQA, a benchmark for Repository-Level Evidence-Grounded Question Answering, comprising 334 tasks across five programming languages and diverse SWE categories, with rigorous rubric-based evaluation. We evaluate five frontier coding models on RepoEviQA, revealing substantial performance differences across task categories and programming languages.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Spiking neural networks for multimodal audio-visual classification reach high accuracy by adopting deep-learning machinery: multi-stage convolutional or transformer encoders trained end-to-end with surrogate-gradient backpropagation through time. These prior works typically rely on full-precision (32-bit) parameters and do not treat hardware efficiency as a primary design objective. In this paper, we propose NeuTNN-AV, a much simpler hardware-amenable multimodal substrate built on the recently proposed NeuroAI Temporal Neural Network (NeuTNN) and demonstrate that it can match or outperform state-of-the-art SNN baselines with a fraction of their synapse counts. NeuTNN neurons carry multiple active dendritic segments. Each input line spikes at most once (temporal coding) and every weight is learned via a local reward-modulated spike-timing dependent plasticity (STDP) rule. All weights are 4-bit integers and inference uses only comparisons and additions. On CREMA-D, it reaches 78.6% with 2.34M synapses establishing a new state-of-the-art SNN accuracy, against 77.14% for MISNet-XL (31M parameters). On UrbanSound8K-AV, NeuTNN reaches 98.82% with 1.38M synapses, against 96.85% for SMMT (9.99M parameters) and 98.96% for MISNet-XL (31M parameters). Weight memory is 23-106x smaller than the spiking baselines, and the measured synaptic energy per inference is two to three orders of magnitude lower. Furthermore, the proposed NeuTNN learns CREMA-D in a single pass with 73.8% accuracy and demonstrates rapid online continual learning by adapting to unseen actors from a few dozen clips.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Block-causal video diffusion generates long videos block by block, conditioned on a persistent key-value (KV) cache. After each block is denoised, an extra noise-free ($t=0$) forward pass writes its clean KV into the cache; once distillation and speculative early exit make denoising cheap, this clean KV update dominates the runtime. Reusing stale KV shortens the update, but stale memory corrupts the conditioning of later blocks and inflates their denoising steps, so the saved compute reappears downstream. We present SAMR (Step-Aware Memory Reconstruction), a training-free method for frozen causal video DiTs that refreshes only part of the cache and reconstructs the rest without DiT forwards. Our key observation is that, once rotary position phases are removed, motion-aligned historical KV and the in-flight KV a token holds at its exit step are complementary estimates of its clean KV. SAMR debiases in-flight KV with offline-calibrated per-head affine maps in Pre-RoPE space, fuses the two estimates, and reserves true $t=0$ refreshes for tokens flagged by an attention-readout error proxy, all executed by tile-aligned, synchronization-free Triton kernels. On 48-second LongLive-1.3B videos, SAMR reduces DiT latency by 20.9% and principal FLOPs by 30.7% relative to dense KV updates, lowers downstream denoising steps, and improves final-quarter PSNR over token reuse by 1.61 dB (1.14 dB on held-out VidProM-16). The reconstruction also transfers to frozen Self-Forcing-DMD-1.3B: zero-shot, it reduces reuse-induced step inflation ($2.47 \to 2.30$ steps/token) and improves late PSNR by $+0.33$ dB, and a one-time training-free calibration recovers the diagnostic score that zero-shot transfer lowers ($89.62 \to 91.10$).

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language model unlearning aims to remove the influence of designated training data while preserving unrelated model capabilities. Existing likelihood-based methods commonly optimize against fixed forget sequences, providing only negative supervision on what the model should not generate and overlooking the states visited by the evolving unlearned policy. We introduce Contrastive On-Policy Self-Distillation (CPSD), a new unlearning framework that constructs an explicit knowledge-reduced target distribution and distills it along the model's own trajectories. Given a student rollout, a frozen reference model evaluates each token under a plain context and a privileged context containing the forget information. CPSD treats the resulting distributional shift as a knowledge-associated direction and reverses it to obtain a counterfactual unlearning target. The student is then trained to match this target at every rollout position while retaining its utility on unrelated data. We further introduce a privacy-enhanced variant that combines distributional unlearning with lightweight sequence-level suppression. Experiments under OpenUnlearning show that CPSD improves the forgetting-utility trade-off over NPO across TOFU forget-set sizes and Llama models ranging from 1B to 8B parameters. On MUSE-News, the privacy-enhanced variant improves all reported knowledge, memorization, privacy, and utility metrics over NPO.

</details>

Abstract (英文): A tool call can fail even when the operation behind it is correct, because the receiving tool parses delimiters, dates or record layouts under a configuration the agent cannot inspect. Compiling a tool-use policy for such a tool requires both calibration, which evaluates candidate calls on candidate configurations, and search over the evaluated calls. The two share one compilation budget: calibrating everything first postpones any improved policy until the end, while re-solving every partially known model delays the next useful query. A call becomes usable as soon as its effects are known on the configurations that reach it. We present CAPE, a training-free compiler built on this observation. It maintains an executable policy at all times, directs calibration to the calls that can improve this policy, and tightens bounds only where doing so ends calibration early. Returned policies preserve every configuration the initial policy completes, and CAPE certifies optimality when its executable and optimistic values meet. On CSV and JSON workflows built from 64 requests, CAPE reaches the independently computed optimum in 502 of 504 compilations while taking 22.0% less compilation time than eager exact search. On larger CSV families it returns policies with higher completion at similar compilation time, and it raises completion by 15.38 to 34.66 percentage points over an LLM that revises its serialization from error feedback.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Vision-language-action (VLA) models must process continuous streams of visual observations, and the resulting computational cost limits real-time robotic control. Token pruning reduces this cost, but a fixed semantic scale of perceptual representation can overlook information needed at different stages of manipulation. We show that perceptual semantics can guide effective token selection without relying on internal attention when the selection scale adapts to the scene's evolving semantic structure. Based on this observation, we introduce TauPrune-VLA, a training-free method that controls the scale of the retained perceptual representation through a single perception temperature $τ$. To adapt this scale as manipulation proceeds, TauPrune-VLA updates $τ$ online using trends in perceptual semantics, with robot-state changes and action magnitude regulating the adjustment strength. While these semantics evolve over the course of manipulation, they remain relatively stable between consecutive frames. This continuity allows retention indices computed from the current observation to be applied at the next policy invocation, enabling token selection to run asynchronously with inference and reducing pruning overhead. Evaluated across diverse VLA models and simulation benchmarks, TauPrune-VLA achieves up to a $1.86\times$ inference speedup while maintaining near-baseline task success rates. We further validate its effectiveness on two real-world robotic platforms.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Flow-based diffusion models have become a mainstream paradigm for text-to-image generation, where task-specific post-training produces specialist models with complementary strengths. Multi-teacher on-policy distillation (OPD) offers an efficient way to consolidate these specialists into a single unified student. Recent methods strengthen supervision through teacher extrapolation, constructing stronger proxy teachers that allow distilled students to surpass the original specialists. However, their extrapolation directions remain defined solely by frozen teacher and reference models, leaving the student's own training progress unexploited. To address this limitation, we introduce DexOPD, a dual-extrapolation on-policy distillation method for diffusion models. DexOPD retains extrapolated teacher supervision while leveraging a lagging historical student as the reference for student self-extrapolation. Sampling across multiple self-extrapolation gains from shared initial noise broadens exploration along the student's evolving trajectory, and task rewards convert pairwise differences in candidate quality into a normalized auxiliary regression direction. Empirically, we evaluate DexOPD on GenEval, OCR, and DrawBench. The resulting unified SD3.5-Medium student achieves a GenEval reward of 0.9736, an OCR score of 0.9515, and a DrawBench Aesthetic score of 6.4875, surpassing the corresponding specialist teachers as well as all evaluated OPD and multi-task reinforcement learning baselines across these three benchmarks while preserving standard single-model inference.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Games, especially modern AAA games, challenge language models to perceive complex scenes, control actions, and make decisions over long horizons. Existing game benchmarks primarily test language models as computer-use agents that query a language model throughout play, incurring repeated inference cost and, in real-time games, acting while the environment continues to evolve. We ask whether coding agents can learn from game interaction to develop standalone game-playing programs that run without language model inference. We thus introduce Coding4Games, a benchmark for evaluating whether coding agents can develop standalone programs that play modern games through visual observations and standard game inputs. Coding4Games has three defining features: 1) Diverse Modern Games, covering 15 games with diverse visual, strategic, and control demands; 2) Development-Test Separation, in which a coding agent uses a sandbox and the standard player interface to iteratively develop a program, after which the selected program is frozen and independently tested without language-model inference; and 3) Outcome and Process Evaluation, which measures not only game performance and reliability but also how agents use feedback, revise programs, and resolve problems during development. Across 15 games, the best program per game outscores the strongest GUI baseline on 13 and ties it on one. The human player outscores all agents on 10 games. We additionally examine the effects of development budgets. Coding4Games evaluates whether coding agents can turn their understanding of complex interactive environments into programs that work reliably on their own.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Shared last-level caches (LLCs) increasingly dominate server processor die area, forcing architects to trade core counts for cache capacity. While intelligent data prefetching offers a pathway to reclaim this silicon, designing coordinated, multi-level prefetching policies for multi-core systems is bottlenecked by day-long, cycle-level simulations. This simulation wall limits even LLM-based microarchitecture discovery; the state-of-the-art ArchAgent system evolves one cache level at a time on single-core benchmarks, adds multi-core configurations only at the end, and trails the human champion under multi-core shared cache contention. We introduce DuoArchitect, an autonomous agentic framework for multi-fidelity, multi-core co-discovery of prefetchers. Agents jointly evolve evaluation proxies and coordinated prefetching policies across the L1D, L2, and LLC hierarchies, constructing simulation proxies 3-26x faster than cluster evaluation and hardware diagnostic oracles to guide the search. To study how much cache silicon a smarter prefetcher can replace, we evaluate and evolve prefetchers under reduced cache budgets, and find that evolving under our tightest cache constraint (with both the L2 and LLC halved) produced our strongest prefetcher overall at every cache size we tested: DuoArch-Nano. On the Fourth Data Prefetching Championship (DPC4) benchmarks with full-size caches, DuoArch-Nano delivers a 9.2% geomean IPC speedup (+10.5% on four-core mixes) while reducing four-core DRAM traffic by 11.4%, more than doubling the gains of the human champion BertiGO and the agentic ArchAgent v2. When the LLC is halved, DuoArch-Nano erases the baseline's 8.6% overall slowdown and edges out the full-LLC baseline overall (+0.88%), showing that agentic frameworks can generate prefetchers that reclaim megabytes of LLC SRAM per core with zero overall IPC penalty.

</details>

Abstract (英文): A masked diffusion language model is fast because it commits several tokens per step. Each token is sampled from its own marginal, so the dependency between the tokens is lost, and the size of that loss is the conditional total correlation C of the committed set. Most decoders choose the set from per-position marginals, which can bound C but cannot determine it. We ask how well they predict it. We estimate C for fixed candidate sets on four masked diffusion LMs from two families. On three of the four, the best marginal-only predictor is off by about one bit (0.74 to 1.17), 1.8 to 3.7 times less than a baseline that knows only the bound, and most of this accuracy comes from the bound itself. On a subset, our estimate ranks sets like the exact KL cost of a parallel commit, but sits a median 0.4 to 1.1 bits below it on prose and when the rest of the text is still masked. Dream-instruct is the exception, mainly on prose, where its conditionals are least self-consistent. Among six common marginal rules, pooling over the set beats gating on its weakest position in all 24 paired comparisons. The measurement needs a validated readout: Dream and LLaDA place a position's prediction at different logit indices, and the wrong index inflates an inconsistency diagnostic 14 to 41 times. We give three checks that measure the convention. Our results concern predicting C for a fixed set, not what a dependency-aware decoder gains.

</details>

Abstract (英文): LLMs can reason over tables, but when tables outgrow the context budget, retrieving relevant rows is only part of the solution. A retrieved subset may omit information required for an exact aggregate; even complete evidence may remain difficult for an LLM to use. We introduce TableRouter, a training-free framework that analyzes each query, then chooses between two routes: a computation route, which derives question-conditioned statistics from the full table, and a coverage route, which retrieves relevant rows and column summaries. The chosen context is passed to a frozen LLM in one call. Across three LLMs and three large-table benchmarks, TableRouter outperforms the strongest of six single-call baselines in all nine settings by 1.2-18.3 accuracy points, using 77-95% fewer estimated FLOPs than truncated-table prompting, with indexing amortized across questions. The gains extend to long tables from databases not used in earlier experiments, where TableRouter achieves 35.8% with Qwen2.5-7B, versus 16.4% for the strongest external baseline. A controlled experiment shows that explicitly providing computed statistics improves accuracy by up to 40.0 points, even when all answer-determining rows are available. These results distinguish evidence access from evidence use: long-table reasoning depends on how information is represented, not only on which rows are retrieved.

</details>

Abstract (英文): As frontier AI models and agents are deployed more broadly in the real world, marketing is emerging as one of their largest applications. Marketing teams use AI models to produce brand assets like website copy and social media posts, while consumers' agents autonomously discover products, research purchases, and navigate websites. However, evaluating marketing capability is difficult because unlike areas like software development, customer support, or legal review where ground truth is more objectively defined, marketing is subjective, and output quality is judged based on taste and brand. We introduce Marketing-Bench, the first open-source marketing benchmark, which pairs discriminative tasks (detecting brand anomalies) with generative tasks (remediation), while keeping answers verifiable. Marketing-Bench tests five key pillars: factuality, consistency, coherence, structure, and brand voice, all crucial elements for good brand content on the web. We find that frontier models are far from solving the benchmark: the average model corrects only about half of the introduced issues (53.1%), whereas a marketing expert corrects nearly all of them. Even the strongest, GPT-6 Astra, reaches only 74.8%, with Claude Opus and Fable, Gemini Argon, and GPT-6 Sol falling well short. These findings suggest that marketing capabilities are less mature than adoption implies, and that principled evaluation should precede further scaling of usage. Marketing-Bench is a first step toward "frontier marketing models" with superhuman marketing performance.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Real-time control (RTC) for urban drainage systems (UDS) remains hampered by sparse in-network sensing. While heterogeneous multi-modal meteorological early warnings often contain decision-critical information for UDS RTC, none of the existing approaches can exploit such externally-sourced information within the control workflow. Here we introduce AgenticAI-Control, an agentic artificial intelligence framework able to assimilate multi-source meteorological early warnings in RTC. Two coupled large language model agents are integrated to generate control strategies by retrieving meteorological early-warnings and fusing them with domain engineering expertise. Validated via simulation studies of a combined sewer catchment in eastern China across 10 real-world rainfall events, the AgenticAI-Control achieved 69.1-90.6% reduction in aggregated flood and overflow volumes compared to the rule-based baseline control. Rigorously standardized prompt engineering and modular workflow constraints substantially compress performance disparities among four distinct LLMs. Our work establishes AgenticAI as a viable tool to unify multi-source meteorological monitoring and practitioner domain knowledge, surmounting longstanding monitoring limitations of UDS RTC and delivering cost-efficient, robust intelligent control solutions for sensor-sparse sewer infrastructures.

</details>

Abstract (英文): World-action models connect visual prediction with executable robot control. We present EffectWAM, which develops this connection through three complementary components: Effect, camera positional encoding (CaPE), and Action Grounding. Effect represents visual change as the difference between independently encoded future observations and a shared current-observation anchor. CaPE conditions endpoint pooling on relative camera pose when reliable extrinsics are available, while sharing the same learned parameters with pose-free pooling for uncalibrated trajectories. Action Grounding trains the visual representation to retain measured changes in end-effector pose and gripper state. An exponential moving average of the observation encoder supplies Effect targets. A shared generation expert learns to jointly predict Effect and an executable action chunk through flow matching, and reuses the sampled pair to generate future video on demand. This allows the controller to execute actions without waiting for video synthesis. We train on more than 20,000 hours of heterogeneous embodied data, excluding unused chain-of-thought data, and accommodate dataset-specific joint, end-effector, and hand-pose representations. Our evaluation uses the Small (8B) configuration on all five benchmarks and additionally evaluates the Large (26B) configuration on RoboDojo. Experiments examine closed-loop manipulation across complementary benchmarks, and comparisons across multiple baseline policies show consistent improvements from Action Grounding.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Reinforcement learning (RL) strengthens model capabilities through feedback from humans and environments. However, emerging evidence suggests that reward optimization can produce outputs whose errors are harder for humans to detect. To address this issue, we construct ITHV (the Image-Text Hallucination Verifiability Dataset), comprising 2,728 visually grounded instances that distinguish between obvious hallucinations, which humans readily detect, and elusive hallucinations, which humans struggle to identify. We then propose DAPO-V, an extension of DAPO that maximizes correctness subject to a budget on verifiability-aware risk. Specifically, DAPO-V combines a verifiability-aware reward with adaptive dual control, where larger penalties are assigned to incorrect responses on questions with lower human verification accuracy. An adaptive multiplier further adjusts the penalty strength according to risk-budget violations. Across three MLLM base models, DAPO-V improves overall accuracy while substantially improving accuracy on elusive hallucination questions by 5.78--9.72 percentage points over accuracy-only DAPO. We further test DAPO-V with a blinded human study, which shows that hallucinations are more readily detected in DAPO-V than those of the base model.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Long-horizon large language model (LLM) agents distill past trajectories into reusable experiences. Misleading or adversarial guidance in source trajectories can corrupt these experiences, allowing harmful instructions to persist across tasks and influence future agent actions. Assessing the risks of potentially corrupted experiences poses three challenges: (1) modeling provenance and execution dependencies before experience reuse, (2) learning task-conditioned risk from trajectory outcomes, and (3) repairing risky experiences at online inference. To address these challenges, we present ExpGuard, a framework for retrieval-triggered experience risk assessment and online repair. Specifically, to tackle challenge (1), ExpGuard integrates experience semantics with provenance and execution dependencies in a task-conditioned heterogeneous graph to predict attack and task-failure risks before experience reuse. To enable task-conditioned risk supervision and tackle challenge (2), we conduct matched counterfactual replays of each original experience and its minimally neutralized counterpart from the same task and environment checkpoint, deriving separate risk labels from differences in attack success and legitimate task completion. To tackle challenge (3) at inference time, ExpGuard repairs candidate experiences flagged as high-risk by the graph-based predictor at retrieval and re-predicts their risks before they enter the agent's active experience context. Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of ExpGuard in identifying risky experiences and defending agents against experience corruption.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Long-term memory retrieval often returns query-relevant records, but relevance does not guarantee sufficient evidence: answering requires a set of records that jointly satisfies a question's factual, relational, temporal, and state constraints. We study this demand--evidence misalignment through MemPath, an interventional framework that diagnoses failures in memory access, demand coverage, and evidence utilization. Guided by this diagnosis, we propose BiDE-Mem, which represents questions as typed demands and uses them to guide retrieval and evidence organization. We formalize demand--evidence support; in the evaluated compact path, it is conveyed through provenance-aware evidence organization and Reader guidance rather than a separate generative four-state verification step. On LoCoMo, BiDE-Mem improves accuracy by 6.23 percentage points over Mnemis; on LongMemEval, it achieves 91.80%, with strong multi-hop and temporal performance. Supplementary Qwen-Reader evaluations place it first among seven systems on LoCoMo and LongMemEval-S under two judging protocols. Ablations and evidence-level analyses show that demand representation, retrieval, and evidence presentation each contribute to answer quality.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Episodic-memory embodied question answering enables agents to answer questions based on previously recorded visual observations of the surrounding scenes. However, growing observation histories, varying observation quality across viewpoints, and limited computational resources make it challenging to construct and maintain compact visual memories while retrieving sufficient evidence for question answering. To address these challenges, we introduce CoverMem, which incorporates three key components: (1) a coverage-constrained memory construction method that selects a compact set of informative full frames satisfying object-quality and representative-scene coverage requirements; (2) a coverage-preserving incremental maintenance strategy that locally reselects only the affected frames to satisfy updated coverage requirements as observations arrive; and (3) a budgeted complementary frame selection method that balances question relevance, additional support for question requirements, and redundancy with selected frames within a fixed budget. Experimental results on three benchmarks covering diverse environments and question types demonstrate that CoverMem improves question-answering performance under limited visual budgets.

</details>
