Abstract (英文): Minimizer schemes sample at least one $k$-mer from every window of $w$ consecutive $k$-mers via a total order $π$ on $Σ^k$. For DNA ($σ= 4$), state-of-the-art lookup schemes such as GreedyMini and OptMini optimize a binary order ($2^k$ ranks) and lift it to $Σ^k$ by ranking one bit-plane primarily and breaking $2^k$-way ties lexicographically on the other bit-plane. At $(w, k) = (12, 5)$, this projection locks sets of $32$ DNA $5$-mers into contiguous blocks and restricts the active universal hitting set (UHS) prefix to $448$ of $1024$ $5$-mers, giving exact expected DNA density $2246214544 / 2^{34} \approx 0.1307468945$ for OptMini (k5-w12-13.txt) and $2248978620 / 2^{34} \approx 0.1309077849$ for GreedyMini (w12_k5.gm). We bypass binary lifting and optimize directly over native $4$-ary permutations ($|Σ^k| = 1024$) from scratch via: (i) an exact de Bruijn endpoint transfer-matrix dynamic program over $B(4, 4)$ ($256$ states), (ii) a weighted set-cover ratio initializer (SetCover4) producing a compact $U = 314$ UHS prefix, (iii) bitmask-sparse simulated annealing preserving the zero-cost $w$-walk-free tail invariant, and (iv) two-pass $O(N)$ exact best-insertion sweeps with large-neighborhood ruin-and-recreate search. The resulting explicit $4$-ary order achieves exact expected DNA density $2234289615 / 2^{34} \approx 0.130052772292$ (density factor $1.690686$), cutting charged windows by $11924929$ contexts ($-0.530890\%$ relative) below OrensteinLab/Minimizers and $14689005$ ($-0.653141\%$) below w12_k5.gm at $8200$-byte lookup storage ($1280$ bytes bit-packed). Warm-start relaxation stalls at $U = 379$ ($0.1301728789$), whereas native $4$-ary initialization breaks $32/32$ high-bit blocks across all $2+2$ nucleotide partitions.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Memory-augmented large language model (LLM) driving agents can reuse past experience, yet previously learned corrections often fail to transfer when traffic conditions change. A key limitation is that existing memories mainly record observed outcomes, providing little evidence about alternative actions. We introduce XMind, a training-free framework that enables LLM-based driving to "Think Twice" through cross-memory reasoning. XMind integrates working memory of recent traffic states with episodic memory of critical risk events. The agent first makes an initial decision from the current context and then cross-checks it against retrieved historical experience. If a conflict arises, it makes a second decision guided by the retrieved evidence. To construct reliable episodic memory, XMind evaluates alternative actions through counterfactual rollouts under perturbed traffic conditions and stores the verified outcomes as structured risk capsules. These capsules are retrieved by jointly matching spatial risk patterns and scenario semantics. Across closed-loop highway simulations, XMind achieves success rates of 83.3-93.3%, exceeding the strong baseline by 6.6 percentage points on average. Evaluation on K-Risk further demonstrates lower decision risk on unseen real-world high-risk interactions. Ablation studies confirm the complementary benefits of working memory, risk-text retrieval, and counterfactual verification. Code is available at https://github.com/xiangxiangliuwo/XMind

</details>

Abstract (英文): Vision-Language Navigation (VLN) requires an embodied agent to relate past experience, current spatial state, and future motion while following natural-language instructions. Recent approaches improve these capabilities through long-horizon memory, state-aware reasoning, and future prediction, yet these components are often modeled separately, leaving navigation state evolution insufficiently connected across time. We argue that camera pose provides a natural geometric representation for bridging past, present, and future states. Based on this insight, we propose PoseVLN, a pose-aware VLN framework that jointly learns navigation decisions and geometric state evolution. PoseVLN organizes visual memory and the current observation in an episode-relative coordinate frame, and jointly predicts the next K navigation actions together with a local $Δ$Pose over the same horizon. The predicted $Δ$Pose is composed with the current trajectory-level pose to obtain the future pose state, which is recursively propagated throughout navigation, forming a continuous geometric state evolution from past memory to future motion. Experiments on the R2R-CE and RxR-CE Val-Unseen splits demonstrate the effectiveness of explicitly modeling geometric state evolution, with PoseVLN outperforming the strongest compared baselines by 2.6 and 2.9 percentage points in success rate, respectively, using only single RGB observations.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Clinical reasoning from incomplete or conflicting patient evidence must account for low-probability but high-risk concerns while remaining grounded in health guidelines. We introduce VerClin, an explainable framework that extracts guideline conditions, exclusions, recommendations, and priorities into executable knowledge and represents patient conversations as scoped cases. A deterministic four-valued reasoner produces traceable guideline matches while preserving uncertainty and enforcing exclusions. Multiple specialty agents then screen matched guidelines for case applicability, and a consultation model synthesizes accepted guidance through specialty recommendations and a sentence-preserving formal-constraint channel. We present accepted guidance in ascending order of the sum of its priority and trigger-strength ranks, which can surface high-risk guidance with weaker triggers. VerClin outperforms direct generation and Top-5 RAG on HealthBench Hard and HealthBench Professional. On the LLMEval-Med subset, it outperforms direct generation and ties Top-5 RAG. Component analyses further examine semantic closure, example-guided refinement, specialty screening, and constraint-conditioned delivery.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Visual On-Policy Distillation (OPD) commonly uses cropped or zoomed-in image regions as teacher-side privileged information to improve fine-grained perception in small Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). However, local pixel-level cues are insufficient for complex visual reasoning that requires global structure, spatial relationships, and high-level semantics. We propose Visual Cue On-Policy Self-Distillation (VC-OPD), which extends teacher-side privileged information beyond local image regions to question-conditioned visual cues. Advanced MLLMs generate cues capturing key visual details, structural relationships, and semantic evidence needed to answer each question. A multi-stage quality filtering process selects reliable cues, which are provided only to the teacher during training. This enables informative token-level supervision along student-generated trajectories, helping the student learn to identify and reason over critical evidence directly from the original image. No additional information is required at inference time. Since advanced MLLMs are used only for visual cue generation, VC-OPD is compatible with both open-weight and proprietary black-box models. Experiments on STEM and general visual reasoning benchmarks show consistent improvements across model scales over reinforcement learning and on-policy distillation baselines. Notably, a 4B model trained with VC-OPD outperforms many baselines with 8B or more parameters, demonstrating effective capability transfer through question-conditioned visual cues.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models advance robotic manipulation, but their reliance on demonstrations exposes them to backdoor threats. Among these threats, trajectory-level targeted backdoors are particularly challenging and may pose greater safety risks than behavior-disrupting attacks: rather than merely causing task failures or local action deviations, they must sustain closed-loop redirection to complete an attacker-specified multi-step task. To reliably induce such behaviors, existing methods typically rely on high poisoning rates to obtain sufficient target-trajectory supervision. However, high poisoning rates may compromise clean-task performance, whereas low rates leave target-trajectory supervision scarce and backdoor learning vulnerable to interference from dominant clean-task updates. To resolve this trade-off at low poisoning rates, we propose a two-stage co-optimization framework guided by vision, language, and action. Stage I freezes the policy and uses differentiable rendering and multimodal supervision to optimize a 3D trigger's texture, translation, and scale, strengthening trigger-target associations from limited poisoned demonstrations. Building on the optimized trigger, Stage II applies source-selective dual-LoRA adaptation to decouple clean and backdoor updates and mitigate their interference, followed by learned column-wise calibration and analytic fusion into a unified policy. Experiments on OpenVLA, OpenVLA-OFT, $π_0$, and $π_{0.5}$ across four LIBERO suites show consistent gains over all evaluated attack baselines. With only 1% poisoned data, our method achieves 86.4% target success and 95.2% clean-task success on OpenVLA-OFT in LIBERO-Spatial. Real-robot trials also demonstrate trajectory-level hijacking.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Alpha-beta pruning is a fundamental technique in game-playing AI, enabling efficient minimax search by avoiding the exploration of branches that cannot affect the optimal decision. We present a monadic formulation of alpha-beta pruning based on the combination of the continuation monad and the reader monad. Unlike previous approaches that encode alpha-beta bounds into the types of available moves, our construction records these bounds within monadic outcomes, leaving the move types unchanged. We further show that the ordinary continuation monad suffices to compute not only optimal outcomes but also optimal plays, by extending the outcome type to include the corresponding sequences of moves. This provides an alternative to generalisations of the continuation monad proposed to address the inefficiencies associated with the selection monad in alpha-beta search. We illustrate our approach with an implementation of Tic-Tac-Toe, observing a 39-fold reduction in the number of plays evaluated, compared with exhaustive search, in computing an optimal play. Finally, we formally establish the correctness of our construction by proving that the monadic alpha-beta search computes the same optimal outcomes and plays as exhaustive minimax search. Our results demonstrate how a foundational algorithm in AI can be expressed and verified using standard monadic abstractions, providing a basis for further development of compositional approaches to adversarial search.

</details>

We introduce FigmaEval, a benchmark for evaluating AI agents on professional UI design tasks, comprising 84 tasks and 783 screens derived from real-world Figma design files. FigmaEval adopts a constrained-specification, open-solution paradigm, standardizing task requirements and design resources while allowing agents to independently determine layouts, visual hierarchies, component composition, and visual presentation. To assess design quality beyond reference similarity, we develop 22 expert-grounded evaluation rubrics across six dimensions and introduce a human evaluation arena where professional designers compare agent-generated designs through blinded pairwise judgments. Evaluating eleven models under a unified Figma agent setup, we find substantial agreement between rubric-based assessments and human preferences, alongside distinct differences in design practices and quality--efficiency trade-offs. FigmaEval provides a framework for systematically assessing professional UI design capabilities and understanding the strengths and limitations of current AI agents.

Abstract (英文): An agent can spend almost as much deciding what to do as doing it. In our documentation workflow, control-flow routing accounts for 42.5% of baseline reference cost on the initial task. Lower token prices alone do not solve this control-flow tax: inexpensive general-purpose routers can trigger additional calls to the expensive generator. We evaluate a heterogeneous architecture in which Jev, a specialized System 1 decision model, controls finite actions while Sol generates documents. Across four software tasks, 480 valid runs comprise 470 completions and ten request-limit endpoints. Jev full routing achieves the lowest mean cost and elapsed time on every task, completing all 40 specialist-controlled runs along the shortest trajectory. On the initial task, mean cost falls by 49.6% (95% paired-bootstrap interval: 42.9% to 56.3%) and elapsed time by 33.2% (19.6% to 46.7%). Across three further tasks, cost reductions are 40.5% to 42.9% and concurrent elapsed-time reductions are 12.9% to 14.4%. Existence-only specialization saves just 1.8% on the initial task. Separately, Sol and Jev each select accepted actions on 100/100 reviewed retail states, with Jev using 97.88% less reference cost and 72.30% less mean request time. The results identify two requirements for efficient agent control: inexpensive decisions and trajectories that preserve those savings.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Neural architecture search and LLM-driven evolution often reduce evaluation to a scalar fitness score, discarding failures that could explain why designs succeed or fail. We present a hypothesis-driven architecture discovery loop in which LLM agents diagnose structured error patterns, formulate falsifiable hypotheses, test counterfactual controls under a frozen nnU-Net pipeline, and distill validated mechanisms into executable trigger-to-action rules. Across eight search waves, the loop identifies two principles for 3D medical image segmentation. First, reallocating computation from high-resolution decoders to the mid-encoder preserves thin-anatomy boundaries while reducing cost. Second, support-confined region contrast addresses errors between nested anatomical compartments, with anatomy-dependent triggers selecting when the rule applies. These principles produce LeanNeXt (0.78 MACs and 0.92 training-step time relative to nnU-Net; +1.63 Dice points on four-fold KiTS23 cross-validation) and trigger-routed LeanNeXt+. The latter improves all 15 evaluated CT/MRI settings over nnU-Net and exceeds the 3.3 larger ResEnc-M on 12 of 15 benchmarks at 0.30 its parameter count. Relative to random sampling, one-shot LLM generation, and score-selected agent search, the loop shows how failure analysis can yield reusable design rules across modalities.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Agent skills provide reusable workflows that guide LLM agents in using tools. Refining these workflows requires feedback on missing or incorrect instructions, but task-specific verifiers are not always available. We propose VISE (Verifier-Independent Skill Evolution), a scripts-first approach that uses tool implementations to guide skill revision. It extracts tool capabilities, input requirements, usage conditions, and output formats from source code. Using the task request and execution trace, it compares this information with current workflow instructions to identify missing or conflicting steps. It then recommends specific edits to SKILL.md, linking each change to the relevant implementation and usage conditions. We evaluate VISE as an additional revision stage for four evolution methods on SkillsBench and SkillLearnBench. Compared with the corresponding No-verifier conditions, it improves overall average task success by 9.14-13.35 percentage points. The proportion of tasks solved in all three runs increases by 12.36-18.29 points. A case study shows how revised instructions connect tool outputs to later steps. These results support tool implementations as a practical feedback source for skill self-evolution.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language model (LLM)-based agents are advancing the automation of data-driven scientific research. Recent advances in recursive self-improvement (RSI) offer the potential for these agents to continuously improve through experience, but evaluating transfer across scientific tasks requires executable, scientifically verifiable tasks and independent assessment of self-improvement gains. We introduce RecurSciBench, a scalable benchmark for evaluating RSI in data-driven scientific research. Its RecurSciPipe framework automatically constructs scientific modeling and data analysis tasks from raw datasets, with execution-based verification and scientific validity checks. RecurSciBench spans four scientific domains, multiple data modalities, and varied scientific objectives. We evaluate four RSI systems by comparing their initial and evolved states on independent tasks after evolution on source tasks, covering within-type, cross-type, and cross-domain transfer. Experiments show that the benefits of self-improvement vary across agent systems and scientific domains. Positive overall gains can coexist with regressions on individual tasks. Experience can transfer across task types and benefit other domains even without measurable gains in the source domain.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Official statistics are critical datasets for a wide variety of actors, including policymakers and businesses. For providers of official statistics, improving accessibility, governance, and disclosure of their data is of key importance. This can be improved by providing question-answering systems based on text-to-SQL approaches. However, existing text-to-SQL benchmarks do not resemble this setting. Official statistics are published as open-domain catalogs of thousands of wide tables whose columns and values are codes, and whose meaning is recorded in separate metadata rather than in a relational schema. To address this gap, we introduce LOCuST, a text-to-SQL benchmark that pairs over two thousand English and Dutch tables from Statistics Netherlands with a semantic knowledge graph. The graph describes the tables, measures, and dimension codes through labels, definitions, hierarchies, and units, and is the external knowledge a system needs to find the right tables and ground a question in the right codes. The benchmark includes expert-annotated questions for different query and aggregation types, including raw value retrieval, summations, proportion calculations, and joins. Alongside standard evaluation measures, we introduce an observation F1 metric that measures how close a query is to the ground truth even when the answers are different. We evaluate a variety of state-of-the-art LLMs and find that they struggle to write SQL that answers the questions correctly.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Surgical resection planning centers on generating executable resection surfaces from anatomical evidence and operative constraints. Medical multimodal foundation models enable anatomical understanding, and segmentation models predict resection regions, but neither translates these capabilities into explicitly constrained, executable resection surfaces. We present GeoSurge, an anatomy-grounded framework that combines mask-derived metrics with structure-aware point-cloud tokens to generate executable, editable resection surfaces. To make resection geometry learnable and editable, GeoSurge maps actions to parametric geometry through a surface-action grammar. Using this grammar, anatomy-oriented and tumor-centered templates generate candidates, which are filtered by hard constraints and semantic deduplication. Training supervision is then derived from candidate-expert geometric agreement and independently recomputed feasibility metrics. At inference, the reasoner uses patient evidence and surgical requirements to select or revise an action, which an independent geometry engine subsequently materializes and verifies. GeoSurge achieves the best overall scores on DeepTumour Liver and CRLM-QA, improving the constraint satisfaction rate from 73% to 100% on CRLM and from 56% to 99% on GeoSurge-Bench.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Can artificial intelligence reproduce the economic dynamics that emerge as households, firms and governments interact and adapt through markets and institutions? To answer this question, we define an economic world model (EWM) as an agent's internal representation of how an economic system works, enabling counterfactual simulation and decision-making under resource constraints. We then propose a general framework for studying EWMs that links three tasks: simulating agent behaviour, learning economic transitions and systematically evaluating the dynamics. As an initial implementation, we learn household policies from microeconomic data and calibrate a structural economic transition model using macroeconomic data, linking individual decisions to aggregate dynamics. Our micro-to-macro evaluation framework compares simulated individual actions and group distributions with observed microdata and assesses macroeconomic dynamics through forecasting and counterfactual experiments. Results show that simulated and observed household consumption distributions overlap by 94.9%, while EWM forecasts closely track real GDP and several other aggregate levels. In government-borrowing counterfactuals, the EWM reproduces the benchmark life-cycle model's short-run transmission from borrowing to consumption. For ex ante planning, the EWM enables an LLM agent to achieve 10.29% higher simulated GDP under the epidemic shock than direct LLM decisions. This initial EWM is only a starting point. We invite economists and AI researchers to build a stronger EWM together. Economists can contribute richer economic models and knowledge, while AI researchers can develop stronger data-driven methods for simulating agent behaviour and learning economic transitions. Together, we aim not only to reproduce real economic dynamics, but also to discover and test new mechanisms that support better decisions and benefit society.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language model (LLM)-based agents access remote services through tools to complete user tasks, often via API calls. In this setting, a remote service may want to identify the backend LLM of an agent to launch model-specific attacks, including prompt injection or selective delivery of manipulated content. However, the service cannot directly query the backend model and must instead collect observable responses through its interactions with the agent, making identification challenging.
  In this paper, we present Completely Automated Preference Test to tell LLMs Apart (CAPTA), a model identification approach inspired by CAPTCHA (Completely Automated Public Turing test to tell Computers and Humans Apart), which distinguishes humans from automated programs. CAPTA presents multiple-choice questions as verification challenges, using differences in models' selection preferences to identify the backend LLM. It selects questions based on cross-model option-generation diversity and uses LLM-guided search to optimize their option sets. Our evaluation shows that CAPTA achieves 99.0% mean identification accuracy with only 10 questions across 25 LLMs from 10 model families. We further evaluate CAPTA with four widely used agents and seven representative LLMs, achieving 99.7% mean identification accuracy across all 28 agent-LLM combinations with 30 submitted answers.

</details>

Abstract (英文): In Minecraft, a common testbed for embodied agents, semantic tools such as mineBlock pick targets from the simulator's loaded world state without a visibility test, so a buried ore the agent has never seen is one call away, and scores cannot show whether the agent searched. We formalize the missing requirement as evidence closure: every decision of an agent system, including those inside its tools, may depend on the world only through facts for which the agent holds evidence. EviCraft is a runtime and benchmark that enforces closure below the agent and makes the admission rule an experimental variable: a perception-grounded regime (G) admits only what is in line of sight or returned by the agent's own actions, and a loaded-state regime (L) reproduces standard tool stacks, with agents, tools, and worlds held fixed. On Survival, the regime effect exceeds the strongest agent's lead: under G, three mid-sized agents reach the diamond in 32%-40% of runs, against 76%-94% under L, and the strongest agent under G scores below each of them under L. In two models, removing only the line-of-sight test closes 93%-96% of the score gap, and loading state only inside tools recovers 70%-98%, so safeguards on observations and queries leave most of the leak open. In open-ended acquisition, the gain from L concentrates in items that depend on buried resources or mobs. Code and benchmark are available at https://github.com/AllenReder/EviCraft

</details>

Abstract (英文): Database agents need more than schema structure: business meanings, stored values and reference queries supply knowledge that becomes relevant at different points in reasoning. We introduce RubikSQL-HB, which exposes these materials through queryable, database-specific knowledge workspaces built on the HeavenBase platform. Its knowledge interface remains available as an agent constructs and investigates an answer. Candidate agents interleave knowledge access with SQL construction and execution; a downstream Judge can reuse the same interfaces to investigate disagreements. A two-stage example interface separates discovering a reference question from expanding its SQL. On a 500-question BIRD Mini-Dev evaluation, the full pipeline reaches 75.2% execution accuracy, against 71.2% for first-candidate selection and 70.4% for execution-majority voting, with a correct candidate available on 79.0% of questions. The raw-knowledge condition, which also regenerates candidates without the built workspace, is 3.0 points lower, and adjudication with tools and revision accounts for most of the gain over selection baselines. A recorded execution probe shows how continued investigation resolves a candidate disagreement. Separate archived runs show semantic revision recovering answers absent from their original candidate pools. A versioned development study further illustrates how recurring corrections become reusable execution checks. The system and results connect knowledge-interface design to agent decision quality: queryable knowledge supports the construction of useful alternatives, and evidence-aware adjudication connects those alternatives to a final answer. Keeping knowledge in the loop lets agents acquire relevant observations as their reasoning develops.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Latent world models enable goal-directed planning from offline visual data, but prediction alone does not specify which action-related distinctions their representations should preserve. Inverse dynamics provides auxiliary supervision, yet squared-error action regression rewards additional information only through changes in the conditional action mean. We propose Contrastive Identification of Actions (CIA), which identifies the executed action among alternatives sampled from a history-conditioned behavior proposal. Comparing actions plausible in the same context targets information supplied by the next latent beyond history. Under exact behavior matching, we show that CIA can reward action information that leaves optimal inverse-regression error unchanged, and verify this distinction through a controlled learning example. CIA achieves higher planner-averaged mean success than the base world model and a regression inverse-dynamics baseline across five visual control benchmarks, without modifying the planner.

</details>

Abstract (英文): The internal activations of modern machine learning models align surprisingly well with the activities recorded in real brains. In the last five years, the alignment between encoding models and brain activities, often called brain scores, has steadily increased. Because brain recordings are noisy, there exists an upper bound to the brain scores that any model can achieve. This bound, called the noise ceiling, is crucial for two reasons: it determines whether further improvements in model performance are possible, and it enables meaningful comparisons of brain scores across benchmarks. The field has developed multiple approaches to estimate noise ceilings, but the statistical properties of existing estimators remain poorly understood, leaving it unclear in which experimental settings they provide reliable estimates. Here, we introduce a unified mathematical framework for brain recordings, use it to derive closed-form expressions for four commonly used noise ceiling estimators, and provide theoretical predictions for the behavior of each estimator. We validate the theoretical predictions with simulations that we compare to noise ceiling estimates in existing fMRI and EEG datasets. We find that the existing methods underestimate the true noise ceiling in different settings. We conclude with recommendations for best practice in different situations.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Computers and their interfaces are usually treated as fixed, so a person, or increasingly an AI agent, must adapt to whatever the operating system presents. This is limiting when someone needs a different workflow, or an interface suited to hands-free use or a disability. We explore an overlooked alternative. Rather than training an agent to operate an existing computer, we train a generative model to be the computer, predicting each next screen from past screens and the user's mouse and keyboard actions. Treating a desktop as an interactive world model raises problems that natural-video world models largely avoid. Without a camera pose to index memory, the model must remember content that has left the screen without any geometric cues. Arithmetic in a terminal has to be exactly right, not merely plausible. Small interface text must also stay legible at interactive frame rates. Our Interactive Neural Computer is structured like a Neural Turing Machine and starts to address these problems with three modules trained end to end from pixels and actions. A surprise-gated slot memory, supervised by latent next-frame prediction (MEMORY-JEPA), stores what is off screen. An exploratory query bridge brings in the reasoning of a pretrained VLM, and a residual renderer predicts only the latent changes between frames. In long closed-loop rollouts, our model recalls occluded files in 64.2% of trials (chance is 33.3%), compared with at most 56.0% for recurrent and sliding-window baselines. It also raises terminal-text OCR accuracy from 1.3% to 50.1%. Finally, because the memory is explicit, it can be transplanted at test time to causally change what the model renders.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Evaluations of quantized language models attribute the change in a benchmark score to quantization. We write the measured change as the true change plus a term contributed by the instrument. Instrument error that is the same at every precision can shrink a paired comparison but cannot create or reverse an effect. Error that interacts with quantization can do both, and it is reported as an effect of quantization. We estimate this interaction for four measurement choices in 8 instruction-tuned models below two billion parameters, at float16, LLM.int8() and NF4, on identical items. Measured against labels from two human annotators, the 4-bit rise in harmful compliance is about half to two-thirds of the rise in non-refusal that a corrected key word detector records. Cloze and lettered multiple-choice scoring disagree about the effect of quantization far more than their noise allows: their estimates correlate at \r{ho} = 0.10, against a ceiling of about 0.77. LLM.int8() answers and generations depend on which items share a batch, and padding alone changes its answers. Rounding activations to 8 bits is enough to produce this dependence. NF4 is batch-invariant. The two largest reported calibration losses are response-bias shifts. We release item-level predictions, option scores and human annotations, and give recommendations for evaluating compressed small models.

</details>

Abstract (英文): AI agents working on long tasks often commit to an answer before they have enough evidence, a behavior called laziness. In our evaluations, they frequently commit even while their stated belief still names several possible answers. An outcome reward at the end of the episode scores only the final answer, so it cannot teach the agent when to commit, because a lucky guess receives the same reward as an answer backed by evidence. During training, however, the simulator can compute the exact posterior over the answer at every step and tell whether the evidence is already enough. We propose Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Beliefs (RLVB), which rewards the agent for taking further steps while this posterior still allows several answers and for committing once the evidence is sufficient. The reward needs no judge, critic or reward model, and it never reads the belief line the agent writes before each action. We also introduce Pro Worlds, a benchmark of visual puzzles where the agent views a large world through a small window. We evaluate three vision-language models on three benchmarks, two of which are never seen in training. RLVB improves the base model in all nine settings, by 17.1% on average. Under the outcome reward, GRPO and PPO fall below the base model on average. RLVB also outperforms PPO with a process reward from an LLM judge by 9.3% on average while using 1.6 times fewer GPU-hours. The trained agent answers with sufficient evidence in 51%, 12% and 74% of the episodes on the three benchmarks, against 8%, 0.4% and 36% for the base model. Its accuracy gain therefore comes from answering more often at high levels of evidence. The belief line shows how the agent reaches this evidence: it acts on its own belief. It commits at only 0.1 to 2.2% of the turns at which the evidence is insufficient and its line still names several answers, against 3 to 68% for the base model.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Fine-tuning an instruction-tuned language model on new facts often erodes its general abilities. We find that standard fine-tuning lowers instruction-following, reasoning and coding benchmarks by 35 points on average, and that these losses track a collapse in answer length: fine-tuned models at least halve their responses, and the further a response drifts from the initial model's length, the more often it fails. We propose InstructReplay: the initial model answers a set of general instructions once, and during fine-tuning the model is kept close to the initial model on these answers. Across three model families and two domains, InstructReplay reduces the average decline on five general benchmarks from 28.1 to 2.4 points and keeps answer length close to the initial model's, while learning the new facts as well as standard fine-tuning. It balances learning and retention better than seven adaptation methods in all six settings. Controlled comparisons attribute the gain to replaying the model's own answers to general instructions rather than to the form of the loss.

</details>

Abstract (英文): A developer checking an assistant's report may need only an alarm that a claim is unsupported. A generative verifier, however, can continue decoding to quote and classify the errors. We study how much of that output is needed to recover its decision. In a frozen 2B span verifier, the choice between an empty error list and its first object precedes all localization. Native8 stops generation at this branch. The preceding seven syntax tokens are fixed, so Prefill1 supplies them as input and generates only the unrestricted branch token, using the same native instruction. On 2,015 code-agent TEST responses, Prefill1 reduces eager resident service by 84.84% relative to full generation, with released-reference F1 of 83.43 versus 83.41. It saves 31.98% over Native8 even though their logical token totals are identical. Under default compiled execution, the extra saving is 6.74-26.73% on the complete workload, with small decision changes. Controlled evidence interventions and first-location decoding establish what these shorter readouts retain and what requires further generation.

Abstract (英文): The use of AI agents among the general public has been increasing exponentially throughout 2026, though it is often hard for users to determine which configuration is most effective for their personal use. To help provide clarity here we introduce ShellBench, 100 day-to-day tasks based on more than 10 million user requests, each set in a realistic workspace with messy files and competing data sources, and graded for both correctness and safety. We evaluate 12 models across 5 agent harnesses at 3 reasoning levels for a total of 53,100 rollouts, 20% of which are on tasks designated for public release. We find that model choice impacts performance more than harness choice, but the three leading models differ by only 1.1 percentage points. GPT-6.1 Sol passed 61.4% of attempts at about one-sixth of GPT-6 Astra's estimated token cost per attempt, while GPT-6 Astra led at 62.5%. We also found that harness choice had little effect on either performance or safety with all harnesses scoring between 42.9 (Claude Code) and 45.2 (OpenClaw) for pass@1.

Abstract (英文): Systematic conservation planning must allocate limited protection budgets sequentially under ecological and climatic uncertainty. Static tools cannot adapt as conditions change, and policies optimised only for expected outcomes can fail on the worst-case trajectories climate change makes increasingly likely. CAPTAIN was the first framework to cast this as sequential reinforcement learning, but its gradient-free Evolution Strategies (ES) optimiser lacks per-timestep credit assignment. We replace ES with Proximal Policy Optimisation (PPO) via a Plackett-Luce solution to CAPTAIN's non-differentiable action space, and replace its scalar critic with an Implicit Quantile Network (IQN) modelling the full return distribution. A matched-granularity comparison shows decision granularity, not the optimiser, drives most of the ecological gain, so the real contribution is training efficiency and extensibility to risk-sensitive objectives. On a New Zealand marine dataset, it matches or exceeds ES's outcomes at a fraction of the training cost, offering a template for future work ES's black-box design could not support

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language model (LLM) serving engines offload the key/value (KV) cache from GPU memory to host DRAM and SSD so multi-turn and agent requests can reuse prompt prefixes. Capacity then limits service at two levels: external tiers govern prefix reuse before eviction, while the GPU KV pool bounds concurrent long requests, and lossy compression alters KV values and outputs. LoomKV brings lossless KV compression to both external tiers (tier mode) and the GPU pool (pool mode) on an unmodified engine. Using a calibrated per-head two-level 3-bit code over BF16 high bytes (sign and upper seven exponent bits), LoomKV stores BF16 KV at 0.71 of its raw size across nine models and FP8 KV at 0.85-0.87. In tier mode, TileStream writes exact-length records with length readback off the load path, leaving tier storage, eviction, and prefetch unchanged, and decompresses fetched records straight into scattered GPU pages. In pool mode, FragPack packs variable-length records into smaller shared-budget GPU pages read in place by a fused decode-attention kernel faster than FlashAttention-3 on BF16. Built with LMCache and vLLM, LoomKV makes compressed tiers act as 1.4x larger raw tiers on AgentX: across LMCache's LRU cliff, tier mode delivers 2.7-3.6x raw's goodput and 1.9-3.1x lower mean time-to-first-token (TTFT), shifts the cliff identically under other simulated replacement policies, and cuts repeated-prompt TTFT up to 1.7x via SSD-to-DRAM promotion. When tiers hold the working set, pool mode fits 1.31-1.34x as many GPU tokens, admitting 12 long requests with roughly raw's queue and goodput at 8. On an AI IDE trace where both tiers and the GPU pool are constrained, tier and pool modes raise goodput by 1.24x and 1.59x. Where raw capacity suffices, compression costs at most 2.2% throughput; tier mode is bit-exact with raw tiers, and pool mode matches FlashAttention-3's numerical accuracy.

</details>

Abstract (英文): As clinical AI agents begin to interact with electronic health records (EHRs), large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to evaluate their actions. But how reliably can these judges recognize clinically dangerous mistakes, especially when an agent appears to have completed its task successfully? We investigate this question using MedAgentBench by introducing controlled clinical errors into verified agent trajectories. The experiments include 416 faulty trajectories, eight error categories, and six open-weight LLM judges. By comparing each faulty trajectory with its correct counterpart, we measure whether judges genuinely recognize errors rather than simply flagging more actions as unsafe. The findings show that access to clinical evidence significantly improves judge reliability. When shown only the final answer, judges cannot distinguish errors that leave it unchanged, while flagging 56% of correct trajectories. With full trajectory information, the ability to distinguish unsafe actions from correct ones reaches a score of 0.75, and providing actual EHR responses improves discrimination by 0.39. Surprisingly, including the agent's reasoning does not improve overall performance and reduces detection of incorrectly reported clinical values by 0.18, which remain the hardest errors to detect. MedGemma performed worse than the general-purpose model and was the only judge to miss potentially dangerous medication overdoses. We also find that the benchmark's original grader accepts all 38 trajectories with an intentionally skipped laboratory check, while combining LLM judges with a rule-based checker informed by the full EHR record detects 97% of injected faults.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Auditing failed agent executions requires identifying the responsible actor, the earliest decisive step, and the error mode. Representative supervised methods either focus on step localization or predict multiple attribution targets from shared trajectory representations. These methods do not use the localization result to select the text for mode diagnosis. To address this limitation, we propose RecordRoute, which couples comparative localization with local diagnosis. Specifically, it represents each record with word and character features and learns to rank records through comparisons within each trajectory. The selected record supplies the actor and step, while its text and metadata drive mode prediction. On controlled-injection Who&When Pro, RecordRoute outperforms StepFinder and ASCon on all four official metrics. With actor and step predictions fixed, local diagnosis reaches 77.47% pooled joint accuracy versus 69.28% for full-trace diagnosis. On natural failures from TraceElephant and CatchBench POST, it outperforms these baselines and three open LLMs in localization.

</details>

Abstract (英文): AI-native radio access network (RAN) control involves interdependent decisions across network entities, protocol layers, and timescales. Understanding how AI and conventional methods can jointly support these decisions requires a common basis for comparing their functional roles. This survey introduces a technique-agnostic taxonomy of RAN decision-making organized around four complementary capabilities: Representation and Reasoning, Prediction and Simulation, Adaptive Control, and Decision Synthesis. These capabilities are defined by their roles in the control process, independently of the techniques used to implement them. Through this perspective, we examine representative AI and model-based approaches, including foundation models, world models, reinforcement learning, and optimization. We then apply the taxonomy to representative RAN control solutions across slow, medium, fast, and cross-timescale applications to identify which capabilities they implement and how these capabilities are combined in hybrid AI systems. We also review datasets, benchmarks, simulators, digital twins, and experimental platforms according to their support for the decision-making process and reproducible evaluation. The review highlights a gap between the availability of resources for closed-loop experimentation and the standardization of tasks, metrics, and evaluation protocols needed for meaningful comparisons.Finally, we identify research directions for hybrid AI in AI-native RAN, including capability integration, multi-agent and cross-timescale coordination, constraint handling, and reproducible closed-loop evaluation. A curated list of surveyed works and evaluation resources is available at https://github.com/pegahani/Hybrid-AI-RAN-Survey.

</details>

Abstract (英文): While Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as automated evaluators in evaluation and training pipelines, their judgements are often affected by systematic biases that conflict with human preferences. Although prior work has identified various biases and proposed methods for detecting and mitigating them, less attention has been paid to using human preferences to inform bias identification and mitigation. Moreover, these approaches rely heavily on pre-discovered bias lists, overlook bias strength, and depend on costly interventions. In this work, we propose HUB-J, an integrated framework based on human-derived bias factors that detects, quantifies, and mitigates biases in LLM-as-a-Judge systems. Our approach leverages human-LLM judgement disagreement cases to automatically discover interpretable bias factors, and utilizes agreement cases to quantify bias strength through controlled input modifications and resulting shifts in model decisions. Finally, building on these quantified biases, we introduce a lightweight, training-free regression-based mitigation strategy that estimates the influence of each bias factor on model judgments and corrects bias-influenced decisions by removing its estimated effect. Empirical results show that HUB-J uncovers both known and novel bias factors, reveals meaningful differences in model susceptibility, and consistently reduces bias-driven decision flips while generalizing across models.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Entity Resolution (ER) pipelines often collapse ambiguity into early hard decisions: upstream Named Entity Recognition (NER) selects a single span and type, and downstream Entity Linking (EL) treats that choice as ground truth. This discards valuable information about the ambiguity of the underlying data (e.g., span boundary disagreement and type confusion), leading to brittle systems that fail unpredictably when faced with uncertainty in inputs. Therefore, this work presents a proof-of-concept for uncertainty-aware ER that treats uncertainty as native data that should be preserved and exploited throughout ER pipelines. We (i) capture uncertainty signals in the NER stage and (ii) leverage these signals into the EL stage to improve candidate retrieval under ambiguity. Using a BERT-based NER model augmented with two auxiliary span-boundary prediction heads, we apply Monte Carlo (MC) dropout at inference time to derive three mention-level proxy signals: the MC-averaged start-boundary probability, the MC-averaged end-boundary probability, and De Luca-Termini fuzzy entropy computed over the Begin-Inside-Outside (BIO) predictive distribution. These signals are used to adapt the fuzzy cutoff in candidate generation (CG) for EL. Because CG is treated as a blocking step, we evaluate retrieval recall. On the AnnoCTR dataset linked to the MITRE ATTCK knowledge base, the boundary-aware and dual-signal fuzzy linking approaches improve Recall@10 from 0.6186 to 0.7966. The contribution lies not in an end-to-end optimised ER pipeline but in establishing uncertainty preservation as a viable and beneficial component of ER pipelines, enabling downstream EL to make better-informed decisions under uncertainty.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Agents built on frontier language models are expensive. At every step, the frontier model re-reads its entire context and executes every subtask regardless of its difficulty. This paper explores the simple thesis that much of this cost can be removed by delegating easy subtasks to a small language model, or SLM, that runs on hardware one already owns. To test it, we build Delegent, a simple and effective agent harness built on Claude Code, in which the frontier model stays in charge of every task but hands focused subtasks, such as investigating code, running tests, and searching a corpus, to SLM subagents. Existing approaches such as cascades and model routers usually choose which model executes the entire task, whereas in Delegent, the frontier model always leads the execution and delegates easy subtasks to the SLM. No router or model is trained, so either model can be swapped freely. With the help of a 27B FP8 SLM, Delegent reduces the frontier cost of long-horizon coding by 67% and of agentic search by 59%, with no loss in task performance, and even a 2B SLM that fits on a laptop reduces the coding cost by 66%. The savings grow with the legwork that the frontier model would otherwise do itself, and a short instruction, which leaves the main coding tasks to the frontier model, accounts for most of the saving and keeps the 2B SLM from losing tasks. Code: https://github.com/castorini/delegent

</details>

Abstract (英文): Behavioral cloning models in chess typically predict human moves from board states while ignoring temporal inputs such as the remaining clock. However, time pressure is a major determinant of human decision-making quality, and temporal state is a missing dimension of human behavioral modeling. This paper introduces a time-aware transformer that extends a Chessformer-style architecture with learned temporal conditioning tokens encoding player clock state, time control, and recent tempo, together with behavioral heads for thinking time and resignation. The model is trained on large-scale online chess data and evaluated against time-agnostic baselines and the state of the art. Results show that temporal conditioning yields modest overall accuracy improvements but substantially stronger gains under time pressure. Beyond move choice, it also produces more accurate thinking-time and resignation predictions. More importantly, the model better matches human move-quality degradation in critical time regimes, reducing the systematic over-strength observed in time-agnostic behavioral cloning models. These results suggest that temporal state is a previously underutilized but important signal for human behavioral cloning in chess.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language models (LLMs) can generate parallel programs, but their correctness and performance depend strongly on the instructions that guide generation. We present \textsc{MetaFORGE}, an execution-grounded meta-learning framework that iteratively optimizes persistent natural-language skill specifications for MPI C code generation. \textsc{MetaFORGE} combines correctness testing, compiler diagnostics, static-analysis findings, runtime measurements, and rank-level load-imbalance profiles into a scalar reward that drives textual-gradient refinement, while an external memory distills and retrieves reward-supported optimization knowledge across runs.
  Across six MPI tasks and three workload sizes, \textsc{MetaFORGE} improves static-analysis quality, runtime, and load balance relative to zero-shot generation and generally exceeds the evaluated \textsc{TextGrad} iterative baseline. A model-matched refinement ablation shows that the MetaFORGE advantage persists when its refinement model is reduced to \texttt{gpt-4o}. An expanded retrieval ablation across all 18 task--workload configurations shows consistent runtime benefit and broad static-analysis and load-balance benefit from retrieved context. Leakage-controlled held-out and zero-update experiments further provide evidence that optimization knowledge persists in both the transferred skill and external memory under the evaluated protocol.

</details>
